We empirically test this hypothesis by training variational autoencoders on four face datasets without attractiveness supervision and evaluating their representations on the 597 faces from the Chicago Face Database. We report three main novel findings. First, we identify that human attractiveness ratings closely aligns with the direction defined by the VAE evidence lower bound (ELBO) in rate-distortion space. Second, we find that independently learned latent spaces contain an attractiveness direction that transfers strongly across random initializations and training data. Finally, we observe that attractive faces are more prototypical in both shape and latent space. Our work provides the first explicit connection between classic accounts of aesthetics with learned generative models and empirical support for a variational interpretation of the processing fluency theory of aesthetic pleasure.|[2608.24219](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24219)|
|**2026-11-05**|**SLICEChat: Progressive In-Encoder Token Pruning for Whole-Slide Pathology Language Models**|Ali Kerem Bozkurt et.al.|Whole-slide pathology images (WSIs) contain gigapixel-scale visual content, creating a major scalability challenge for slide-level multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Existing approaches process thousands of patch tokens and typically apply compression only after slide encoding, leaving multimodal attention computationally expensive. We introduce SLICEChat, a slide-level MLLM that integrates progressive token pruning within a hybrid Mamba--Transformer slide encoder. Mamba layers enable efficient long-range propagation, while Transformer layers preserve global interactions as the sequence is progressively shortened. Between stages, language-supervised, region-aware pruning removes spatially coherent low-utility regions under a controlled keep-rate schedule, producing compact slide representations before multimodal fusion. On SlideBench VQA, SLICEChat achieves 79.84% accuracy on TCGA and 59.09% on BCNB cohorts, outperforming prior slide-level pathology MLLMs, and achieves the highest overall WSI-Bench metrics. It also provides competitive memory usage and the inference latency among the evaluated models. These results demonstrate accurate and computationally efficient multimodal reasoning over gigapixel WSIs.|[2609.24894](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24894)|
|**2026-11-05**|**MMAP: Multimodal Missing-Aware Pretraining for Longitudinal Alzheimer's Prediction**|Fiona Kekwick et.al.|Clinical decision making heavily relies on predicting the disease progression trajectory by seeking to understand patient's health status which is characterised by multimodal medical data. AI holds great potential for learning useful representations from multimodal medical data to predict disease progression and aid clinical decision making. However, development of predictive AI models is constrained by missing modalities and incomplete tabular data frequently occurring in medical datasets. In addition, disease labels alone may only provide limited supervisory signals for learning representations from high-dimensional multimodal data. Here, we present MMAP, a novel Multimodal Missing-aware Alignment Pretraining method for learning image-tabular representations from incomplete data. An image encoder is pretrained with efficient sigmoid contrastive learning combined with generative reconstruction. A tabular encoder is built upon a tabular foundation model. A missing token generator enables the two encoders to take incomplete data as input, enabling the model to be robust against missing modalities, either with missing images or missing tabular data. We evaluate the clinical usefulness of the learnt multimodal representations on two challenging longitudinal clinical tasks for Alzheimer's disease: predicting disease stage conversion and predicting amyloid status. The proposed method outperforms strong multimodal and unimodal baselines.|[2609.26617](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26617)|
|**2026-11-05**|**MM-VeriAgent: Learning to Use Extensive Tools to Verify Multimodal Misinformation with Reinforcement Learning**|Peipei Li et.al.|Real-world multimodal misinformation often involves mixed forgery sources, requiring sample-specific detection strategies. Existing tool-augmented methods rely on predefined workflows or inference-time planning, limiting adaptability or increasing inference cost. To address this issue, we introduce \textbf{MM-VeriAgent}, which learns to verify mixed-source multimodal misinformation with tools. We first build \textbf{MM-VeriTools}, a specialized toolkit for misinformation detection agents. By benchmarking various candidate models and methods on the sub-tasks required by mixed-source detection, we select the strongest for textual, visual, and cross-modal forgery analysis and encapsulate them as callable tools with a unified interface. On top of this toolkit, we train the LVLM agent with reinforcement learning to teach it how to use these tools to better solve mixed-source detection. Since many of the tools are specialized models whose online execution at every rollout severely limits RL efficiency, we further introduce \textbf{Tool-Execution Cache}, which pre-executes candidate tool calls and reuses their cached outputs during training. This preserves multi-step rollouts while reducing online tool execution, largely improving the training efficiency.Experiments on MMFakeBench demonstrate substantial accuracy gains over the base model without explicit tool search at inference time. Ablation and efficiency analyses further validate the learned tool-use policy and show that Tool-Execution Cache reduces online tool executions during training.|[2609.30698](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30698)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Marathoner: Ultra-Long-Horizon Autonomous Intelligence**|Ruiyang Zhang et.al.|Humans naturally possess the ability to work persistently toward long-term goals. Given a challenging task, humans can continuously work for months or even years to accomplish a specific objective. In this paper, we propose Marathoner, an autonomous agentic model possessing the ability of ultra-long-horizon execution. Specifically, we propose a comprehensive post-training pipeline to instill this critical capability into base model. For Ultra-Long-Horizon Task Synthesis, we leverage major release PRs containing 1000+ lines of new code from diverse GitHub repositories as the primary source for synthesizing challenging task-level data. Additionally, we introduce Multi-Task Chaining, which chains multiple generated tasks into a single more challenging task, enabling the synthesis of tasks with frontier-level difficulty. For rejection sampling finetuning, we combine strong teacher model with diverse harnesses to generate trajectories on our synthesized tasks and conduct supervised finetuning on base model with rejection sampled trajectories. For reinforcement learning, cold-started model performs real-world execution through harnesses in independent sandboxes during rollout process, effectively facilitating the acquisition of genuine ultra-long-horizon execution capability. We further propose a novel reward strategy, Later Stage Bonus Reward, which explicitly encourages model to perform meaningful maneuvers during later stages of execution. Through extensive evaluation on 5 benchmarks containing ultra-long-horizon tasks, Marathoner achieves consistent and substantial performance improvements over base model and even surpasses performance of strong proprietary model. Further analysis shows that Marathoner can consistently work for 10+ hours and conduct 1000+ tool calls on highly challenging tasks.|[2609.34378](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34378)|
|**2026-11-05**|**SoL-Refiner: Speed-of-Light One-Step Refinement for High-Resolution Video**|Haozhe Liu et.al.|High-resolution video generation is expensive, as its cost grows rapidly with the number of spatiotemporal tokens. A practical alternative first generates a lower-resolution video and then applies a refiner, but conventional multi-step refinement introduces a second sampling bottleneck. We present SoL-Refiner, a one-step video refiner that transforms low-resolution model outputs into 4K videos with a single denoising step. Our three-stage recipe combines high-resolution continual training, reinforcement learning (RL) post-training, and a final one-step distillation. We introduce Refiner-Bench, a video refinement benchmark constructed from the outputs of different video generators, and use a shared-input protocol to compare refiners at approximately 2K output resolution. With LTX-2.3 Refiner as the base model, the one-step SoL-Refiner outperforms all external refiners at 2K on the VBench and UniPercept averages, while at $3840\!\times\!2176$ it improves both metrics over the three-step LTX-2.3 Refiner. With the complete acceleration stack, SoL-Refiner achieves an $8.91\times$ speedup in refinement latency over the same baseline in our 2K latency setting. With MiniMax H3 as the base model, SoL-Refiner produces 2K videos in three refinement steps and is preferred over the official MiniMax H3 2K API in 87% of user-study judgments.|[2609.37969](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37969)|
|**2026-11-05**|**What Do Verifiable Rewards Teach Video-Language Models About Time? A Controlled Multi-Model Study**|Avyay Sadhu et.al.|Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR) has produced large reasoning gains in language models, and verifiable video benchmarks make the recipe directly applicable to causal-temporal video question answering. We present a controlled study of what RLVR actually teaches video-language models about temporal structure. We fine-tune four open models with group relative policy optimization (GRPO) under three data recipes using annotated synthetic questions, self-supervised real-video pretext tasks, and a one-to-one mixture, plus a targeted real-video augmentation arm. We evaluate the model-recipe grid on 105 CLEVRER questions with one correct option and on two real-video transfer sets. The 8B ordered gain is 4.8 points (95% video-bootstrap CI [-5.6, 15.1]), while blind accuracy increases 10.5 points ([0.9, 20.6]). The 4B model gains an accuracy advantage for ordered over shuffled frames. On the real-video sets, the 8B synthetic-only checkpoint loses 26.7 and 25.2 points, with smaller regressions under mixed and augmented recipes. In a separate scene-disjoint, single-gold Qwen3-VL-8B experiment, supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and GRPO each complete 1,200 updates at three fixed seeds. GRPO minus SFT is -3.89 points in ordered accuracy (nominal 97.5% video-bootstrap CI [-7.36, -0.45]) and -2.78 points in ordered-minus-shuffled accuracy ([-6.60, 0.87]). Transfer differences are sensitive to parseability. Intervals quantify test-video uncertainty conditional on the fitted runs. The results show why benchmark accuracy, transfer, and presented-order sensitivity need to be evaluated separately.|[2610.03792](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03792)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Dynamic Quadtree Tokenization and Transformer for Adaptive Mesh PDE Forecasting**|Yilin Zhuang et.al.|The quadratic attention cost of Vision Transformers (ViTs) forces a trade-off between spatial resolution and rollout horizon, particularly for fine-scale PDEs where shocks, reaction fronts, and material interfaces occupy small, evolving regions of the domain. Conventional neural surrogates also lack mechanisms to adapt resolution dynamically. We propose WAMRViT, a ViT that tokenizes inputs as balanced quadtrees using a wavelet-inspired refinement criterion, jointly encodes position and refinement level with 3D rotary positional embeddings, and regrids in cell space during inference for stable long-horizon rollouts. A multi-scale variant retains each leaf at its native source resolution and lets the model learn across resolution levels. Unlike fixed-budget adaptive-tokenization methods, WAMRViT imposes no predetermined token count and supports fully adaptive topology throughout autoregressive rollout. To our knowledge, it is the first machine-learning surrogate to natively tokenize multi-level Adaptive Mesh Refinement (AMR) data. On uniform-grid benchmarks, uniform-patch WAMRViT improves finest-level region-of-interest VRMSE over a finest-patch uniform ViT while using substantially fewer tokens. The multi-scale variant achieves the lowest first-step full-field RMSE and VRMSE on both benchmarks and improves rollout-averaged full-field and refined-region accuracy at long horizons. With parallelized regridding, its end-to-end rollout cost lies between finest-patch and approximately token-matched coarser-patch ViTs. On a complex AMR combustion problem whose finest features cannot be represented natively by the evaluated uniform-grid baselines, WAMRViT operates on adaptive cells built from the simulator hierarchy and substantially reduces finest-level error at matched transformer capacity. Code: https://github.com/tonyzyl/wamrvit|[2610.04044](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04044)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Feed-Forward Multi-View Feature Coding for 3D Gaussian Splatting**|Seungmi Choi et.al.|Feed-forward 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) methods enable efficient reconstruction of 3D scenes from sparse multi-view images in a single forward pass, without scene-specific optimization. However, existing feed-forward scene-coding pipelines follow a "generate-then-compress" framework, first generating a Gaussian representation and then encoding its geometry and appearance through separate coding paths, complicating codec design and limiting the exploitation of cross-attribute redundancy. We propose multi-view feature coding for Gaussian splatting (MFGS), an end-to-end feed-forward framework for scene coding. Instead of compressing an explicit Gaussian representation, MFGS extracts multi-view features from the input images and encodes them into a compact bitstream. Through end-to-end rate-distortion training, MFGS learns a compact latent representation of multi-view features that jointly represents geometry and appearance, enabling the receiver to predict Gaussian attributes from the decoded features. We further introduce importance-guided Gaussian selection to accommodate different rendering budgets, allowing the receiver to adjust the number of Gaussians used for rendering from the same bitstream. Across RE10K, ACID, and DL3DV, MFGS achieves 99.76-99.86% bitrate savings over the generate-then-compress pipeline, as measured by BD-rate, while reducing total GMACs by 52-55% and providing an approximately 12 times end-to-end speedup.|[2610.20590](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20590)|
|**2026-11-05**|**AdaLoop: Thinking with the Vision Encoder**|Xinran Nicole Han et.al.|Most vision-language models (VLMs) treat the vision encoder as a static feature extractor: it encodes an image once, independently of the question, and leaves subsequent reasoning to the language model. Yet different questions may require different visual operations and processing depths. We present AdaLoop, a framework for question-conditioned visual recurrence in VLMs. AdaLoop adapts visual computation to the question and reuses the encoder's transformations across multiple iterations, with the pretrained vision weights frozen. We also introduce VisRO, a procedural dataset and generation pipeline for training and evaluating vision-centric reasoning. On six VisRO tasks, AdaLoop outperforms standard fine-tuning and question-conditioned single-pass baselines with matched training data and optimizer updates. These gains hold across three VLM families on both in-distribution and out-of-distribution examples. Our findings suggest that pretrained vision encoders can contribute to reasoning through task-directed, iterative visual computation.|[2610.21323](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21323)|
|**2026-11-05**|**From Adults to Children: Benchmarking Foundation Models for Chest X-Ray Classification under Domain Shift**|Sara In\'acio et.al.|Foundation models have shown significant potential across diverse fields and have been applied to medical imaging, but their robustness across patient populations remains unclear. In this work, we investigate foundation models' ability to generalize from adult to pediatric chest X-ray classification. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first benchmark to compare multiple foundation models under a common adult-to-pediatric evaluation setting while also analyzing the effect of adaptation depth. We harmonize VinDr-CXR and PediCXR into a five-label classification task and evaluate six foundation models and two CNN baselines. All models are adapted using adult data only and tested in both the in-domain and pediatric domains using linear probing, partial fine-tuning, and full fine-tuning. The benchmark reveals a clear and consistent trend: all models suffer a substantial performance degradation when transferred from adult to pediatric data, with drops exceeding 10 percentage points. While Macro-AUROC values reach approximately 92% on the adult test set, performance decreases to around 78% on the pediatric domain. Another important finding is that increasing the depth of adaptation improves pediatric performance on average, although the effect varies across models. Moreover, strong in-domain performance does not necessarily indicate greater robustness to domain shift.|[2610.21809](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21809)|
|**2026-11-05**|**FutureLift: Lifting Trajectory Proposals into Bidirectional Future Graphs for Motion Forecasting**|Daniel Grimm et.al.|Accurate motion prediction is critical for safe autonomous driving, yet even strong forecasters leave residual errors in their multimodal proposals. Existing refiners compress complete proposals or update their segments sequentially, preventing the full proposed future from serving as explicit relational context for correction. We introduce FutureLift, a backbone-agnostic refiner that represents each multimodal proposal as a heterogeneous future graph, with spatially grounded temporal nodes connected to agent histories, map elements, alternative modes, and, for joint prediction, other agents future trajectories. Bidirectional temporal edges allow later evidence to revise earlier segments in a single pass, and the updated nodes predict residual coordinate and confidence corrections. Requiring only proposals, scores, and scene context, FutureLift provides a common refinement interface for structurally different forecasting backbones. On Argoverse2 and the Waymo Open Motion Dataset, it consistently improves frozen proposals and outperforms native and specialized refiners across marginal and joint forecasting. On the WOMD motion-prediction leaderboard, FutureLift ranks first in minADE (0.5049m) and minFDE (0.9942m) among the compared standard-input methods, without LiDAR or ensembling, while achieving a competitive 0.4874 Soft mAP.|[2610.22215](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22215)|
|**2026-11-05**|**FrameScout: Scouting Query-Relevant Frames for Long Video Understanding**|Haonan Hu et.al.|Long video understanding requires VideoLLMs to answer queries about videos spanning tens of minutes to hours, but such long videos produce far more visual tokens than current VideoLLMs can process. To address this issue, keyframe selectors are proposed to select the most query-relevant frames by computing the similarity between frame and query embeddings. Existing selectors either encode frames independently without temporal context or process chunks in isolation with ranking objectives that are difficult to optimize. To alleviate those limitations, we propose FrameScout with two core designs: (i) a streaming selector architecture that leverages a sliding-window KV cache and successor aggregation to produce temporally-contextualized frame embeddings; (ii) a frame-query contrastive objective that aligns frame and query embeddings in a shared space, directly training the selector to distinguish relevant frames from irrelevant ones. Extensive experiments on three long-video benchmarks and six base VideoLLMs demonstrate that FrameScout consistently achieves state-of-the-art performance. Code is available at https://github.com/Hubert-1102/FrameScout.|[2610.23975](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23975)|
|**2026-11-05**|**PanoDent-KD: From Generalist Knowledge Distillation to Cross-Dataset Specialist Detection and Embedded Deployment in Panoramic Radiographs**|Muhammad Waqas Ashraf et.al.|Reliable panoramic-radiograph detection requires consistency across datasets with different class definitions and deployment software. We present PanoDent-KD, an auditable 1280-pixel YOLO26m pipeline linking a 12-class source generalist, feature-level knowledge distillation (KD), exact classifier-row transfer, teacher-free five-class specialization, and embedded deployment. A common training and evaluation pipeline compares three source-training arms across three seeds: a supervised baseline (B1), single-teacher KD (STKD), and multi-teacher KD (MTKD). Five shared classifier rows initialize nine OralXrays-9 specialists, while the backbone, FPN-PAN neck, and dual-detection-head topology are retained unchanged. On the 2,686-image test subset, all three arms achieve macro box F1 near 0.82. B1 achieves the highest three-seed arm-level mean macro box F1 (0.8239$\pm$0.0027) and $mAP_{50:95}$ (0.6130$\pm$0.0067). STKD seed 1, selected after development but before final testing, was used for deployment. Its Jetson Orin Nano Super FP16 TensorRT engine achieves 77.03-ms median and 80.86-ms P95 prediction-call latency, throughput of 12.90 images/s, and gross total-module energy of 1.39 J/image, with high numerical agreement between within-device PyTorch and TensorRT FP16 inference. These findings demonstrate the feasibility of an auditable, device-specific deployment workflow, but do not establish clinical validity or improved target-domain accuracy from KD.|[2610.25256](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25256)|
|**2026-11-05**|**AEON: Unifying Video and 3D World Models**|Weiqi Zhang et.al.|Current world models for explicit world simulation typically follow two distinct pathways: (1) video generative models (e.g., Sora), which simulate realistic appearances but fail to preserve 3D consistency; and (2) 3D generative models (e.g., Marble), which generate geometrically consistent scenes but often yield static, less realistic observations. We present AEON, a unified framework that bridges video-based and 3D-based world models and combines the benefits of both. The key insight of AEON lies in a generative model that operates within the text- or image-conditioned video latent space while producing renderable 4D graphics primitives---specifically, 4D Gaussian Splatting (4DGS)---as the explicit world representation. We train a 4D reconstruction model to predict both 4DGS and camera poses from monocular videos, serving as our initialized 4D decoder. Furthermore, we design a domain adapter that translates generative video latents into reconstructive 4D latents through a distillation process on the video VAE. AEON naturally supports video-based world modeling by rendering 4DGS at predicted camera trajectories, and constructs a persistent 4D environment with scene dynamics for realistic world simulation. Consequently, AEON empowers a diverse range of world modeling applications and achieves state-of-the-art performance on standard benchmarks. Project Page: https://weiqi-zhang.github.io/AEON|[2610.28631](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28631)|
|**2026-11-05**|**From Augmentation to Synthetic Evidence: Evaluating Generated Chest Radiographs at Dataset Intake**|Shaikh Akib Shahriyar et.al.|While generated chest radiographs can serve as valuable data augmentation when explicitly declared, the same pixels become unsupported evidence when submitted as records of real clinical examinations. We study this dataset-intake vulnerability by disentangling three questions: (1) whether an image passes an automated technical quality check, (2) whether an origin classifier flags it as synthetic, and (3) whether a trusted provenance record supports the claimed clinical acquisition. In this study, a submitter uses Qwen3.8-27B to select up to 20 candidate radiographs from pools of 1,000 Cheff-identified or SynthCheX images, while a receiver evaluates image quality via MedGemma 1.5 and predicts origin using a linear probe over frozen DINOv3 features. Although quality selection increases MedGemma quality passes from 12/20 to 19/20 for Cheff-identified images and from 8/20 to 17/20 for SynthCheX, we find no evidence that candidate selection increases passage through both checks simultaneously: zero selected Cheff-identified images pass both gates, and uniform, quality-guided, and surrogate-guided selection yield 2, 1, and 2 SynthCheX passes out of 20, respectively. On unprocessed test cohorts, the origin classifier rejects 4,677 of 5,000 SynthCheX images at a nominal 5% false-alarm rate (flagging 110 of 2,297 real NIH radiographs), yet leaves 152 SynthCheX images that pass both pixel-level checks. Moreover, pixel-level screening is fragile to routine distribution shifts: JPEG compression reduces SynthCheX-versus-NIH AUROC from 0.987 to 0.841, and applying the NIH-calibrated threshold to an external genuine cohort (Open-I) triggers false alarms on 568 of 957 authentic radiographs (59.4%). Because pixel-level checks can filter submissions but cannot verify clinical acquisition claims, we argue that dataset intake pipelines must record and verify source provenance independently of image pixels.|[2610.29152](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29152)|
|**2026-11-05**|**From Presence Labels to Bounding Boxes: Can Multimodal Large Language Models Scale Surgical Instrument Localization?**|Mohamed Hamdy et.al.|Spatial localization of surgical instruments through bounding-box annotations is a foundational component of surgical scene understanding, supporting detection, tracking, interaction analysis, and grounded vision-language applications. Yet existing annotations often cover selected frames, video subsets, or dataset variants rather than complete procedures. Manual extension is time-consuming and expert-dependent. Additionally, weakly supervised and detector-based pseudo-labels require task-specific training and may be imprecise. In this study, we design a scalable MLLM-based annotation framework that converts surgical frames and existing instrument-presence labels into accurate bounding boxes, and systematically analyze its reliability, cost, and design sensitivity. We instantiate the framework using six MLLMs and a multi-call configuration on laparoscopic cholecystectomy in Cholec80. Using a carefully designed stratification scheme, two annotators independently label 1,000 frames, and an expert surgeon reviews one annotation set to establish the reference. Results show that instrument-set F1 ranges from 88.2 to 92.7,whereas mIoU ranges from 0.180 to 0.735, showing that comparable recognition can conceal large localization differences. A structured multi-stage annotation pipeline achieves 0.784 mIoU, compared with 0.735 for the best single-call baseline and 0.814 human-human agreement. Using the best accuracy pipeline, we create and publicly release SurgBox, a spatial extension of Cholec80 comprising 285,977 bounding boxes across all 184,498 annotated frames from the 80 videos. The annotations and human references are made available on GitHub|[2610.34218](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34218)|
|**2026-11-05**|**TokenRepel: Generating Diverse Image Sets Without Compromising Quality**|Qingtao Yu et.al.|We present an inference-time approach that enhances the visual diversity of image sets generated by text-to-image models, without compromising the average image quality. Our method leverages the intrinsic geometry of hidden representations in pretrained models to diversify images generated from the same prompt. First, we introduce a token repulsion mechanism that increases the angle between feature tokens across samples. It takes a negative Riemannian gradient step on a von Mises-Fisher mixture distribution of tokens on the unit hypersphere. Second, to preserve the quality of each output image, we propose a token density preservation mechanism that projects the repulsion-induced prediction change onto a half-space aligned with the model's score direction. This encourages the updates to remain within the high-density regions learned by the generative model. We further extend this mechanism to the output of each intermediate layer, enabling finer-grained iterative guidance. We evaluate our method on three types of pretrained text-to-image models. Experiments show substantial gains in visual diversity under identical prompts, while maintaining a comparable level of image fidelity.|[2610.39913](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39913)|
|**2026-11-05**|**GeoFlip Preview: Benchmarking Geometry-Sensitive Physics in Video Generation**|Yuqi Li et.al.|Video generation models synthesize realistic motion, but can they respond correctly to changes in initial geometry? We introduce GeoFlip, a video generation benchmark centered on physical outcome flips induced by geometry. With shared physical settings and textual prompts held fixed, GeoFlip applies controlled interventions to support extent, object placement, and mechanism configuration, constructing paired scenes with contrasting reference events, such as remaining stable versus falling or tipping. These pairs test whether models translate geometric differences in the first frame into correct physical evolution. The benchmark comprises 482 scene pairs and 964 reference videos across six physical categories, with reference outcomes established through procedural scene construction, physics simulation, and numerical validation and verification of physical events. We evaluate 16 models for image to video generation along three dimensions: event correctness, structural integrity, and similarity to reference videos. We examine evaluation consistency with independent vision language judges. On the 442 conditions shared by all models, the highest event correctness averaged across tasks is 27.98%, revealing substantial limitations in physical generation conditioned on geometry. Model strengths vary across physical mechanisms, and the three evaluation dimensions capture distinct capabilities. Typical failures include ignoring geometric changes, generating incorrect object motion, bending rigid objects, and introducing additional components. Rank correlations between judges reach 0.906 for event correctness and 0.929 for structural integrity. By combining controlled geometric interventions, verifiable physical references, and multidimensional evaluation, GeoFlip assesses progress from visual realism toward physical consistency conditioned on geometry.|[2610.42002](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42002)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Learning to Group and Order: Cross-Instance Self-Supervised RL for Vision-Centric MLLMs**|Songming Yang et.al.|Reinforcement learning (RL) post-training has become an important paradigm for improving multimodal large language models (MLLMs), especially with automatically verifiable rewards. Self-supervised visual pretext tasks provide such rewards without human annotations. However, existing methods mainly exploit within-instance structure, offering limited supervision for cross-instance discrimination and fine-grained comparison. We introduce Group-and-Order Self-Supervised Reinforcement Learning (GO-SSL), a cross-instance jigsaw framework for vision-centric MLLM post-training. Given two visual instances, GO-SSL mixes their local elements and trains the model to group them by instance of origin while recovering the order within each group, coupling instance-level comparison with spatial, temporal, or geometric structure recovery. This paired formulation turns the same unlabeled data into richer verifiable supervision through diverse pairings, shuffles, and a random-to-hard curriculum. We instantiate GO-SSL mainly on cross-image jigsaw tasks and further extend it to 3D depth and video temporal ordering. Across diverse benchmarks, GO-SSL consistently improves over the base MLLM and prior self-supervised post-training baselines, with clear gains in fine-grained perception and spatial understanding. Further evaluations across model scales and families support its generality. These results suggest that cross-instance comparison provides a useful self-supervised RL signal for improving transferable visual understanding in MLLMs. Code is available at https://github.com/SEU-VIPGroup/GO-SSL.|[2611.00892](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00892)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Testing concept-level audits of melanoma classifiers with diffusion counterfactuals and matched positive controls**|Md Shohanoor Rahman et.al.|Concept-based explanations of melanoma classifiers are rarely tested against controls with known targets. We built a counterfactual audit from a diffusion model conditioned on seven 7-point checklist concepts and tested it with such controls. Colour-calibrated start noise reduced colour outliers from 78.5-84.8\% to 6.6-13.3\%. Four of seven knobs were target-dominant but also changed other concepts. Quartile-based positive controls were confounded, and the audit failed its pre-specified rule. It recovered the targets of all 16 controls matched on the generator's pseudo-labels and of 12 of 12 controls labelled by a second evaluator, for three concepts; concept activation vectors recovered fewer (11 of 16). In 18 permuted-label classifiers, significant sweep responses and target rankings also arose, whereas standardised effect margins separated the controls from these null classifiers. The pigmentation sweep raised the melanoma logit in 15 of 15 ISIC 2020 classifiers (+3.12 on average) and 6 of 6 HAM10000 classifiers (+4.01), about four times the null response in scale-free units; the regression effect did not transfer. An exploratory decomposition, evaluated against the controls, suggested that knob cross-talk partly hides vascular responses. Matched controls and null classifiers are practical checks of concept audits.|[2611.02119](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02119)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Conditional Diffusion-Based Generation of T2-Weighted Prostate MRI for Clinically Significant Prostate Cancer Classification**|Xavier Rafael-Palou (Quantitative Imaging Biomarkers in Medicine) et.al.|Scarce and unrepresentative training data limit the performance of prostate MRI prediction models. Synthetic T2-weighted (T2W) MRI may augment training data containing clinically significant prostate cancer (csPCa) and transition zone (TZ) lesions. In this retrospective, single-center, ethics-committee-approved study, we developed a conditional generative model for T2W prostate MRI extending MAISI-v2 with csPCa class, zonal, and lesion-mask-guided conditioning, incorporating a relative advantage weighted loss fine-tuned on 1,303 patients (1,654 lesions). Fidelity was assessed by incremental ablation, and downstream utility was evaluated with T2W-based csPCa classifiers across five simulated training-data scenarios, each testing 18 synthetic-data configurations. The proposed model reduced Fr\'echet inception distance (FID) from 13.28 (pretrained MAISI-v2) to $2.04 \pm 0.06$ internally (PI-CAI: $3.83 \pm 0.51$). Synthetic augmentation significantly increased AUC in all five scenarios (+0.027 to +0.088; adjusted $p = 0.016$--$0.043$), though cross-institutional augmentation of PI-CAI yielded a non-significant gain (+0.013; $p = 0.397$). Overall, our model improved T2W MRI fidelity, and synthetic images improved csPCa classification under simulated data constraints, although benefit did not transfer significantly across institutions.|[2611.02197](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02197)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Exploiting Textual Semantics for Robust Cross-View Object Correspondence**|Bing Fan et.al.|Cross-view object correspondence (CVOC) aims to establish associations of the same target between query and search videos captured from different viewpoints. Existing methods often rely on visual cues for CVOC. Despite recent progress, these methods struggle in complicated scenarios with substantial viewpoint changes and appearance variations, due to lacking sufficient target information. Addressing this, we introduce TeSCo, a framework that exploits rich Textual Semantics of an object, in addition to its visual cues, for cross-view object Correspondence. Our key insight is, textual description of a target, by capturing diverse attributes such as category, texture, and color, provides viewpoint- and appearance-invariant semantics, which are complementary to visual cues and can hence enhance correspondence robustness in complicated scenes. Inspired by this, TeSCo first generates a textual expression of the target from the given query view using a vision-language model, and then extracts textual feature to enhance visual features for localization in the search view. To realize this, we introduce a text-conditioned cross-modal fusion (TCF) module that incorporates the textual feature into visual features with text-guided modulation, producing more robust multimodal target representation for object correspondence. Since not all textual cues are equally compatible with the target in current search-view frame, we propose an iterative textual feature refinement (ITFR) module that progressively adjusts textual feature using search-view information before the TCF module, enabling search-view-aware textual semantics to be fused into visual features and leading to better performance. In extensive experiments on Ego-Exo4D and HANDAL-X, TeSCo demonstrates state-of-the-art results and largely outperforms other models, validating its efficacy. Our code is released at https://github.com/VAIL-Fan/TeSCo.|[2611.02356](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02356)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Gnomon4D: Learning Where and When to Focus for Geometric Motion-Grounded World-Action Modeling**|Lingwei Dang et.al.|World-action models (WAMs) predict scene changes to guide robot actions, yet their RGB futures entangle state changes with appearance and lack 3D geometry. Recent WAMs add geometry as a training signal or a control interface, or build native 3D models, but decide what geometry to model rather than how the action should read it. Intuitively, control-relevant cues lie at embodiment--object interactions and their transitions, yet probing existing WAMs reveals that their action attention underweights these interactions in favor of the rest of the embodiment. To focus the action on these interactions, we present Gnomon4D, a WAM that jointly generates future video, robot actions, and GeoDelta, a video-aligned 4D geometric motion field recording the image-plane displacement and depth change of embodiment and object points. With the embodiment and objects in one field, their relative motion, which marks an interaction, can be read off. Guided by this motion and the task, Interaction-Salient Attention (InSAttn) selects where the action reads, and Transition-Anchored Aggregation (TransAgg) selects when, around grasp and release. As GeoDelta encodes surface motion rather than embodiment-specific controls, it serves as a human--robot quasi-action that links action-free pretraining on EgoManip-5000H, which we curate, to robot mid-training on Robot-30H. Gnomon4D leads on RoboTwin~2.0, VLABench, and RoboDojo, and outperforms real-robot baselines, especially under visual shifts. Project page: https://droliven.github.io/Gnomon4D.|[2611.02474](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02474)|
|**2026-11-05**|**PhotoGuider: Composition-Aware Cropping and 360{\deg} View Framing with Explainable Photographic Assessments**|Yuan-Kang Lee et.al.|Traditional image cropping assumes the photograph already contains the composition worth keeping, and asks only where to crop. A full 360$^{\circ}$ scene offers no such guarantee: the viewing direction in a scene has to be discovered before anything can be framed. We present PhotoGuider, a measure-then-frame method that answers both questions with compact learned scorers over one shared set of composition-aware features. The designed features are extracted using foundation and task-specific vision models and structured into four photographic tokens: Subject, Composition, Aesthetics, and Integrity. Our MLLM explainer is fine-tuned to generate composition-related explanations conditioned on images and measured token assessments. To our knowledge, PhotoGuider is the first method to combine explicit composition-based direction selection in 360$^{\circ}$ panoramas with refined framing, camera-parameter output for native re-rendering, and measurement-conditioned explanations. On two standard 2D cropping benchmarks, PhotoGuider achieves the highest average mIoU performance against ground-truth crops. In a blind ranking study, 120 participants ranked three cropping methods over 100 panoramic scenes from the public Poly Haven HDRI library, placing our framings first in 53.2% of cases, against 27.3% and 19.5% for two strong alternatives. Project page: https://ntuneillee.github.io/research/photoguider/|[2611.02897](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02897)|
|**2026-11-05**|**3D Gaussian Splatting within Self-Learned Neural View-Dependent Color Fields**|Yixiao Yang et.al.|3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) has become a vital tool in novel view synthesis. 3D Gaussian functions with attributes like color, opacity, and scale are stacked together to explicitly model a radiance field. However, discrete 3D Gaussians usually produce poor depth with few geometry details but lots of floaters, which hinders the effective modeling of smooth radiance fields. To address this issue, we propose to learn a radiance field within a continuous view-dependent color field parameterized by neural networks. With continuous constraints in color, the bias on low-frequency signals of neural networks can highly encourage the geometry modeling to contribute to high-frequency color variations on images, getting rid of solely relying on the color attribute itself. Moreover, we impose a constraint to improve the Lipschitz continuity of the neural color field, making large changes of each Gaussian's color also match with large changes of its position in terms of a ratio. To this end, we additionally introduce novel self-learning strategies to improve the learning of the neural view-dependent color field without any external knowledge or priors, aiming for better generalization on unseen scenes. Our numerical and visual comparisons on widely used benchmarks justify our idea and show better ability of high fidelity geometry recovery in 3DGS. Project page: https://github.com/yangyixi21/Neural-Color-Fields-3DGS.|[2611.03114](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03114)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Bg-Mix: On Using Image Backgrounds for Data Augmentation in Electron Microscopy Images**|Ankur Srivastava et.al.|Electron microscopy (EM) serves as a cornerstone in virology enabling the visualization of viral particles and is widely used in research, diagnostics and morphological characterization of pathogens. Computer-assisted image analysis can help facilitate and speed up this labor-intensive method, however, typical EM virus datasets are small and sparsely labeled, resulting in a lack of readily available deep learning models due to training limitations. To overcome data shortages and leverage content-rich, diverse sample backgrounds, we employed background-based data augmentation. To this end, we propose Bg-Mix, a form of data augmentation method, which decomposes training images into foreground objects and backgrounds and subsequently rearranges both into novel augmented images. This not only enhances the performance of object detection models but also increases the diversity of the training data. Using Bg-Mix provides an increase between +0.8 and +3.3 box mean average precision (mAP) points over the baseline when tested across two datasets using two architectures, thereby demonstrating its effectiveness.|[2611.03455](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03455)|
|**2026-11-05**|**FlowRig: Unified Skeleton Generation for 3D and 4D Meshes via Flow Matching**|Fengzhi Guo et.al.|Automatic rigging is a fundamental task for both static 3D and dynamic 4D mesh animation, yet existing methods do not provide a unified feedforward solution for these two settings. While static 3D riggers achieve strong quality, they often provide limited control over bone count and become temporally unstable when applied frame by frame to dynamic meshes. Existing 4D methods, in contrast, typically rely on expensive per-sequence optimization. We propose FlowRig, a template-free, data-efficient framework for unified 3D and 4D skeleton generation. Built on flow matching, FlowRig generates temporally aligned skeletons for an entire sequence in a single feedforward pass, while naturally handling single-mesh 3D rigging. The model further supports explicit skeleton-count control, uncertainty estimation for candidate selection, and masking-based interpolation, forecasting, and backcasting. To address limited paired 4D rigging data, we adopt a curriculum that initializes from large-scale 3D rigging data and adapts to mid-scale 4D mesh-skeleton data. Experiments show that FlowRig achieves strong static 3D rigging performance with controllable bone complexity, as well as high-quality and temporally consistent 4D reconstruction and prediction results.|[2611.03775](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03775)|
|**2026-11-05**|**FloMo: 3D Scene Flow as a World Action Model Intermediate for Learning from Human Video**|Jeremy Collins et.al.|Pretrained video generation models distill internet-scale motion priors into robot policies, but full video is a redundant signal for action prediction. We argue that dense 3D motion is the medium through which video pretraining best transfers to robot actions, and that predicting motion rather than video affords better sample-efficiency for action prediction. FloMo is a 5B video-generation model fine-tuned to predict 3D scene-flow and action tokens. Rendering scene flow as RGB and encoding it into the same latent space as video enables a pretrained video generation backbone to efficiently adapt to predict 3D scene flow. Co-trained on action-free human video and action-labeled robot teleoperation data, our model benefits from human-video pretraining and generalizes to unseen objects, motion primitives, and tasks. Replacing motion prediction with a video prediction objective degrades out-of-distribution generalization, while adding a video prediction target alongside motion lowers policy success rather than improving it. When motion, video, and action are predicted jointly, action tokens attend far more to motion than to video, suggesting that predicting video wastes model capacity. Together, these results indicate that motion effectively mediates the transfer from video pretraining to action. More information can be found at flomo-wam.github.io.|[2611.05062](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05062)|
|**2026-11-05**|**High-fidelity training-free photovoltaic module mapping from multi-view UAV imagery**|WenHao Duan et.al.|High-fidelity photovoltaic (PV) module mapping requires complete instance recovery and accurate delineation. Vision foundation models offer zero-shot segmentation, but converting incomplete and inconsistent predictions across views into a complete, geometrically accurate map of individual modules remains challenging. We propose the Multi-View PV Module Mapper (MV-PVMapper), a training-free framework coupling vision foundation models with unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV) photogrammetry. First, Segment Anything Model 3 (SAM 3) generates candidates from original images using text prompts. Their contours are then transferred into object space through per-module slope correction to limit distortion from surface-model noise, and verified against representative module dimensions to guide cross-view instance selection. Finally, accepted instances return to all covering images as box prompts for SAM~3, supplementing text prompts with spatial cues for iterative segmentation, verification and fusion. On five-direction surveys of three operational rooftop PV plants at approximately 1 cm nadir ground sampling distance, two feedback iterations recover 7,710 of 7,711 reference modules with no false positives at an intersection-over-union (IoU) matching threshold of 0.5. Macro-averaged area precision and recall reach 99.00 % and 98.52 %, respectively. Averaged across sites, the aggregated Jaccard index reaches 97.55 %, compared with 60.73 % and 41.24 % for orthomosaic segmentation and multi-view pixel-voting baselines, respectively; average precision at the stricter IoU threshold of 0.95 reaches 95.67 %. Ablation studies show that module-size constraints are critical for instance separation, while slope correction and feedback to all covering views improve boundary accuracy. These results are achieved without task-specific image annotation or segmentation-model weight updates.|[2611.05131](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05131)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Factorized MLLM Embeddings for Composed Image Retrieval**|WonJun Moon et.al.|Composed Image Retrieval (CIR) aims to retrieve a target image given a reference image and a modification text. Although recent approaches leveraging Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have shown significant progress, we argue that the MLLM's compositional understanding remains underutilized. This stems from directly mapping composed queries into a retrieval embedding via contrastive supervision alone, without explicitly resolving the intended visual modifications. In this work, we introduce Factorized MLLM Embeddings (FactEmbed), which decouples compositional query comprehension from building discriminative embeddings. Rather than directly projecting the composed query into a single embedding without internal reasoning, FactEmbed employs a set of factorized latents that simulate the intended visual modifications via an intermediate target imagination objective. This process encourages the latents to ground into target semantics before forming discriminative representations for retrieval. Subsequently, to propagate these factorized representations throughout the model for retrieval, we optimize a factor-level contrastive loss while distilling the decomposed representations into a deeper attention layer of the MLLM. Finally, for efficient inference, we retain the [EOS] token to establish a global-level embedding on top of these factorized latents, while we exploit the mean-pooled factorized latents to rerank a short candidate list at negligible cost, as both are produced by the same forward pass. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that FactEmbed outperforms prior works across CIR benchmarks.|[2611.05193](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05193)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Diffusion Plug-in Priors for Self-Supervised Ultrasound Video Restoration**|Chu Chen et.al.|Ultrasound videos are limited by hardware-constrained spatial resolution and signal-dependent speckle, while paired low- and high-resolution acquisitions for supervised restoration are rarely available. We aim to jointly super-resolve and despeckle ultrasound videos without paired training data or network retraining. We propose UltraDiff, which uses a frozen pre-trained diffusion model (DM) as a plug-in prior and restores each frame by optimizing the latent seed of the DM sampler under a range-constrained maximum a posteriori formulation. We derive a speckle-aware data-fidelity term from a Rayleigh scale-family model and characterize its relative-residual and variance-adaptive weighting properties. We further introduce a stabilized fidelity that is bounded below and strictly convex in log intensity for positive observations in its unclamped form. A total-variation regularizer complements the DM prior, and a temporal seed update warm-starts each frame from the optimized seed of its predecessor. On EchoNet-LVH videos with simulated speckle and additive noise, UltraDiff with a natural-image prior improves PSNR by 1.24\,dB over the strongest baseline, and ultrasound-related priors improve it further. On real clinical echocardiography videos, UltraDiff reduces the mean absolute error of zero-shot ejection fraction (EF) prediction from 7.31 to 6.37 EF points relative to DUP, the strongest baseline. Combining a speckle-aware fidelity with frozen diffusion priors enables ground-truth-free ultrasound video restoration that benefits downstream measurement. UltraDiff needs neither paired data nor retraining and is not tied to a specific diffusion checkpoint. Code associated with this work will be made publicly available upon acceptance.|[2611.05206](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05206)|
|**2026-11-05**|**GSCV++: Lightweight 3D Gaussian Splatting Sequence Compression via Standard Video Codecs**|Qi Yang et.al.|This paper presents GSCV++, a lightweight pipeline for 3D {G}aussian {S}platting (GS) sequences {C}ompression with standard {V}ideo codecs. To meet the requirements of low-complexity and hardware compatibility from the industry, GSCV++ jointly improves preprocessing, 3D-to-2D mapping, and practical video compression configuration of GS compression. First, for preprocessing, energy-adaptive inter-frame spherical harmonic (SH) alternative current (AC) dimensionality reduction (EA-SHDR) is proposed to produces a compact and stable temporal SH AC feature space. It can reduce the data volume of following mapping and practical compression, as well as less complexity while eliminating redundant information. Second, for 3D-to-2D mapping, prior-based Inter-MiniPLAS (P-MiniPLAS) is designed to generate smooth 2D feature maps through prior-based weighting factor inference and small scale remapping. Compared with vanilla Parallel Linear Assignment Sorting (PLAS) and Morton scan, P-MiniPLAS ensures the generated 2D maps are more suitable for the video codec while maintaining a low level of computational complexity. Third, for practical compression, content-aware generalized compression (CA-GC) is developed to use a non-uniform bit-depth split for coordinate map generation, and a coding unit size selection is introduced for efficient compression complexity control, which fully exploits the performance of video codecs without involving normative changes. Experiments demonstrate that GSCV++ reports over 20\% in intra- and 60\% in inter-prediction rate-distortion gains compared with state-of-the-art (SOTA) methods. GSCV++ also achieves faster 2D map generation, encoding, and decoding than other SOTA methods using the same type of video codecs. Ablation studies verify the effectiveness and robustness of the proposed modules and provide insights for subsequent research.|[2611.05359](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05359)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Learning Optimal Lattice Vector Quantizers for End-to-end Neural Image Compression**|Xi Zhang et.al.|It is customary to deploy uniform scalar quantization in the end-to-end optimized Neural image compression methods, instead of more powerful vector quantization, due to the high complexity of the latter. Lattice vector quantization (LVQ), on the other hand, presents a compelling alternative, which can exploit inter-feature dependencies more effectively while keeping computational efficiency almost the same as scalar quantization. However, traditional LVQ structures are designed/optimized for uniform source distributions, hence nonadaptive and suboptimal for real source distributions of latent code space for Neural image compression tasks. In this paper, we propose a novel learning method to overcome this weakness by designing the rate-distortion optimal lattice vector quantization (OLVQ) codebooks with respect to the sample statistics of the latent features to be compressed. By being able to better fit the LVQ structures to any given latent sample distribution, the proposed OLVQ method improves the rate-distortion performances of the existing quantization schemes in neural image compression significantly, while retaining the amenability of uniform scalar quantization. Code is available at: https://github.com/xzhang9308/OLVQ.|[2411.16119](http://arxiv.org/abs/2411.16119)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Certified Circuits: Stability Guarantees for Mechanistic Circuits**|Alaa Anani et.al.|Understanding how neural networks arrive at their predictions is essential for debugging, auditing, and deployment. Mechanistic interpretability pursues this goal by identifying circuits--minimal subnetworks responsible for specific behaviors. However, existing circuit discovery methods are brittle: circuits depend strongly on the chosen concept dataset and often fail to transfer out-of-distribution, raising doubts whether they capture the concept or merely dataset-specific artifacts. We introduce Certified Circuits, which provide provable stability guarantees for circuit discovery. Our framework wraps any black-box discovery algorithm with randomized data subsampling to certify that inclusion decisions over circuit components--neurons or edges of the model graph, depending on the base algorithm--are invariant to bounded edit-distance perturbations of the concept dataset. Unstable components are abstained from, yielding circuits that are more compact and more accurate. We validate across three architectures (ResNet, ViT, GPT-2) on vision (ImageNet and four OOD datasets) and language (IOI, IOI-Hard, Greater-Than) tasks. Certified circuits achieve up to 56% higher accuracy and up to 80% fewer components, and remain reliable where baselines degrade. Certified Circuits puts circuit discovery on formal ground by producing mechanistic explanations that are provably stable and better aligned with the target concept. Code: https://github.com/AlaaAnani/certified-circuits.|[2602.22968](http://arxiv.org/abs/2602.22968)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Backdoor Attacks under Lossy Compression: From Failure to Reactivation and Adaptation**|Qian Li et.al.|Real-world backdoor attacks often require poisoned datasets to be stored and transmitted before they are used to compromise deep learning systems. In the era of big data, however, the inevitable use of lossy compression poses a fundamental challenge to invisible backdoor attacks. We observe that triggers embedded in RGB images can become ineffective once the images are lossily compressed into binary bitstreams, such as JPEG files, for storage and transmission. Consequently, poisoned data may lose their malicious functionality after compression, causing backdoor injection to fail. Prior compression-based attacks typically exploit compression artifacts as certain triggers to distinguish poisoned RGB samples from uncompressed benign ones, rather than addressing whether malicious information can survive a shared lossy storage-and-transmission pipeline. In this paper, we highlight the necessity of explicitly accounting for lossy compression in backdoor attacks. This requires attackers to ensure that transmitted binary bitstreams preserve malicious trigger information, such that effective triggers can be induced after decompression. Building on the region-of-interest (ROI) coding mechanism in image compression, we propose two poisoning strategies tailored to inevitable lossy compression. First, we introduce Universal Attack Reactivation, a general method that uses sample-specific ROI masks to reactivate trigger information in bitstreams for learned image compression (LIC). Second, we present Compression-Adapted Attack, a new attack strategy that employs customized ROI masks to encode trigger information into bitstreams and applies to both traditional codecs and LIC. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of both strategies.|[2603.13864](http://arxiv.org/abs/2603.13864)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Reinforcement Learning in GUI Agents: A Survey Toward Digital Inhabitants**|Junan Hu et.al.|Graphical User Interface (GUI) agents have emerged as a promising paradigm for intelligent systems that perceive and interact with graphical interfaces visually. Yet supervised fine-tuning alone cannot handle long-horizon credit assignment, distribution shifts, and safe exploration in irreversible environments, making Reinforcement Learning (RL) a central methodology for advancing automation. In this work, we present the first comprehensive overview of the intersection between RL and GUI agents, and examine how this research direction may evolve toward digital inhabitants. We propose a principled taxonomy that organizes existing methods into Offline RL, Online RL, and Hybrid Strategies, and complement it with analyses of reward engineering, data efficiency, and key technical innovations. Our analysis reveals several emerging trends: the tension between reliability and scalability is motivating the adoption of composite, multi-tier reward architectures; GUI I/O latency bottlenecks are accelerating the shift toward world-model-based training, which can yield substantial performance gains; and the spontaneous emergence of System-2-style deliberation suggests that explicit reasoning supervision may not be necessary when sufficiently rich reward signals are available. We distill these findings into a roadmap covering process rewards, continual RL, cognitive architectures, and safe deployment, aiming to guide the next generation of robust GUI automation and its agent-native infrastructure.|[2604.27955](http://arxiv.org/abs/2604.27955)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Bridging the Modality Bottleneck in Pathology MIL through Molecularly Guided Feature Adaptation**|Yucheng Xing et.al.|Multiple instance learning (MIL) is the dominant framework for whole-slide image analysis in computational pathology, typically combining a frozen patch encoder, a projection layer, and a slide-level aggregator. While encoders and aggregators have been extensively studied, the projection layer remains a largely morphology-only bottleneck. This limits endpoints such as biomarker status and survival, which are governed by a molecular state that is not fully captured by H&E morphology. We introduce Molecularly Informed Staining Transform (MIST), a plug-in replacement for the MIL projection layer that uses paired spatial transcriptomics only during training to construct virtual molecular stains. MIST clusters gene expression profiles into cross-modal prototypes, anchors them in the frozen foundation model feature space, and uses them to reorganize H&E patch features along molecularly guided axes. It requires no transcriptomics at inference and can be inserted before standard MIL aggregators. We evaluate MIST across 23 downstream tasks and 8 MIL aggregators. MIST improves 240 of 256 configurations over the standard projection layer, with an average gain of +3.5%, observed consistently across endpoint types: +5.2% on survival prediction, +3.3% on tissue subtyping, and +2.6% on biomarker prediction. Ablations confirm that gene-derived prototypes are the primary source of the gains, while spatial, biological, and pathological analyses show that cross-modal prototype affinities capture spatially coherent molecular programs from H&E alone.|[2605.16392](http://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16392)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Two-Stage Teacher-Student Reliable Prior Learning for Robust Underwater Image Enhancement**|Yifan Chen et.al.|Underwater image enhancement (UIE) aims to recover clear images from observations affected by wavelength-dependent absorption, scattering, and spatially nonuniform degradation. Although existing generative methods can handle complex degradations, severe information loss may lead to semantic drift in the restored results. To address this issue, we propose RPL-UIE, a two-stage teacher--student framework for reliable prior learning. In the teacher stage, the network learns reliable and complementary spatial priors characterizing appearance and photometric properties from paired degraded and reference images. To reduce the prior-learning discrepancy between the teacher and student models, we further develop Residual Prior Refinement Diffusion (RPRD) and Frequency-Aware Prior Residual Calibration (FPRC). RPRD uses the coarse priors as anchors and progressively predicts the necessary corrections in the residual space. FPRC retains stable low-frequency residual components and selectively modulates high-frequency detail residuals, producing calibrated priors to support high-quality reconstruction. Experiments on multiple UIE benchmarks demonstrate competitive restoration performance. Downstream underwater object detection and instance segmentation experiments further demonstrate the improved utility of enhanced images for visual perception, while tests on real-world data captured by a remotely operated vehicle (ROV) support the robustness and practical applicability of RPL-UIE.|[2608.00137](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00137)|
|**2026-11-05**|**MMUSV-Sim: A Maritime Simulation Platform for Multi-USV Cooperative Perception**|Ziao Li et.al.|Perception from a single unmanned surface vehicle (USV) is limited by sensing range, field of view, and occlusions caused by islands, port infrastructure, and nearby vessels. Multi-USV cooperative perception can alleviate these limitations by integrating complementary observations from distributed viewpoints. However, progress is constrained by the high cost of coordinated sea trials, the scarcity of multi-USV datasets, and the lack of integrated maritime simulation and evaluation tools. We present MMUSV-Sim, a perception-oriented maritime simulation and data-generation platform built on Unreal Engine 5 and Project AirSim. It supports configurable maritime scenarios, synchronized multimodal acquisition across multiple USVs, and consistent 3D annotations in a shared world frame. Using MMUSV-Sim, we construct MarU2U-200m, a synchronized multi-USV RGB--LiDAR dataset with 341 sequences and 14,385 scene frames. We establish a cooperative bird's-eye-view (BEV) vessel detection benchmark with LiDAR and RGB--LiDAR inputs. Experimental results show that Early Fusion improves LiDAR detection AP@0.5 from 64.26 to 81.46 compared with the single-USV baseline. RGB features further improve intermediate fusion, increasing AP@0.5 by up to 4.55 percentage points. LiDAR and RGB transfer experiments also show the value of the generated data as a pretraining source for real maritime detection. The project are available at https://github.com/lizaowo/MMUSV-Sim|[2608.14207](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14207)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Continuous Adversarial MeanFlow Transfer**|Yara Bahram et.al.|Training fast generators on new domains with limited data remains challenging for two reasons. First, adapting a pretrained diffusion or flow model leaves its costly multi-step sampling intact, and existing acceleration methods are tied to the source parameterization (x, epsilon, v, or u), so heterogeneous pretrained models share no common acceleration target. Second, while adversarial post-training sharpens generation quality, existing criteria each cover only one end of the step range. Discrete criteria compare jump endpoints and lose their signal as the jump shrinks, while continuous criteria score the instantaneous velocity and misjudge the large jumps of few-step sampling. Neither fits MeanFlow (MF) models, which predict average velocities over intervals of any length. We propose MeanFlow-Transfer (MF-T), which adapts a source domain model of any parameterization and compresses it into a few-step MF generator through a common velocity map. We then introduce Continuous Adversarial MeanFlow (CAMF), which scores the change of a learned potential along each predicted interval jump of MF. CAMF needs no gradient penalty or Jacobian-vector product (JVP). We also prove it stays well-conditioned for every interval length and recovers the instantaneous criterion as the interval vanishes. Adapting four ImageNet-based source models (DiT, SiT, JiT, iMF) to five target domains, MF-T + CAMF approaches or exceeds the fine-tuned many-step teacher in FID and FDD at up to 125x fewer Neural Function Evaluations (NFEs). |**2026-11-05**|**Scalable Neural Video Representation Compression**|Tianhao Peng et.al.|Scalable video coding (SVC) encodes a video into a layered bitstream of a base layer and one or more enhancement layers, enabling decoding at different bitrate/quality/resolution operating points for diverse devices and network conditions. SVC has been incorporated into major video coding standards and has recently attracted growing interest for both scene-agnostic and scene-adaptive neural video codecs. Among the latter, implicit neural representation (INR) based codecs overfit a compact neural network to an individual video, offering fast decoding and competitive coding efficiency. However, research on scalable INR-based compression remains in its infancy: existing methods introduce additional network layers, which couple the bitrate with the decoding complexity and cannot match strong scalable/non-scalable codecs. This paper proposes S-NVRC, a scalable INR-based video codec that jointly supports fine-grained bitrate and decoding complexity scalability from a single embedded bitstream. It adopts a coarse-to-fine prefix for feature grids and a nested prefix for network layers, which scale bitrate and decoding complexity, respectively. With a single encoding, S-NVRC spans a wide range of bitrate and decoding complexity and outperforms SHM-12.4 and the multi-layer VTM-20.0 by 43.7% and 5.6% in BD-rate on the UVG dataset.|[2609.04273](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04273)|
|**2026-11-05**|**SimForcing: Distilling Simulation Motion Priors into Real-Domain Robot World Models**|Xiaodong Wang et.al.|Action-conditioned robot world models must respond precisely to robot trajectories while preserving realistic visual dynamics, yet learning both from heterogeneous robot videos remains challenging. Second, we introduce multi-block simulation conditioning with condition dropout to exploit predicted simulation trajectories without relying excessively on their accuracy. Our simulation-conditioning classifier-free guidance scheme unifies these two ideas by balancing predictions based on internalized motion knowledge with those additionally guided by simulation latents. The jointly trained student generates both simulation conditions and real-domain videos, requiring no additional world model at inference. On Bridge, SimForcing achieves the best PSNR, SSIM, LPIPS, and FVD among the compared methods without external embodied pretraining. Evaluation on InternData-A1 further supports its applicability across robot datasets. Moreover, using our trained world model to initialize a vision-language-action model improves LIBERO success, suggesting its utility for downstream policy learning. https://github.com/Wang-Xiaodong1899/SimForcing|[2610.06598](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06598)|
|**2026-11-05**|**X-Fly: Learning to Reach and Stop in Full-Chunk Aerial Vision-Language Navigation**|Ruibin Ju et.al.|Aerial vision-language navigation (VLN) agents built on large vision-language models are expensive to query. Executing a complete predicted action chunk before replanning amortizes this cost, reducing model inference from 195 to 42 ms per executed waypoint for our policy. It also moves the states at which the policy is queried away from expert demonstrations, which supervise neither recovery from these states nor termination at the learner's own arrival. On OpenFly test-seen, no-stop failures account for 47.78% of full-chunk behavior cloning (BC) outcomes. We present X-Fly, a staged training framework for full-chunk aerial VLN. Recovery DAgger labels learner-visited decision states with smooth chunks that reconnect to future expert segments and with STOP at the first in-region state. With eight controls per decision, X-Fly reaches 74.18% SR on test-seen and 51.73% on test-unseen, compared with 16.45% and 18.43% for full-chunk BC and 32.04% and 29.46% for DreamFly.|[2610.21450](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21450)|
|**2026-11-05**|**RefCraft: Benchmarking Visual-Reference-Conditioned Agentic Generation through Web Development**|Yuchi Wang et.al.|Beyond basic tool use and coding skills, effectively incorporating visual references into agentic generation is equally essential for applying AI agents to real-world productivity tasks. We study this crucial capability through webpage development, a practical and readily evaluable setting in which existing benchmarks remain largely idealized: they typically focus on reconstructing visual targets or generating webpages from textual instructions alone, overlooking the more common workflow of developing from visual examples. Specifically, we introduce RefCraft, a benchmark comprising 300 realistic multimodal webpage-development tasks that combine natural-language instructions with screenshots from one to five reference webpages. We evaluate the resulting open-ended artifacts using independently verifiable keypoints and complementary programmatic and VLM judges. Across nine frontier (M)LLMs, the strongest system achieves an overall score of only 52.12 out of 100, revealing that visual-reference-conditioned agentic generation remains a substantial challenge for current models in practical webpage-development settings.|[2610.26826](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26826)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Entity-Aware Evaluation of OCR Systems for Historical Document Transcription**|Camilo Mari\~no et.al.|Optical Character Recognition (OCR) is essential for digitizing historical archives, yet standard evaluation metrics such as Character Error Rate (CER) do not necessarily reflect whether meaningful information, such as names, dates, and identifiers, is preserved. In this work, we investigate entitylevel analysis of OCR errors as a complementary perspective for evaluating OCR quality in historical documents. Our experiments show that OCR rankings depend on the evaluation criterion: while Qwen 3.6 achieves the highest entity coverage across all tested tolerances, several systems with lower exact-match coverage recover substantially when small errors are tolerated, suggesting that many of their errors correspond to localized character-level deviations rather than fundamentally incorrect entity transcriptions. Conversely, some systems remain unable to recover many entities even under relaxed matching. These findings show that OCR quality cannot be fully characterized by aggregate transcription metrics alone and highlight the importance of evaluating how OCR errors affect the preservation and recoverability of meaningful information.|[2610.33004](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33004)|
|**2026-11-05**|**PDRVLA: A Vision-Language-Action Model with Physical Dynamics Representations**|Hanqing Wang et.al.|Vision--Language--Action (VLA) models generate robot actions from visual observations and language instructions, but their understanding of physical dynamics remains limited. |**2026-11-05**|**AuroraWorld: Structured 4D World Modeling for Exo-to-Ego Video Generation in Robot Manipulation**|Hao Xue et.al.|Egocentric observations provide a robot-centric view of manipulation processes, capturing contact interactions and object-state changes that are important for visuomotor policy learning but difficult to observe from external cameras. However, existing methods struggle to synthesize physically consistent and policy-relevant egocentric videos under substantial viewpoint transformations, severe occlusions, complex object interactions, and inherently sparse observations from available exocentric viewpoints.Code will be made publicly available at https://github.com/xueha0/AuroraWorld|[2611.00177](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00177)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Learning to Live: A Step Toward Lifelong Embodied Intelligence**|Lirong Che et.al.|Embodied agents are usually organized around individual tasks, yet the physical world keeps changing between tasks and during execution. We introduce LIFE (Lifelong Intelligence from Experience), a framework in which a robot observes continuously, remembers selectively, and turns experience into new capabilities. RoboBeats, a family of lightweight streaming gates, tracks conditions set by the agent and discovers new events, deciding when an observation warrants a response or a memory write. Selected observations enter Plastic Memory, a flat store of images and structured records that keeps the source evidence, so new fields and indexes can be added when later questions need them. When existing tools fall short, Explore-Distill-Mount (EDM) turns solutions found during problem solving into validated, reusable tools and skills. Under matched protocols, RoboBeats substantially improves condition tracking over its base models and, at the same writing budget, retains more useful evidence for memory question answering than uniform sampling. A case study of limited scale, replaying 24 hours of continuous video recorded by a real robot, illustrates how the integrated system builds memory from a day of experience and uses it to answer everyday questions. LIFE takes a step toward robots that become more familiar with their surroundings and more capable through experience: learning not only to complete tasks, but to live in the world.|[2611.00180](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00180)|

|**2026-11-05**|**Who May Overrule the Agent? Evidence-Gated Authority in LLM Review Institutions**|Fuad Ali et.al.|Multi-agent language-model systems often add a reviewer to improve an upstream agent's decision. Yet a reviewer can both repair errors and replace decisions that were already correct. We study this as a problem of institutional jurisdiction: conditional on the same proposal, task information, model, and review output, when is the reviewer authorized to make its recommendation binding? We compare broad override authority with an evidence gate that permits replacement only after the reviewer produces a mechanically valid record of a binding-constraint violation and a compliant repair. Across two locally run open-weight models, 96 spatial-policy tasks, and a 96-task portfolio-transfer benchmark, the gate sharply reduces corruption of correct proposals while retaining most repairs of violations. The directions of this tradeoff are partly implied by the rule; its magnitude depends on reviewer transitions and upstream proposal states. A prospective validation then gives the reviewer an explicit choice to retain, replace, or escalate. This eliminates correct-proposal corruption for Qwen, but Mistral still replaces all 48 correct proposals; Qwen also declines every welfare-only repair that Mistral frequently attempts. Thus interface design changes reviewer behavior but is not a model-independent safeguard. The result is not that restricted review is universally better. It is that reviewer competence, action interface, and binding jurisdiction jointly determine both the correction domain and the corruption surface.|[2611.05390](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05390)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Agent Harness Awareness: Turning Verified Telemetry into Operational Control for Tool-Using Agents**|Manu Agrawal et.al.|Tool descriptions tell an agent what an action can do, but they do not necessarily reveal whether that action is healthy or suitable at the moment of use. Execution harnesses often possess this information through monitors, traces, and recent failures while the model choosing the next action does not. We call this gap operational blindness. We introduce Agent Harness Awareness, a trust-separated design in which the host supplies a small, fresh, verified state snapshot before a consequential action while retaining measurement, enforcement, evaluation, and final dispatch authority.
