Omni-Encoder achieves competitive results on 16 benchmarks across all four modalities with one shared parameter set, and joint training improves each modality over its separately trained counterpart. Code and models will be publicly released.|[2611.05406](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05406)|
|**2026-11-05**|**PEEK: One-Step-Look-Ahead Exposure Bias Correction for Diffusion Sampling**|Yunwei Bai et.al.|Diffusion image generators are successful due to accurate denoising networks and strong numerical samplers. Yet the two components are not fully matched. During sampling, the denoiser predicts for the next step based on flawed input that differ much from the real data. This train-test gap, or exposure bias, caps image generation quality (i.e., blurry, lacking in details or awkward in shape). To mitigate the problem, we propose PEEK, a novel training-free deterministic sampler plugin. At each eligible denoising step, PEEK first performs a provisional one-step update, evaluates the denoiser once at the look-ahead state, and interpolates it with the one-step-later prediction to form the next noisy state. By taking the additional step, we obtain a generally more-image-like prediction from the higher-signal noisy state, mitigating the exposure bias. To optimize the interpolation positions, PEEK searches for an interpolation schedule search made efficient by the prefix and baseline noise-schedule-rebasing caches. On 8 pixel-space and latent-space diffusion models with unconditional, label-guided, and text-guided settings across various output resolutions, PEEK consistently improves over NFE-matched baselines, with FID improvements up to 49.3\% at minimal extra computation cost (e.g., the one-time-per-model calibration takes at most 2.2 minutes across our experiments.). Project website is at https://dachengruoque.github.io/PEEK-Video/.|[2611.05407](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05407)|
|**2026-11-05**|**CAST-Bench: Tracing Every Sound to Its Source in Multi-Shot Audio-Video Generation**|Yucong Liu et.al.|Existing benchmarks for joint text-to-audio-video (T2AV) generation evaluate perceptual quality and audiovisual alignment, but balanced sound-source sam?pling and separate reporting by shot structure remain limited. This makes it dif?ficult to determine whether a specific sound is missing, attributed to the wrong source, or lost across shot transitions. To address this, we introduce CAST?BENCH, a benchmark designed to trace every generated sound back to its in?tended source in multi-shot audio-video generation. The core idea is to formu?late T2AV correctness as an audiovisual contract in which each requested sound must be emitted by the right source, synchronized with the corresponding visual action, and carry the right semantic content. Concretely, the benchmark orga?nizes 900 prompts into 9 sound-source categories, with 100 prompts per cate?gory, spanning speech, animals, instruments, ambient sounds, and other emitter types. The prompts include 439 single-shot and 461 multi-shot narratives, each derived from a structured specification that links every audio event to its emit?ter, subject, and temporal slot. The benchmark provides an 18-metric evaluation suite covering three dimensions: Visual Performance, Content Consistency, and Audio & Semantic Alignment. We further benchmark 12 representative gen?erators and find that visual quality is nearly saturated across leading systems, yet audio semantic scores still differ markedly. Across the nine shared mod?els, multi-shot prompts receive lower aggregate scores under the subset-specific scoring rules and yield different model rankings. Multi-person speech, con?sistent speaker identity across shots, and cross-shot event retention remain the main unresolved challenges. All prompts and evaluation code are available at https://github.com/yucong-liu/CAST-Bench-public.|[2611.05422](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05422)|
|**2026-11-05**|**LLM-based NPCs in Video Games: A Systematic Mapping Study**|Dilan Mian et.al.|For decades, non-player characters (NPCs) have been driven by rule-based systems whose personalities and interactions are scripted before release. Large language models (LLMs) promise NPCs that converse openly, adapt to new situations, and behave in ways developers did not fully author, prompting a wave of research and commercial experimentation. Yet the field lacks a unified picture of what has been built, how well it works, and at what cost. We address this gap with a systematic mapping study of 377 papers from the academic literature, yielding 42 papers analysed against a main research question and ten sub-questions across four thematic axes. We provide the first quantitative characterisation of component adoption, latency, safety coverage, and reproducibility: short-term memory and world-state grounding are near-universal (60% and 69%), whereas long-term memory (31%), reflection (38%), and retrieval-augmented generation (14%) remain rare, and response times span 2.6 to 15 seconds. Finally, we identify six structural barriers to commercial adoption and offer evidence-based recommendations for developers and researchers.|[2611.05427](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05427)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Constraint-Preserving Flow Matching for Spectral Graph Generation**|Anita Yang et.al.|Spectral graph generation models graphs through the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of their Laplacians, providing a compact and continuous alternative to direct graph construction. However, these eigensystems must retain the structural properties of graph Laplacians. Existing approaches preserve eigenvector orthonormality but do not enforce the Laplacian nullspace constraint, potentially producing eigensystems inconsistent with Laplacian structure. To address this, we introduce Laplacian-Constrained Stiefel Flow Matching (LaSFM), which preserves the nullspace condition alongside eigenvector orthonormality and positive eigenvalues during generation. We characterize the underlying constrained Stiefel manifold and formulate flow matching on it. We further extend LaSFM to property-guided generation, steering samples toward desired graph properties while maintaining these constraints. Experiments demonstrate improved performance on both spectral and graph-level metrics, along with better alignment with target properties.|[2611.05436](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05436)|
|**2026-11-05**|**SurgTransfer: Multi-Step Latent Action Modeling for Action Transfer in Surgical World Model**|Saurabh Koju et.al.|Surgical world models have the potential to provide intra-operative guidance, accelerate risk-free training and facilitate transfer of expert surgical maneuvers. However, the use of world models for transferring surgical expertise has been largely unexplored. Moreover, recent approaches in this domain have mainly relied on ground truth kinematics actions or expert annotations for specific tasks, limiting their applicability beyond the narrow range of tasks with ground-truth annotations. In contrast, existing works in the natural domain have shown promising results in learning latent actions solely from unlabeled videos and transferring the learned actions across contexts. We observe that this paradigm is short-sighted for the surgical domain, failing to capture the hierarchical and goal-directed structure of surgical procedures. Thus, we propose a novel multi-step action learning framework to learn context-invariant latent actions that are easily transferable across various surgical contexts. We perform extensive experiments on the publicly available datasets, SurgToolLoc-2022 and PitVis-2023, verifying our approach with both quantitative metrics and qualitative evaluation by surgical experts. We have published our code and the implementation details at https://github.com/bhattarailab/Surgical-Vision-World-Model.|[2611.05443](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05443)|
|**2026-11-05**|**LIBRA: Programmatic Induction of Executable Tool Libraries**|Zekai Li et.al.|Large language model agents increasingly retrieve tools from large catalogs, yet most retrievers rank each tool independently by request similarity. For multi tool tasks, this recovers the goal matching tool while omitting prerequisite tools required by the workflow.We introduce LIBRA, a framework for Library Induction by Runtime Assembly that turns a flat tool catalog into an executable tool library. An LLM proposes per tool contracts of candidate invocations, reusable outputs and state changes, and success conditions; LIBRA then tests these compositions in a sandbox and retains the relations supported at runtime. Constructed before user queries, the library requires no dependency annotations, tool use trajectories, or task specific training, and supports both one-shot and agentic retrieval. On AppWorld, BFCL, and SciToolEval, LIBRA improves one-shot Recall and NDCG over the same dense retriever by 7.9 and 3.7 percentage points on average. Within ToolReAGt, it yields corresponding gains of 4.5 and 2.9 points after ten retrieval iterations.|[2611.05445](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05445)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Jev with Foresight: Safe Language-Guided Autonomous Driving via a Language World Model**|Yansong Qu et.al.|Large language models understand passengers' driving commands, but as driving agents they reason in generated text for seconds and must infer each action's consequences implicitly from the scene. We present Jev-AD, which pairs Jev, a typed decision model that answers predefined questions with probabilities, not generated text, with a language world model that predicts for every candidate action how nearby vehicles move over the next two seconds. Code turns these predictions into foreseen consequences, which Jev compares instead of inferring them from coordinates. In simulated highway merging (500 car-episodes), this foresight lowers the same decision model's collision rate from 26.0% to 2.4% at 0.25 seconds per decision, and Jev-AD achieves the passenger's intent in 93% of free-form command trials. In 500 replayed real-world NGSIM episodes, foresight lowers the collision rate from 48.2% to 2.8%, and the world model, learned from this traffic, foresees 93% of dangerous actions. Project page: https://ys-qu.github.io/jev-ad-website.|[2611.05448](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05448)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Hybrid LLM-Deterministic Pipelines for Enterprise-Scale Code Migration at Bloomberg**|Mario Corchero et.al.|Large organizations carry a long tail of infrastructure migrations that are worth doing but rarely urgent enough to compete with feature work, because each one costs significant engineering time per repository. This paper reports on our industrial experience from Automated Uplift, a multi-month initiative applying LLM-assisted migration pipelines across thousands of Bloomberg repositories. We examine two distinct production campaigns: a heterogeneous Python packaging migration (approx. 1,900 candidate repositories) and a uniform C++ build system migration (approx. 6,700 candidate repositories). Together, these campaigns generated 2,333 pull requests, resulting in 729 successful migrations. We detail our hybrid architectural solution which maximizes deterministic processing, confines the LLM to bounded file transformations within a pre-computed context, and incorporates runtime production telemetry (such as execution logs and usage traces). Our findings show that generating migrations turned out to be the easier part; the bottleneck was the cost, for owning teams, of reviewing, testing, and deploying them. These costs were lowered when changes could be verified directly in the pipeline before opening a PR (as with C++ compilation and test validation), and when the population was uniform enough for fixes to carry over from one repository to the next. We conclude by distilling our experience and findings into actionable, transferable patterns for architecting hybrid LLM-deterministic pipelines and scaling code migrations across the enterprise|[2611.05457](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05457)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Objective-Conditioned Active Preference Learning for Multi-Objective Alignment**|Duc Anh Vu et.al.|Aligning large language models (LLMs) with several objectives, such as helpfulness, harmlessness, and honesty, typically relies on preference judgment for each objective, growing the annotation cost linearly with the number of objectives. Active preference learning can reduce this cost by selecting the comparisons whose labels are expected to change the policy most. Existing remedies either lack an objective-aware query selection mechanism for the multi-objective setting or regard conflicting preferences as harmful, therefore excluding them from the training data. We introduce OGC-DPO (Objective-conditioned Gradient Coverage DPO), an active learning framework that treats each (response pair, objective) combination as a separate query and measures its informativeness through the policy's own implicit gradient, without relying on an external reward model. OGC-DPO then selects the queries that most expand each objective's gradient coverage. Empirical experiments across varying numbers of objectives demonstrate the effectiveness of OGC-DPO over existing active preference learning methods under the same annotation cost. Further analysis demonstrates that informative conflicts, rather than being uniformly harmful, provide a useful policy training signal.|[2611.05506](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05506)|
|**2026-11-05**|**TSFM Meets LLM: Context as Covariates**|Hemanth Ram et.al.|Textual information is ubiquitous in real-world time series datasets, appearing as news, reports, and annotations that hold key predictive signals not deducible from numerical history alone. Large Language Models (LLMs) interpret text but lack numerical precision, while Time Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) are quantitatively accurate but cannot reason about the effects of text. Moreover, the lack of large-scale paired multimodal datasets limits progress in bridging this gap. In this work, we address this challenge by providing a scalable approach for generating realistic multimodal datasets by annotating real-world time series with LLMs. Building on this, we propose Context as Covariates (CoCo), a forecasting framework in which an LLM distills its textual reasoning into numerically grounded forecast and confidence covariates that guide a TSFM backbone, thereby exploiting the strengths of both LLMs and TSFMs. We align the two models in stages, first fine-tuning the LLM via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with a reward tied to covariate-induced improvement in forecasting performance, and then fine-tuning the TSFM on covariates generated by the aligned LLM. Our experiments show that models trained on our synthetic multimodal corpus generalize to real benchmarks spanning finance, economics, weather, traffic, and security. Notably, CoCo with Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507 as the LLM and Chronos2 as the TSFM achieves up to 12% MAE improvement over state-of-the-art TSFMs and LLM forecasters, such as GPT-4o. Code, checkpoints, and the evaluation datasets are publicly available.|[2611.05515](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05515)|
|**2026-11-05**|**When to Ask, When to Answer: Strategic Clarification for Ambiguous Reference Resolution in Dialogue**|Ibrahim Khebour et.al.|For humans, referential disambiguation is a collaborative process involving clarification questions asked when they will meaningfully change the outcome. However, large language models (LLMs) have been shown to be biased toward premature answering, preferring definite single-turn responses over more clarification exchanges. In this paper, we study the problem of "strategic clarification" -- asking clarification questions in a reference game when their answers will narrow the set of candidate referents. We construct a controlled test environment from the SIMMC 2.1 dataset that records how many questions each ambiguous reference needs to be successfully resolved, and examine how baseline LLM behavior and common post-training methods ask clarifying questions vs. attempt to answer the referent. We also propose BACE (Belief-Aware Critic with Epistemic tracking), a lightweight action chooser method on top of a frozen LM backbone, and show that BACE more consistently chooses the optimal number of clarifying questions than methods such as GRPO and ArCHer.|[2611.05517](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05517)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Decision Models as Scalable Economic Surrogates that Capture Human Variation**|Maxime Saxena et.al.|Decision Models (DMs), a new class of AI models, give fast responses and return a probability distribution over a fixed set of options. These properties make them candidate human surrogates in economic simulations and surveys, a role increasingly filled by Large Language Models (LLMs), which are costly at scale and under-represent the variation in human behaviour. We present a first evaluation of DMs as human surrogates in economics, comparing 10 DMs with 8 LLMs in three economic settings: a household spending survey on consumption, several classic economic games and a labour-market field experiment. Frontier DMs, those that score highest on general decision-making benchmarks, reproduce the variation in human responses more closely than LLMs in all three settings. They capture the modal human response as well as LLMs in the spending survey, but less well in the economic games. Frontier DMs are also roughly ten times faster than the fastest LLM and can be over a hundred times cheaper. Together, these results suggest that DMs capture the heterogeneity of human behaviour better than LLMs and make large-scale economic simulations more feasible.|[2611.05553](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05553)|
|**2026-11-05**|**How Finely Can Vision-Language Models See Biology? Bio-VQA: A Benchmark for Taxonomic, Morphological, Spatial and Individual Reasoning**|S M Rayeed et.al.|In biodiversity monitoring, a single image raises several questions: which taxon, how many, which trait, and whether the individual has been seen before. Vision-language models (VLMs) could answer all of these from the image, but their ability is largely untested in biology, as existing benchmarks focus mostly on species identification within a few taxonomic groups. We present Bio-VQA, the largest biological visual question answering (VQA) benchmark and the only one to span four aspects of biological visual understanding: hierarchical taxonomic reasoning, fine-grained attribute grounding, quantitative spatial reasoning, and individual re-identification. Bio-VQA contains 1.86M questions over 217K images from 11 public datasets, and extends right-or-wrong scoring to graded scoring, so a near-miss counts for more than a distant one. Evaluating 18 open-weight models from 3.75B to 38.4B parameters, we find that general visual capabilities do not transfer to biological organisms: the best model counts animals exactly on 41.7% of images with 2 to 10 individuals, less than half its accuracy on everyday objects at the same counts. Taxonomic accuracy falls to one sixth, from 0.72 at phylum to 0.12 at scientific name, and scaling helps mostly at coarse ranks. The gap to a domain specialist widens accordingly, from 0.731 against BioCLIP-2's 0.874 at class (1.2x) to 0.269 against 0.544 at scientific name (2x). On trait size measurement, 15 of the 18 models do no better than an image-blind baseline that predicts one fixed number for every specimen, and on re-identification, 17 of the 18 models fall below chance under adversarial conditions. These failures persist across a tenfold range of model size. We hope Bio-VQA becomes a standard test of how well VLM capabilities transfer to the biological domain.|[2611.05559](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05559)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Discarded or Unreachable? Diagnosing and Repairing Spatial Relation Failure in Vision--Language Similarity**|Mahdi Mostajabdaveh et.al.|Contrastive vision-language models score left/right relations near chance, but the symptom has two causes that call for opposite repairs: the relation may survive in the pooled embedding yet be unreachable by native image-text similarity, or pooling may discard it while the dense features retain it. On clean two object photographs, across ten encoders, the lateral polarity is linearly decodable from the flip odd component of the pooled vector at 0.77-0.99, yet a search over a million captions leaves nine of the ten at chance under native scoring: an access failure. On controlled C4 orbits, where the relation is the only thing that varies, pooling keeps the arrangement axis at 0.998-1.000 and loses its polarity, which the patch grid retains: a pooling failure. Both sit downstream of the dense features, so one readout covers both. Our zero parameter, query conditioned spatial moment recomputes the geometry from the patch grid and stores it as a vector beside the native embedding. On a 118,287 image index it raises left/right pairwise accuracy against reversed geometry negatives from 0.51-0.53 to 0.81-0.96 and conditional R@1 among 21 category matched candidates from 0.05 to 0.37-0.75, at under a millisecond per batched query, while reproducing the native ranking exactly for queries that name no relation.|[2611.05565](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05565)|
|**2026-11-05**|**MESA: An Evaluation Framework for Compositional, Semantic, and Spatial Generalization in Robotics**|Albert Wilcox et.al.|Despite growing interest in foundation models for robotic manipulation, systematic evaluation of their capabilities remains elusive. Existing benchmarks are either narrow and saturated, offering little signal on SOTA methods, broadly scoped, providing diversity at the cost of controlled experimentation, or require training on large, messy open-source datasets. We introduce MESA, an evaluation framework designed for precise, reproducible measurement of language-conditioned policy generalization. MESA provides five evaluation suites that isolate in-distribution performance and four distinct axes of generalization: spatial configuration, object instance, object category, and subtask composition, enabling researchers to diagnose where and why policies fail, not just whether they fail. To populate these suites at scale, we develop MESA-Gen, a pipeline for task and demonstration generation built by improving MimicGen. We instantiate the framework on two platforms and release both: the single-arm MESA suite, and BiMESA, the first large-scale benchmark for bimanual generalization. We evaluate seven baseline methods spanning diffusion policies, VLM-based approaches, and pretrained VLAs, finding that the strongest model achieves only 52% on MESA and 49% on BiMESA, providing meaningful signal while leaving ample room for progress. MESA rankings are broadly consistent with RoboArena scores, suggesting that MESA captures method-level differences relevant beyond simulation. Project website: https://pairlab.github.io/MESA.|[2611.05567](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05567)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Just the Facts: How Dialogues with AI Reduce Conspiracy Beliefs**|Thomas H. Costello et.al.|Personalized dialogues with AI substantially reduce conspiracy beliefs. To identify why, we conducted two experiments with U.S. adults recruited online (total N = 2,193) that removed possible causes one at a time. The debunking effect was robust to disclosing the AI's persuasive goal, framing the exchange as a debate, instructing the AI to inform rather than persuade, halving its response length, altering how beliefs were measured, and replacing the dialogue with a single tailored message. Only instructing the AI to persuade without factual evidence and arguments substantially reduced the debunking effect. Content analyses of the AI's output and participants' own accounts linked larger reductions in conspiracy beliefs to factual argumentation rather than rapport, storytelling, or appeals to empathy. AI dialogues appear to reduce conspiracy beliefs primarily by supplying relevant, well-reasoned counterevidence. Our results suggest that humans might be similarly effective if they could supply comparable evidence.|[2611.05572](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05572)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Readable Is Not Steerable: Pre-Registered Tests of Physical Structure in Learned Fluid Representations**|Manvendra Modgil et.al.|Self-supervised world models are often said to organize their latents around physical quantities. We test this on 2D decaying Navier-Stokes flow in three pre-registered studies, each tested once on held-out data. In Study 1, a joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA) trained to predict the flow fails the pre-registered criteria for organizing its latent around fluid quantities better than an untrained network of the same architecture (not supported); a JEPA trained on non-physical linear dynamics and a static invariance model that never sees time do as well or better. In Study 2, plain autoencoders that render the field make enstrophy prominent in their slowest unsupervised latent directions (best r 0.80-0.86 vs 0.38 untrained; fresh seeds, sealed data). Adding VICReg lowers this prominence in every comparison, though not by the pre-registered margin for vorticity, while raising the autoencoders' linear decodability (partially supported). In Study 3, we build steerable directions ("dials") by routing: the decoder gets overall scale only through a small conditioning code, while its other pathway carries a degraded copy of spectral shape. Label-free rules find a loudness dial in all 8 trained models and a shape dial in 3 of 5 fresh seeds on sealed data, against a pre-registered bar of 4; neither control shows a shape dial (partially supported). Across the studies, what a latent contains and what can be controlled through it come apart. In exploration, a supervised direction reading spectral shape almost perfectly moved nothing when steered. During the build, the loudness dial had to be found by its effect on the output (closest to a pure rescaling), not by data variance; for shape, a variance rule and an effect-constrained rule each worked in 3 of 5 seeds. The program used about 70 logged hours on one consumer GPU; all pre-registrations, deviations, code and outputs are public.|[2611.05577](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05577)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Multimodal Fusion Methods for Whole-Slide Images and Clinical Data in Inflammatory Bowel Disease**|Ankana Banerjee (University of Stavanger et.al.|Multimodal AI fusion of histology and clinical data is an active research area in computational pathology, but fusion methods are rarely compared under identical conditions and across different clinical endpoints. We present a modular framework in which slide-level embeddings, obtained by training an attention-based aggregator over patch embeddings from a frozen foundation model, and per-visit clinical embeddings are fed into 15 interchangeable fusion methods with a shared output dimension and prediction head (except decision-level methods). Using a prospective cohort of newly diagnosed inflammatory bowel disease patients, we predicted STRIDE-II-aligned remission outcomes from baseline or follow-up visits. The best fusion method reached macro-F1 of 0.57-0.75, outperforming both single-modality models in four of six scenarios, and differed by outcome and input. Image reliance, the drop in balanced accuracy when the slide embedding is removed, tended to follow the remission type - highest for histological and lowest for endoscopic remission; methods with similar accuracy relied on the slide to very different degrees.|[2611.05579](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05579)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Whiel: A Neurosymbolic Platform for Certified Relational Program Verification**|Jesse Comer et.al.|We present the Whiel (a contraction of Whi(le-R)el) system for Lean-certified reasoning about imperative relational programs. The system can encode containment and equivalence problems for Datalog, its extensions with negation, and the core fragment of graph query languages. It can also represent optimized or incremental evaluation algorithms that are not expressible in the original query language, as well as first-order specifications of integrity constraints, schema mappings, and views arising in data integration, exchange, and sharing. Whiel's program states are database instances, and its imperative structure enables compositional Hoare-style reasoning with first-order relational specifications, using prophecy variables and leveled invariants to support inductive arguments. Whiel supports neurosymbolic discovery of loop invariants whose verification conditions can be checked using the Vampire first-order theorem prover. Counterexamples are fed back into the neurosymbolic pipeline, while proposed solutions are translated into Lean proof certificates. To validate the Whiel system, we evaluate its performance on a curated benchmark suite. In summary, we envision the Whiel system as a tool for tackling mathematically demanding, typically undecidable problems involving complex query and constraint languages, with a high degree of trust in its output.|[2611.05612](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05612)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Spatio-Temporal Multi-Order Interaction for Video Recognition**|Kevin D. Nguetche et.al.|Many temporal approaches for video recognition rely on explicit cross-channel aggregation projections, which have quadratic complexity in the number of channels, introducing significant computational overhead. In addition, the spatio-temporal representation used for video recognition is biased either toward local order interactions (for CNNs) or toward global order interactions (for transformers). Starting from the multi-order spatial representation of MogaNet, our method introduces two complementary contributions to tackle these problems. First, for the temporal modelling, we introduce parallel multi-order processing with depthwise-only convolutions and implicit gating-based aggregation, which is linear in the number of channels, inducing a considerable computational reduction. Second, we propose a dual interaction between spatial and temporal dimensions, which allows a better spatio-temporal representation without increasing the computational cost. By combining these two contributions, our proposed VideoMogaNet achieves performance on par with or better than state-of-the-art action recognition approaches across multiple video benchmarks (UCF101, HMDB51, Kinetics-400, Diving48), while considerably reducing the cost of the temporal branch.|[2611.05633](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05633)|
|**2026-11-05**|**VIPBench: A Human-Aligned Benchmark for Voice Identity Perception in the Age of Voice Cloning**|Xudong Tang et.al.|Speaker-recognition benchmarks test how accurately a model recovers the speaker label of a recording, treating voice identity as a property of the person who produced it. However, these benchmarks do not evaluate how humans perceive voice identity. Whether a listener hears two voices as the same person has consequences for intellectual property rights, cybersecurity, and everyday life. We introduce the Voice Identity Perception benchmark (VIPBench), which contains 124,876 same-or-different judgments from 1,290 listeners on 9,800 recording pairs from 100 public figures. The pairs include real recordings, voice clones generated by a text-to-speech system, and morphs between two speakers' voices. We also define four tasks that evaluate the alignment between speaker-representation models and human identity perception. The first two tasks measure how well a model's similarity score predicts the proportion of listeners who respond "same" and the majority response. The other two measure whether a model and listeners find the same speaker pairs similar, and whether a model's relationship to listener judgments holds equally for real and synthetic recordings. We evaluate eleven publicly available speech models. Listener judgments diverge from speaker labels most often for voice clones: in 41% of responses, listeners judged a clone and a real recording of the same speaker to be different people. No model reaches the noise ceiling; the best model explains 61% of the explainable variance. Models trained with speaker labels align with listeners better than self-supervised models do, yet a model with a 12% error rate on speaker labels aligns with listeners as well as models with error rates below 2%. VIPBench enables the evaluation of speaker models against human perception on real, cloned, and morphed speech, and our results motivate training speaker models directly on listener judgments.|[2611.05638](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05638)|
|**2026-11-05**|**TACT: A Pragmatic Approach to Qualitative Analysis with an AI Council**|Rudrajit Choudhuri et.al.|Qualitative data can tell software teams what their stakeholders need, yet rigorously analyzing thousands of qualitative responses takes expert time that few teams can afford or justify. Teams therefore often face a false choice: leave the responses unanalyzed, or hand them to a large language model and accept its interpretation and judgment. In this paper, we take a pragmatic approach to AI-assisted qualitative analysis and present TACT (Traceable AI Council for Thematic analysis), a procedure that enables such analysis at scale while keeping teams in control of interpretation and judgment. TACT comes in two variants: TACT-Lite, the core AI council procedure, and TACT-Full, which adds adversaries (models from other model families that critique each model's output) and a decision model that surfaces high-uncertainty code assignments. We evaluated TACT on an independent dataset of 256 Stack Overflow threads qualitatively coded by other researchers. Against their labels, TACT's candidate codebook achieved an F1 of 0.75-0.77 and its final coding an F1 of 0.71-0.72. On a held-out sample, agreement between our own manual coding and TACT's coding was Cohen's $\kappa$ of 0.79-0.85. TACT is also economical: model calls cost \$13.77 (TACT-Lite) to \$33.59 (TACT-Full) per 100 threads. Finally, drawing on our experience building and validating TACT, we identify pitfalls in AI-assisted qualitative analysis, describe safeguards to address them, and offer guidance for teams and researchers applying TACT in practice.|[2611.05702](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05702)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Do Language Models Know Which Constraints Belong Together?**|Junbo Jacob Lian et.al.|Dantzig-Wolfe reformulation strengthens the linear relaxation of a mixed-integer program by convexifying a chosen set of constraint families, and every candidate costs a column generation run. The choice is hard when families strengthen the bound only together, because a search that adds one family at a time then stops at the linear relaxation. The classical inequalities of lot sizing, network design and facility location rest on such zero-marginal groups. We prove that when each evaluation convexifies at most $s$ of $k$ families, any method that observes only bounds needs a covering number of evaluations, of order $k^w$, to find a hidden group of $w$ families, also on actual Dantzig-Wolfe relaxations. A prediction that misses $e$ members reduces the cost to $\Theta(k^e)$, through one staged search that does not need to know $e$, so the evaluations a prediction saves measure what the predictor knows. We study two sources of predictions. A one-pass lookahead at an optimal LP dual is provably blind to groups whose families share no variable, and a language model reads the names and text of the model. JointDW verifies the prediction of the language model with the staged search and interleaves it with the lookahead at a bounded loss. Groups that strengthen the bound only jointly appear in a quarter of the MIPLIB-NL models whose bound can be improved. There the strongest language models identify them about as often as the lookahead, their predictions rest on the names for textbook models, and reasoning about doubles the accuracy of the same model. On larger models with distracting families, the lookahead finds the group in one evaluation of three families. Language models given only the text seldom name it, while one reasoning model given every coefficient names it in all but one answer and then needs fewer evaluations than the lookahead for pairs of families.|[2611.05726](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05726)|
|**2026-11-05**|**OdorSpaces: Visual-Olfactory Embodied Navigation in 3D Environments**|Changyeon Lee et.al.|Olfaction provides critical information about airborne chemical cues, yet it remains largely underexplored in embodied AI, with limited prior machine olfaction work focusing primarily on semantic odor perception. We introduce OdorSpaces, a physics-based simulation platform and benchmark for visual-olfactory embodied navigation. OdorSpaces augments Habitat-Sim with 3D odor transport and dispersion simulated by Fire Dynamics Simulator (FDS), enabling agents to navigate photorealistic indoor environments using both visual and olfactory observations. The benchmark includes two representative source-seeking scenarios: invisible gas-leak localization and early-stage fire localization. We develop an initial multimodal reinforcement learning policy that integrates visual observations and olfactory measurements over time. Experiments show that the policy outperforms a diverse set of baselines, establishing a strong reference for future research on visual-olfactory embodied navigation.|[2611.05738](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05738)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Learning Hierarchical Patch Splitting Policies for Faster Vision Transformers**|Aditya Gupta et.al.|Vision Transformers (ViTs) are bottlenecked by the length of their input token sequence. Standard patchification fixes this length with a single patch size applied across the image, regardless of regional content. Prior work on adaptive patch sizing reduces token count by assigning coarser patches to visually simple regions, but relies on noisy pixel-level heuristics such as local entropy to make patchification decisions. We argue that this compute allocation should be grounded in the model's interpretation of task relevant information, not in raw pixel statistics. We introduce Split Policy Learned Image Tokenization which learns to allocate token resolution based on what the model finds meaningful, producing shorter token sequences that are better matched to task-relevant content. SPLIT matches the accuracy of full-resolution ViT classification baselines while using 30% fewer tokens. The same gains hold on dense prediction: SPLIT achieves competitive accuracy on COCO and ADE20K, demonstrating that semantically aligned patchification generalises well beyond classification.|[2611.05742](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05742)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Text-Only Domain Adaptation for Low-Resource Medical ASR: Shallow Fusion and Density Ratio Rescoring for Hausa**|Oche David Ankeli et.al.|Automatic speech recognition (ASR) systems often struggle with specialised medical vocabulary, especially in low-resource languages where domain-specific speech data are scarce. We investigate text-only domain adaptation for Hausa medical ASR using the fixed MMS-1B-FL102 acoustic model and two decoding-time language-model fusion methods: Shallow Fusion (SF) and the Density Ratio Approach (DRA). A 4-gram KenLM trained on 19,421 Hausa health sentences serves as the medical target model, while a 75,372-sentence general-domain model provides the source prior for DRA. Evaluation uses 250 recordings of 25 physician-reviewed prompts from 10 speakers across six northern Nigerian states. Under prompt-disjoint five-fold cross-validation, SF and DRA reduce WER from 29.56% to 23.55% and 23.33%, respectively. Both significantly outperform greedy decoding (p < 0.001), while their difference is not significant (p = 0.423). However, general-LM-only SF achieves 23.96% WER, indicating that the medical LM's additional improvement over a general LM is modest. Medical Term Error Rate also improves under fusion, but does not establish a distinct medical-domain advantage. These findings demonstrate that lightweight, text-only language-model fusion can improve Hausa medical ASR without additional domain-specific speech collection.|[2611.05744](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05744)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Pairing Expert Critique with LLM-Driven Refinement: An Iterative Approach for Curating Quality AI Evaluation Datasets**|Honson Ling et.al.|Evaluating and improving domain-specific LLM responses requires high-quality reference data and rubrics, yet expert authoring is slow and expensive, and synthetic datasets may fail to capture practitioner preferences unless they are grounded in expert judgment. We present SME-C (Subject Matter Expert Critique), a human-AI workflow that captures expertise through lightweight critiques of candidate responses and complements it with LLM-driven analysis and refinement to iteratively curate response datasets and evaluation rubrics. We validate SME-C in the domain of financial business briefs, collecting 3,853 critiques from 411 finance professionals on 520 queries across two survey rounds. We evaluate two LLM-driven refinement strategies: one guided by the experts' written critiques and one that infers critique solely from their preference votes. In a blind validation survey with 100 additional professionals, we found that both strategies significantly improved quality over the experts' top-ranked baseline responses. The gains were concentrated among weaker original responses, and the two strategies excelled in different ways: critique-based refinement produced more domain-grounded improvements, whereas vote-based refinement was more effective at identifying and removing irrelevant content. The resulting rubrics and refined dataset support reproducible offline evaluation and benchmarking of LLMs, suggesting that structured expert critique is a viable alternative to expert authoring for building high-quality, domain-specific evaluation resources more quickly, at scale, and at lower cost.|[2611.05749](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05749)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Scheduling Data to Compute: Hierarchical Data Pipelining for LLM Training on AMD GPUs**|Gengyu Rao et.al.|Large language models (LLM) have been the key focus of recent machine learning breakthroughs. As the model size grows exponentially, the computation needed for training is astronomical. A GPU can do a huge amount of arithmetic. However, the data its compute units need is not always available when they are ready. This data may still be in slow memory or on another GPU. While the chip waits, expensive compute sits idle, and at scale even a small drop in utilization translates into substantial cost. In this paper, we treat this waiting time as the primary bottleneck. We focus on hardware features designed for explicit data movement. These features load data from off-chip memory onto the chip, rearrange it into the layout the computation requires as it is loaded, and transfer it between GPUs. By orchestrating them, we keep a steady pipeline of data flowing to the compute units so that they remain busy. We present kernel and framework implementations that address this bottleneck using three key data-movement instructions and hardware features. The Tensor Data Mover (TDM) moves tensor data from high-bandwidth memory (HBM) into on-chip memory. Transpose-load instructions transpose tensor data into the layout that LLM computations require as it is loaded on-chip. Finally, System DMA (SDMA) and Remote DMA (RDMA) move data between GPUs over the scale-up and scale-out networks, respectively. Our results show that TDM and transpose-load provide on average 1.88x and 1.19x speedup at the kernel level, respectively. The SDMA and RDMA lead to on average 1.16x and 1.33x speedup for computation overlapped with communication. This is the first AMD LLM training system to utilize all the GPU data pipelining features. Our implementation has been integrated into open source framework and library for AMD GPUs. https://github.com/AMD-AGI/Primus-Turbo https://github.com/AMD-AGI/Primus|[2611.05751](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05751)|
|**2026-11-05**|**BobFlow: Towards Human-Agent Collaborative Data Science Using Dataflows**|Jiadong Bai et.al.|Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have established human-agent collaboration as a promising paradigm for data science. Effective collaboration requires a shared computational abstraction that enables agents to reason about processing logic while allowing human analysts to understand, guide, and refine the analysis. Although scripts written in Python or SQL provide precise control, their syntax and execution details can be difficult for analysts to understand and modify. In this paper, we argue that dataflows are an effective abstraction for human-agent collaboration in data science. We introduce BobFlow, a system that combines the dataflow abstraction with the ReAct paradigm. It enables agents to iteratively construct and execute dataflows for data science tasks while allowing analysts to inspect the analysis and provide feedback. Our user study shows that BobFlow helps analysts understand, inspect, and verify agent-generated analyses. A thorough experimental evaluation across proprietary and open-weight LLMs shows accuracy score gains of up to 18.6 points at just over 1/3 of a script-based ReAct agent's inference cost.|[2611.05761](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05761)|
|**2026-11-05**|**How Well Can Large Language Models Impersonate Individual Authors? A Comparative Evaluation**|Harshit Gupta et.al.|This study investigates the authorship impersonation capacity of six widely used large language models (LLMs): Gemini 3 Pro, Claude Opus, GPT 5.2, GPT 5.2 Pro, DeepSeek 3.2, and Grok 4.1 Fast. The evaluation was conducted using short product review texts selected to ensure high within author consistency in individual writing style. Two types of in context prompt were designed: a simple prompt with minimal instruction and a complex prompt incorporating explicit stylometric guidance for mimicry. Authorship mimicry was evaluated using a cosine distance based distance to training metric, supplemented by visual analyses of embedding distributions. The results indicate that authorship impersonation remained challenging for most LLMs, with Gemini 3 Pro showing the strongest apparent mimicry performance. However, subsequent overlap analysis revealed that Gemini 3 Pro extensively copied and reused sentences and phrases from the training texts, at levels far exceeding those observed among human authors. Follow up factor analyses showed that training set consistency was the most robust predictor of mimicry success across models, whereas author distinctiveness did not generalise as a reliable predictor. Global embedding visualisations further revealed a consistent macro level pattern: for most models, generated texts occupied regions of the embedding space that were largely separated from the corresponding human training clusters. This indicates limited convergence towards target author style and suggests the presence of model specific lexical and phraseological preferences. The findings highlight the importance of distinguishing genuine stylistic imitation from direct textual reuse and have implications for the forensic analysis of textual evidence involving potential LLM enabled impersonation.|[2611.05771](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05771)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Unsupervised Neural Operators for Rotating Relativistic Stars: High-Fidelity Equation of State Inference and Pulsar Spin Evolution at Scale**|Anarya Ray et.al.|Observed populations of rotating neutron stars~(NSs) with millisecond-order periods are powerful probes of fundamental physics. Mass-radius measurements inform poorly constrained properties of degenerate nuclear matter, whereas high-fidelity population synthesis connects unknowns in NS formation and binary evolution to large radio-timing surveys. However, repeated structure calculations are prohibitive due to the computational cost of solving the highly coupled partial differential equations of axisymmetric general relativistic hydrostatic equilibrium that govern rotating NS interiors. Non-rotating calculations or approximate rotational corrections can bias equation of state inference and limit the fidelity of population synthesis in tracing the evolutionary history of progenitors and propagating equation of state uncertainties. In this \textit{Letter}, we develop physics-informed neural operators that can solve the axisymmetric problem with high accuracy and negligible computational cost. \textit{Our solvers are trained in a fully unsupervised setting, with the training objective informed solely by the partial differential equations, and achieve a $10^3\times$ speed-up with negligible accuracy loss}. These solvers enable scalable EoS inference that self-consistently accounts for rotation and repeated stellar-structure calculations during binary evolution, making spin predictions that account for rotational structure and EoS uncertainties computationally accessible to population synthesis.|[2611.05790](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05790)|
|**2026-11-05**|**DARE: Difficulty Analysis for Repair Enhancement**|Jikwang Lee et.al.|LLM-based coding agents are primarily evaluated by their solve rates. However, this metric does not reveal how difficult the solved tasks are or account for differences intask difficulty across benchmarks. Determining the difficulty of individual tasks by repeatedly running multiple agents is costly, while a predicted difficulty score alone provides limited guidance on what support an agent needs. To address these challenges, we propose DARE (Difficulty Analysis for Repair Enhancement), which integrates repair-task difficulty measurement, prediction, feature-group contribution analysis, and task-specific repair support. We define continuous task difficulty as the observed failure rate across multiple coding agents. The DARE difficulty model predicts this difficulty using 151 static features extracted from benchmark artifacts. The framework also analyzes the contributions of seven Repair-Task Feature Groups to identify the characteristics most salient to each task and uses this information to select task-specific agent support. We evaluate DARE on four benchmark partitions comprising 2,594 tasks. The difficulty model's aligned predictions achieve Spearman's $\rho$ = .476-.621 on held-out partitions, and the most salient Feature Groups vary across tasks and benchmarks. Furthermore, coding-agent experiments show improved repair success when support is matched to each task's salient Feature Group. These findings demonstrate that difficulty prediction and interpretation can lead to practical improvements in automated program repair (APR), while also providing a basis for difficulty-stratified agent evaluation, cross-benchmark comparisons, and identifying areas of repair capability that warrant further improvement.|[2611.05797](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05797)|
|**2026-11-05**|**GREP: Grounding Generated Room Evaluation in Principles**|Mohan Cao et.al.|Recent LLM-centric advances can generate attractive furnished rooms by selecting and arranging assets. Yet evaluation remains limited to specific perspectives or predefined questions, failing to reflect overall scene quality. To address this pressing need, we introduce GREP. Its core is a focus shift from defining specific questions to establishing a three-tier evaluation philosophy: basic physical validity checks whether a scene obeys physical constraints; visual coherence concerns compatibility in object scale, style, and composition; and, at the highest level, human-use logic requires object arrangements to align with everyday human habits. Guided by these principles, an evaluation agent autonomously and adaptively identifies issues from scene context, gathers evidence, and produces scores and reports. This supports scene-specific issue discovery and reliable judgments. Experiments show that (1) GREP achieves 87% agreement with human majority preferences, substantially outperforming baseline evaluators. (2) GREP reports improve scene quality through refinement, whereas score-only and no-feedback baselines show slight declines. (3) GREP suggests that as planning ability improves, recent models such as GPT-5.6 Sol can move from relying on pipelines and harnesses to challenging their guidance and constraints, but still lack the ability to resolve the resulting conflicts. Stronger models are therefore expected not only to plan well, but also to adapt to external guidance and constraints more flexibly. Project page: https://han-808.github.io/GREP/|[2611.05802](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05802)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Matching the Training-Time Weight Rule in Inference-Time Expert Deactivation**|Hao Cheng (Zhejiang Gongshang University) et.al.|Expert deactivation at inference time changes both token routing and expert weights, and its effect on performance depends on how these changes are implemented. We compare removal, freezing, remaining-expert renormalization, and unnormalized re-routing on identical disabled sets across five instruction-tuned checkpoints from four model families, with a base OLMoE checkpoint as an additional comparison. We develop a weight-sum model that relates the loss of expert contributions and changes in branch scale to the weight rule used during training. With half of the experts deactivated according to activation-based rankings, remaining-expert renormalization yields the highest perplexity in models trained with raw router probabilities, whereas unnormalized re-routing yields the highest perplexity in models trained with normalized weights. Perplexity ranges from 1.4 to 73 times the intact baseline when the training rule is retained, and from 14 to 28,740 times the baseline under a rule mismatch. Task outcomes vary across evaluation types: on OLMoE, GSM8K strict-match accuracy falls to zero under all four implementations. Extending the model to changes in the number of active experts yields a further prediction, supported by the OLMoE experiments: the perplexity penalty from additional renormalization increases as fewer experts are activated.|[2611.05804](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05804)|
|**2026-11-05**|**When File Ingestion Misleads Language Models: Measuring and Mitigating Divergences in Office Documents**|Penghui Li et.al.|Office documents are now routinely uploaded to large language models (LLMs) for processing. However, an LLM does not always see what a human sees, because it typically reads the text that an extractor produces from the document's underlying Office Open XML (OOXML) files. Depending on the implementation, extractors differ in which OOXML parts they read and how they interpret them, so their output can differ from what Office applications display. For example, by slightly shifting the positions of text boxes on a slide, an adversary can swap the order in which the model reads them while the slide looks unchanged. We show that this extraction divergence is an attack surface for LLM applications. In this work, we first measure what an adversary can exploit by analyzing the widely used extractors that LLM applications rely on. By combining static source code analysis with dynamic analysis, we identify 19 OOXML channels where extractors read content a human cannot see and 12 where they drop content a human can. We also find that widely used extractors ignore display formatting and reading order, so even visible content reaches the model in a different form. Building on these findings, we construct five attacks that lead the model to interpret a document differently from a human reviewer, without injecting any instruction. We test the attacks on four major chat assistants (ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, and Microsoft 365 Copilot) and four coding agents (Codex, Claude Code, Antigravity, and GitHub Copilot CLI), and every one of them is misled by at least one attack. Finally, we propose Prism, a lightweight defense that reconstructs the human view from the OOXML specification before passing the text to the model. Prism also generalizes to unseen attack payloads.|[2611.05806](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05806)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Learning When Human Decisions Matter: Executor-Relative Consultation Value for LLM Agents**|Shuli Jiang et.al.|Large language models increasingly automate complex tasks, but human decision capacity does not scale with their capabilities. In many applications, humans remain essential for providing private context, authority, or judgments unavailable to the agent. We formulate human decision capacity allocation: given multiple consultation opportunities and a limited budget, at which decisions should an LLM agent consult a human? The central challenge is estimating consultation value, which is executor-dependent because the same human input may benefit different downstream agents differently. We introduce Consultation Value Distillation (CVD), which estimates consultation values offline using Monte Carlo executor rollouts and distills these values into lightweight predictors for budgeted allocation, requiring no rollout-based value estimation at deployment. Across five batch and three sequential settings with two frontier executors, CVD improves allocation through joint and continuation-aware value learning, while independent consultation values remain competitive in several settings. CVD also broadly outperforms direct prompting and Value of Information where applicable, remains robust with substantially less rollout supervision, and enables nearly lossless approximate allocation at low search cost.|[2611.05814](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05814)|
|**2026-11-05**|**A Hybrid Approach for Automated Security Pattern Recovery**|Anh Minh Nguyen et.al.|Security-by-design emphasises integrating security into software design and development. Security pattern catalogues operationalise this principle by prescribing reusable architectural solutions to recurring security problems. However, recovering their implementations from source code remains challenging because security patterns are abstract, span multiple components, and admit many valid implementations. We present SecPatRec, a hybrid approach for automated security pattern recovery from source code repositories. We treat a security pattern as a reference architecture and frame its recovery as a deductive task: determining which prescribed roles are present, grounding them in code entities, and recovering their interaction sequences. SecPatRec pairs a ReAct-style LLM agent equipped with semantic and lexical code search for role detection and grounding with static code property graph analysis for interaction recovery. We curate a human-validated ground-truth dataset of 100 pattern instances covering five security patterns across 93 open-source Java and Python repositories, and introduce a three-level evaluation framework measuring recovery correctness against this ground truth. We evaluate SecPatRec using four LLMs, including proprietary and open-source models. Averaged over all models and pattern instances, SecPatRec achieves role-detection accuracy of 0.84, role-grounding similarity of 0.84, and an interaction-recovery score of 0.66. Compared to a one-shot end-to-end LLM baseline, SecPatRec achieves higher aggregate recovery scores and lower estimated LLM API costs per run, while processing repositories that exceed the baseline's configured input limit. The recovered role mappings and statically derived interaction sequences provide structural and behavioural evidence that can support inspection of security-pattern implementations.|[2611.05823](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05823)|
|**2026-11-05**|**RACE: Calibrated Cumulative Scoring for Rollout-Free Robot Policy Checkpoint Selection**|Changyeon Kim et.al.|Assessing the deployment performance of learned robot policies during training is challenging, as directly measuring task success requires costly real-world trials for each candidate checkpoint. Averaged validation loss offers a rollout-free alternative, but its checkpoint rankings can be inconsistent with deployment performance. We analyze two limitations of this criterion: it penalizes action deviations without considering whether the policy can recover from them, and gives later reference predictions equal weight even when earlier deviations may lead execution away from the reference. We propose RACE, a novel rollout-free metric for ranking checkpoints using a fixed set of held-out successful trajectories. RACE calibrates action deviations against state-conditional thresholds derived from action differences at similar states in these trajectories. We aggregate these scores through a running product, so that a substantial deviation reduces the contribution of subsequent reference predictions. Averaging the resulting cumulative scores over time and across trajectories yields the checkpoint score. In simulation, RACE outperforms baselines across validation sources, while several baselines degrade sharply when the validation set changes. On real robots, its rankings align with deployment success across the evaluated policy backbones and tasks of varying difficulty. Perturbation analyses further show that RACE distinguishes deviations with markedly different effects on task success, even when baselines respond similarly.|[2611.05838](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05838)|
|**2026-11-05**|**PEARL: Prediction Error-Adaptive Regularized Learning for Selective Forgetting under Partial Preference Drift**|Rashmi Gangadharaiah et.al.|User preferences shift as needs, context, and tastes evolve. This matters for personalized recommendation, LLM response generation, and RLHF-trained reward models, which can become miscalibrated as preferences drift. Existing non-stationary bandit methods forget uniformly across all dimensions, but real preference shifts are typically partial: some dimensions go stale while others remain valid. Neural contextual bandits accumulate confidence monotonically via the Gram matrix, with no mechanism to direct uncertainty where predictions have gone wrong. We propose PEARL (Prediction Error-Adaptive Regularized Learning), which reverses the Gram update along feature directions where predictions are surprisingly wrong, while leaving confidence intact elsewhere. We prove that (i) any non-adaptive policy suffers linear regret after a preference shift, (ii) no fixed exploration rate achieves low regret in both stationary and non-stationary regimes, and (iii) PEARL's cumulative regret scales with the number of shifted users N_s, not the total population N. On PRISM, a personalized LLM response selection benchmark (1,500 users, 21 LLMs), PEARL beats greedy by +4.0 points and the best neural bandit baseline by +2.5 points (p<0.001), while performing on par with greedy when preferences are stable. It beats sliding-window and discounted approaches by 3 to 10 points, showing that selective forgetting outperforms uniform forgetting. The selection policy is a ~100K-parameter MLP that runs in milliseconds on CPU, leaves LLM weights frozen, and works with any API-based model.|[2611.05842](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05842)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Generative AI for urban planning: synthesizing land-use maps with satellite imagery via DualControlNet**|Qingqi Song et.al.|Various land-use models characterize land-use patterns and facilitate scenario planning. However, existing models are largely predictive, forecasting transitions based on numeric data without synthesizing new land-use maps from simple text prompts. Meanwhile, generative AI in urban systems remains unimodal, weakly controllable, and evaluated mostly by generic image quality rather than domain-specific metrics. To address these gaps, we introduce DualControlNet, a generative land-use model that jointly synthesizes high-resolution land-use maps and satellite imagery, addressing multimodality, controllability, and verification challenges. Trained on over 40,000 paired tiles from Orlando and Chicago, DualControlNet is evaluated using land-use, spatial consistency, and image quality metrics. Relative to unimodal baselines, DualControlNet improves land-use diversity consistency by 62.8% and building coverage ratio consistency by 16.3%, more than doubles cross-modal spatial consistency, and triples visual quality. Scenario-based experiments demonstrate that DualControlNet supports sustainable development through multidimensional controls, increasing density and diversity, and responding to text prompts like "high-density development." Overall, this work advances land-use modeling via a multimodal, controllable, and verifiable generative framework, lowering technical barriers and enabling intuitive exploration of urban futures.|[2611.05859](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05859)|
|**2026-11-05**|**AI-Assisted Classification under Correlation Neglect and Trust**|Dingwen Kong et.al.|When does an AI assistant improve human decision-making, and when does it hurt? We argue that the answer depends not just on AI accuracy but also on how much its evidence overlaps with the human's and how the human combines the two. A worker makes a binary decision by observing her own signal and receiving a recommendation from an AI. We develop a human behavioral model with two primitives: her awareness of the overlap and her trust in the AI. Our analysis gives a closed-form phase diagram over AI capability and overlap, with regions of complementarity, impairment, and automation. A worker who neglects the overlap (correlation neglect) can be made less accurate by a more capable AI, double-counting the shared evidence. Trust reshapes the regions - undertrust may compensate for correlation neglect, so that the trust level maximizing accuracy depends on the deployment parameters. Empirically, we identify the two model parameters by pairing the same workers with different AIs, and break the accuracy loss into a correlation-neglect cost and an undertrust cost, one tied to reducing overlap on the AI side, the other to calibrating trust on the human side. On ChatBench, AI assistance raises accuracy by 21 points, yet workers apply only about a quarter of the Bayes-rational weight to AI information; GPT-4o falls in the automation region and Llama-3.1-8b stays at the complementarity boundary; and calibrating trust recovers about 2.6 points for GPT-4o but almost nothing for Llama-3.1-8b.|[2611.05870](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05870)|
|**2026-11-05**|**CellCanon: An Agentic Cellular World Model for Prediction, Explanation, and Observation-Driven Updating**|Zhaokang Liang et.al.|Biological discovery requires using molecular knowledge and new measurements to infer responses beyond the conditions already observed. We introduce CellCanon, the first agentic cellular world model to unify single-cell expression prediction, evidence-grounded explanation, and observation-driven updating. Its predictive core reuses condition-level biological evidence across gene-specific numerical readouts to guide single-cell expression generation. A shared world state links predictions, acquired measurements, and source-linked claims, supporting explanation and revision. On 339 held-out SciPlex3 conditions across three cell lines, CellCanon improves differential-expression AUPRC and Macro-F1 over the best baseline for each metric by 63.3% and 17.3%, respectively, and direction Macro-F1 by 7.2%. Relative to gene-wise reasoning on matched drug-cell cohorts, measured language-model token use falls by 99.77% for Predict and 98.41% for Explain. On the source-grounded benchmark, Explain correctly answers all answerable questions with supporting proofs and identifies all insufficient-evidence cases. In simulated experimental feedback, Update uses one measured source context per drug to exceed the best fine-tuned baselines in differential-expression AUPRC and Macro-F1 without changing model weights. Together, these capabilities support a cumulative account of cellular responses, with predictions and biological judgments that can be revised as evidence accumulates.|[2611.05890](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05890)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Does Your Trial Simulator Know What the Drug Does? An Intervention-Blind Test of Interventional Validity on 7,207 Registered Trials**|Alicem Koyun et.al.|Patient world models are increasingly proposed as simulators of clinical trials, and recent surveys rank their capabilities on a ladder from temporal prediction to counterfactual rollouts. Position on that ladder is currently assigned by reading papers rather than by measurement. We turn the counterfactual rung into a measured quantity. From 30,119 completed phase 2/3 trials with results posted to ClinicalTrials.gov we construct 9,996 audited within-trial contrasts from 7,207 trials, and we score a simulator on how much of its accuracy depends on knowing which intervention was tested. The instrument is an intervention-blind reference: the same model, trained on the same trials, with every intervention-identifying field withheld. Interventional skill is the error the full model removes relative to that reference. On a temporal split (878 test contrasts from 655 trials) a registry-trained gradient-boosting simulator removes a fifth of the error of a trivial baseline, but its interventional skill is -0.12 (95% CI -0.21 to -0.05): withholding the intervention makes it better, and no alternative featurisation, stratum, temporal cut or same-molecule retrieval yields an interval above zero. A frontier language model reading only the registered protocol has interventional skill 0.20 [0.08, 0.31]; smaller and open-weight models sit at the limit of detection. Perturbation tests show the large model uses the intervention as such: swapping the arms reverses the sign of its prediction in 86% of contrasts. The blind reference is itself not drug-blind (a language model recovers the withheld drug class 45% of the time from the blind prompt), so interventional skill is a conservative lower bound. A reliability layer shows that ranking by predicted error lowers it but that calibrated error control does not transfer under temporal shift. Benchmark, code, prompts and per-call model outputs are released.|[2611.05907](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05907)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Do Saliency Maps Fail Dense Breasts? A Chance-Normalized, Confounder-Aware Audit of Explanation Fairness in Mammography**|Ahmed Jaheen et.al.|Saliency maps, offered as evidence that mammography classifiers use the cancer, are untested in dense breasts, which can hide cancers. We tested whether they locate cancers worse there, and why. On 1,345 malignant CBIS-DDSM views, we scored three CNNs' Grad-CAM, Integrated Gradients and occlusion maps by whether their peak hits the lesion and a chance-normalized lesion-to-background saliency ratio (bits; +1 doubles it). Unadjusted gaps were large: Grad-CAM peaks hit 44% of dense vs. 58% of non-dense lesions. Case mix, chiefly more calcifications in dense breasts, explained about half or more of each gap. Occlusion kept a significant average of -0.56 bit gap. Controls also implicate the classifiers: unadjusted gaps exceeded an image-only conspicuity map's, and models relied less on dense-breast lesions. We recommend reporting density gaps before and after case-mix adjustment, per classifier, with conspicuity and lesion-reliance controls.|[2611.05909](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05909)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Autonomous ETL Pipelines for Salesforce M&A Consolidation: A Reference Framework, Jitterbit Mapping, and Reproducible Simulation Study**|Imran Basha Shaik et.al.|Merging companies that each run Salesforce must consolidate their customer data into a single organization while both source systems remain in use. This requires reconciling different schemas and picklists, resolving duplicate customers across and within organizations, preserving relationships between records, and keeping the target current until cutover. This article proposes a reference framework for an autonomous extract-transform-load pipeline for this task. It combines partitioned bulk extraction, declarative mapping templates, rule-based and statistical quality gates, master data management with deterministic and blocked fuzzy matching, constrained loading with per-record error capture, and change data capture guarded by a data-health circuit breaker. Each stage is mapped to Jitterbit Integration Studio constructs. The framework was implemented as an open reference prototype and evaluated on synthetic two-organization scenarios with known ground truth at three scales, from 32,040 to 383,973 source records, over five random seeds. At a preset threshold, hybrid matching reached pairwise F1 scores of 0.918 for accounts and 0.885 for contacts; calibrating the threshold per object would raise these to 0.938 and 0.963. The quality gates prevented all load failures and all silent loss of unmapped picklist values without wrongly flagging any clean record, whereas removing them produced about 1,000 load failures per run. Over five simulated days of hourly incremental synchronization, replayed events were ignored, an injected upstream fault was contained in the batch in which it occurred, and the target agreed with a full rebuild on more than 99.3 percent of field values. Validation on a production deployment remains future work.|[2611.05918](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05918)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Deep Meets Classical: A CPU-Efficient Decision-Fusion Model for Automatic Modulation Classification**|Zhiyi Yin et.al.|Automatic modulation classification (AMC) underpins spectrum sensing and non-cooperative signal identification in cognitive radio, yet the two standard CPU-feasible approaches to it, a compact convolutional neural network (CNN) on raw in-phase and quadrature (I/Q) samples and a classical higher-order-cumulant feature classifier, are rarely compared against each other conditioned on signal-to-noise ratio (SNR). We show that they are, in fact, complementary: the CNN is markedly more robust at low SNR, where hand-crafted statistics collapse toward chance, while the cumulant classifier is more accurate at high SNR, where higher-order statistics cleanly separate dense constellations. We exploit this complementarity with FuseAMC, a lightweight decision-fusion model that trains a logistic-regression meta-combiner over the concatenated class-posteriors of an approximately 198k-parameter residual CNN and a gradient-boosted-tree cumulant classifier, on a validation split disjoint from test. On RadioML 2016.10a (5 seeds, 44k-frame held-out test set), FuseAMC reaches 55.8% overall accuracy, improving on the best cumulant-feature baseline by 5.1 points and on the CNN alone by 3.2 points (paired-bootstrap 95% confidence-interval lower bounds of 5.0 and 3.1 points, both positive), while matching the best classical baseline in its own strong high-SNR regime and the CNN in its strong low-SNR regime. With about 0.2 million parameters, a model footprint of a few megabytes, and roughly 3 MFLOPs per frame, the whole pipeline trains and runs on one CPU core, targeting general-purpose and embedded processors without hardware acceleration.|[2611.05937](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05937)|
|**2026-11-05**|**BAM: Bivariate Additive Models for Interpretable Tabular Prediction**|Atsushi Koike et.al.|Generalized Additive Models with pairwise interactions (GA$^2$Ms) are regarded as glass-box models, since each pairwise shape function can be visualized as a contour plot. In practice, the shape functions learned by methods such as EBM or NODE-GAM are so complex (irregular contours and sharp local fluctuations) that a practitioner cannot meaningfully interpret them or check them against domain knowledge. Auditability in this stronger sense requires substantially simpler shape functions. We argue that such simplification can match the accuracy of standard GAMs if the right feature pairs are captured. We propose Bivariate Additive Models (BAMs), a class of GA$^2$Ms in which every pairwise shape function is constrained to a simple parametric form, instantiated as an asymmetric bivariate Gaussian. Each component is visualizable as a unimodal contour map, and its feature pair is selected automatically during training. Experiments on six tabular benchmarks show that BAM attains predictive accuracy comparable to standard GAMs and approaches that of GA$^2$Ms. We also show the trade-off between accuracy and the number of components to inspect.|[2611.05940](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05940)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Ray-Tracing-Based Dynamic Channel Sounding With Autoregressive Wi-Fi CSI Prediction**|Lyutianyang Zhang et.al.|Channel sounding in Wi-Fi multiple-input multiple-output (MIMO) systems must balance channel state information (CSI) accuracy against CSI acquisition overhead. Device mobility causes channel aging, while autoregressive CSI prediction introduces errors that accumulate over sounding intervals. However, standardized statistical channel models cannot fully capture the environment-specific channel dynamics encountered in physical artificial intelligence (AI) applications, motivating the use of Sionna-based ray tracing to evaluate CSI prediction under device mobility and evolving propagation geometry. This paper develops a dynamic sounding framework that integrates autoregressive CSI prediction with signal-to-interference-plus-noise ratio (SINR)-constrained interval selection. A Transformer with rotary position embedding that converts device positions into CSI sequences predicts CSI across multiple subcarriers for beamforming updates between sounding exchanges. We formulate sounding-interval selection as a predicted average-throughput maximization problem under per-stream SINR lower-bound constraints, including CSI acquisition and processing overhead. A prediction-error perturbation analysis provides the SINR bounds, and cumulative-sum enumeration globally solves the finite single-variable problem. Actual SINR feasibility holds whenever the prediction errors satisfy the bounds. Ray-tracing experiments assess channel prediction, trajectory transfer, and autoregressive prediction errors. In single-STA Wi-Fi 7 link-level simulations, predicted-CSI beamforming improves mean goodput by 67.3%, 112.7%, and 119.1% over stale CSI for linear, circular, and cosine trajectories, respectively.|[2611.05970](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05970)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Clips2Story: Training-Free Video Storyboarding and Editing Using Multimodal Retrieval-Embedded Generation**|Junnuo Wang et.al.|Video editors often work with large collections of fragmented footage and must select and organize relevant clips into a coherent story. However, many existing video editing and summarization methods assume a temporally ordered source video and aim to preserve its original narrative and temporal structure. In this paper, we introduce Clips2Story, a training-free, LLM-based pipeline that enables users to create keyword-specified short videos from a pool of video clips. Given a pool of clips and a user-specified keyword, we first extract text-based multimodal descriptors for each clip and retrieve the clips most relevant to the keyword. We then provide the textual descriptors of these candidate clips to an LLM, which selects and arranges them into an editable storyboard with connective narrations. Finally, we assemble the selected clips and retrieve accompanying visuals for the narrations to compose the output video.
