The effectiveness of such systems depends not only on the agents themselves, but also on how collaboration mechanisms are designed and organized. Note that real-world collaboration is typically partially observable, where each agent can only access partial information about the environment due to physical or privacy-related constraints. However, many existing multi-agent benchmarks assume global observability, and leave limited support for systematically evaluating collaboration mechanisms. To bridge this gap, we introduce MASBench, a multi-agent collaboration benchmark designed under partially observable constraints. It is organized into three progressive task categories: Reasoning, Scheduling, and Game. Through this structure, we progressively evaluate three representative collaboration mechanisms: Protocol, Memory, and Routing. MASBench further provides deterministic evaluation metrics, including performance score, communication cost, and cost effectiveness, to characterize both collaboration outcomes and communication overhead. Experiments across diverse LLM backbones and mechanism configurations offer empirical guidance for effective MAS design. Code is available at: https://github.com/BUPT-GAMMA/MASBench|[2610.04672](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04672)|
|**2026-11-05**|**RetryBench: Separating Retry Permission from Recovery Outcomes in GPT Tool Use**|You Liu et.al.|LLM agents that call external tools must decide what to do when a tool call times out: retrying risks duplicate side effects, while not retrying can leave the task incomplete. We present RetryBench, an evaluation protocol for retry decisions and failure recovery in tool-using LLM agents. Each decision is scored on retry permission (is the retry justified by an idempotency-key guarantee or proof of non-execution?), settled effects (duplicates and omissions), and completion, by replaying it across all hidden execution worlds consistent with the observation. On four synthetic software-engineering tasks, we run two controlled prompt-sensitivity studies (1,728 decisions). Study 1 (idempotency-key descriptions): with the contract fixed, GPT-5.6 Luna reuses the valid idempotency key in 10/40 decisions when an extra key is described as a "Candidate recovery key" but in 39/40 when it is an "Alternative key string"; across three further phrasings per family the gap is 27/120 versus 113/120. GPT-5.6 Sol and Terra are at ceiling (40/40). Study 2 (action order): under underspecified lookup guarantees, retry rates are 17-27 points lower for GPT-5.6 Terra and GPT-5.4 when retry is listed first (option-order bias), mainly on two tasks; we make no claim about which behavior is better. Findings are limited to researcher-chosen phrasings, four tasks and unpinned model aliases. Code and all model responses are released.|[2610.15884](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15884)|
|**2026-11-05**|**A latent control model for realistic rodent motion**|Aidan Sirbu et.al.|The development of embodied models of animal behavior is a major goal of the neuro-AI community. Embodied models will enhance our ability to make direct comparisons between biological and artificial agents. To make such comparisons effective, though, we need embodied models that actually move like animals. Previous work has developed virtual animal bodies and techniques for training realistic motion through imitation. However, to date, there is no system for embodied animal modeling that produces natural movements both during trained goal-directed behavior, and during exploration driven by random generation. Here, we introduce Simulated Control via Augmented Motor Priors for Embodied Reinforcement learning (SCAMPER), a system for training an embodied virtual rat model, enabling natural movements during both goal-directed behavior and random exploration. SCAMPER uses a variational architecture with two distinct priors, a motion prior for control and a behavior prior for exploration. We show that combining both of these priors allows SCAMPER to generate realistic behavior during exploration and exploit the task structure effectively. SCAMPER can also learn from sparse rewards as a result of the diverse movements induced by the behavior prior, allowing the system to model realistic sparse-reward tasks from experimental neuroscience. Altogether, SCAMPER provides the neuro-AI community with an effective new system for training embodied models of rodents on realistic tasks.|[2610.27279](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27279)|
|**2026-11-05**|**HyScale: Scaling Long-Context Training for Learned Sparse Attention**|Yangshen Deng et.al.|We present HyScale, a long-context training system for learned sparse attention. Learned sparse attention is a promising architecture for million-token contexts, widely adopted in production since DeepSeek-V3.2. Selection, however, grows into the training bottleneck. For each query, it scores every previous token whereas sparse attention reads only $k$; worse, current systems materialize a full score table and repeat selection during recomputation. Our key insight is that the output of selection is not an activation, but rather a set of indices. Building on it, HyScale introduces FlashTopK and selection replay. FlashTopK finds each query's exact top-$k$ on the fly without a score table, while selection replay reuses forward-pass selection in recomputation. Together with holistic system optimizations tackling end-to-end challenges, HyScale speeds up GLM-5.1 training on 1M-token sequences by $2.07\times$ over Megatron-LM on 512 H20 GPUs. It also speeds up the CSA layers of DeepSeek-V4-Flash and the QSA layers of Qwen3.8-Flash-Next by $2.01\times$ and $1.57\times$ at 1M tokens on 32 H800 GPUs.|[2610.32057](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32057)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Isharah-Selfie: Continuous Sign Language Recognition Dataset for One-handed Signing**|Ahmed Abul Hasanaath et.al.|Current sign language recognition and translation benchmarks are commonly collected in controlled settings using fixed or studio-style cameras, particularly limiting their ability to reflect how signers communicate in everyday mobile scenarios. As such, this gap becomes increasingly evident when considering the Arabic Sign Language (ArSL), which is a primary sign language used across Arab countries. In this work, we introduce Isharah-Selfie, a large-scale ArSL dataset collected entirely using front-facing smartphone cameras under unconstrained selfie conditions. The dataset comprises 8,997 video clips performed by 16 signers across 1,493 unique sentences, covering several domains such as healthcare, education, and transportation. Unlike conventional datasets dominated by fixed-camera, two-handed signing, Isharah-Selfie captures realistic mobile communication characterized by one-handed signing, close-range viewpoints, partial body visibility, camera motion, unconstrained environments, and heterogeneous device resolutions. Each video is annotated with both gloss sequences for continuous sign language recognition (CSLR) and Arabic sentence translations for sign language translation (SLT). Moreover, we define signer-independent and unseen-sentences evaluation protocols to assess generalization across unseen signers and novel sentence compositions. Extensive benchmarks using RGB-based and pose-based data show that current CSLR and SLT methods remain challenged by selfie-style signing conditions, particularly under unseen-sentence evaluation, where compositional generalization remains weak. By releasing Isharah-Selfie, we aim to support research toward robust, user-centered sign language technologies that operate reliably in realistic smartphone-based communication settings. The Isharah-Selfie dataset is available on https://isharah-selfie.github.io/.|[2610.33200](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33200)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Local Entropy Fluctuations for Black-Box LLM-Generated Text Detection**|Xinpeng Wang et.al.|Zero-shot detection of machine-generated text is vital for mitigating the risks of large language models (LLMs) misuse. While the state-of-the-art dual-model detectors and resampling-based detectors outperform single-model statistical detectors on unprompted continuations, they suffer a catastrophic breakdown on instruction-conditioned text in the black-box setting, where the source model is unknown. We analyze this failure and trace it to poor prompt conditioning for resampling-based methods and diminished cross-model discrepancy for dual-model detectors. Motivated by recent studies that human writing maintains a steady, uniform information flow, whereas machine-generated text exhibits sharp, localized entropy fluctuations, we propose a new metric, the Relative Peak Prominence Rate (RelPPR) to capture this structural contrast along the entropy trajectory and further leverage it as a signal for LLM-generated text detection. Extensive evaluations across diverse model families and datasets demonstrate that RelPPR achieves state-of-the-art black-box detection performance and remains the most reliable detector under various practical situations. Our code is available at https://github.com/brushing-git/entropy-llm-detector.|[2610.33767](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33767)|
|**2026-11-05**|**OS-Wizard: Scaling Computer-Using Agents to Specialized Software via Knowledge-Guided Exploration**|Zhoumianze Liu et.al.|Computer-using agents are increasingly capable of performing complex tasks in the digital world. To advance such agents, supervised learning on annotated interaction trajectories has become a common approach to improving their task-solving capabilities. However, high-quality interaction trajectories remain scarce for specialized desktop applications, where limited application knowledge, complex GUI workflows, and incomplete exploration hinder automated collection. To address these challenges, we introduce \aks{}, a knowledge-guided automated trajectory collection framework. It combines tutorial-guided exploration with a hybrid GUI--Code action space, providing application-specific knowledge and simplifying GUI workflows through direct execution of application-native commands. The framework also checkpoints and revisits pivotal states during tree exploration to explore alternative workflows, complementing linear rollouts for broader coverage. We collect 10.9K automatically validated trajectories and use them to fine-tune open-source base models. The resulting model achieves 64.1\% accuracy on a benchmark of specialized desktop workflows, improving performance by 58.2 percentage points, establishing a new state of the art. The fine-tuned model also improves performance on general desktop workflows using GUI actions only, suggesting that the collected supervision benefits general desktop tasks as well. Comparative experiments and ablations demonstrate the efficiency of our collection framework and the value of code actions in specialized workflows.|[2610.33907](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33907)|
|**2026-11-05**|**AutoEnergyDesign: An LLM-Driven Agent for Automatic Energy System Design Optimization**|Amedeo Buonanno et.al.|The design optimization of building energy systems represents a complex, high-dimensional search problem involving discrete technology selections, continuous sizing parameters, and intricate economic trade-offs in which every candidate must be evaluated by a year-long simulation. This paper presents AutoEnergyDesign, in which a Large Language Model (LLM) agent reasons over past simulation results, proposes configuration changes and learns in context from each outcome, while a git-based mechanism reverts changes that do not improve the design. The agent couples a reasoning model with explicit memories, a constrained action interface, and an environment-grounded keep/revert feedback signal. The simulator covers eight technologies and computes the 25-year Net Present Cost (NPC). On a case study with measured building loads, the agent, instantiated with three Claude models (Haiku 5.5, Sonnet 5.5 and Opus 5.5; ten runs of 50 simulations each), is compared with random search, Tree-structured Parzen Estimator (TPE) Bayesian optimization and a (1+1) evolution strategy that shares the agent's acceptance rule (ten seeds of 300 evaluations). With all three models the agent reaches an NPC of 3.53MEUR, 1.20% and 0.23% below the best results of TPE and of the evolution strategy, and comes within 1% of it after a median of 18 to 27.5 simulations; among the baselines, only the evolution strategy does so, after a median of 192 evaluations. Every decision is recorded, making the search auditable.|[2610.35160](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35160)|
|**2026-11-05**|**MedConstBench: Benchmarking How Constitutions Steer LLMs in Medical Ethics**|Yibo Yang et.al.|As large language models advance rapidly, they are increasingly used to answer medical questions, and many such models are now deployed locally. Once a model is deployed, its behavior is governed mainly by the ethical policy written into its constitution. However, most existing work focuses on factual correctness rather than the trade-off between competing ethical values. Even benchmarks that do evaluate ethics treat it as a fixed property of the model rather than as a variable, so how a constitution reshapes medical-ethics behavior remains largely unexplored. We introduce MedConstBench, a medical-ethics benchmark that treats the deployment constitution as a controlled variable, is organized around Beauchamp's four principles, and is scored by human-calibrated multi-model peer review. It includes MedDilemma, our own dataset of clinical dilemmas, and supports a systematic analysis of how constitutions steer LLMs. Evaluating ten open-source LLMs under four constitutions, we find that the effect of a constitution is dimension-selective and model-dependent, and that no constitution is universally optimal. Motivated by these findings, we propose a do-no-harm adaptive selector that chooses a constitution per model, matching the per-model oracle while guaranteeing a worst-case floor and generalizing to unseen models at low cost.|[2610.36513](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36513)|
|**2026-11-05**|**AuroraWorld: Structured 4D World Modeling for Exo-to-Ego Video Generation in Robot Manipulation**|Hao Xue et.al.|Egocentric observations provide a robot-centric view of manipulation processes, capturing contact interactions and object-state changes that are important for visuomotor policy learning but difficult to observe from external cameras. However, collecting such data at scale remains expensive due to additional hardware requirements and platform-specific deployment costs. Synthesizing egocentric observations from large-scale exocentric manipulation videos provides a scalable alternative. However, existing methods struggle to synthesize physically consistent and policy-relevant egocentric videos under substantial viewpoint transformations, severe occlusions, complex object interactions, and inherently sparse observations from available exocentric viewpoints.Code will be made publicly available at https://github.com/xueha0/AuroraWorld|[2611.00177](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00177)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Quantifying Local and Global Fidelity of Nonlinear Embeddings**|Henry Han et.al.|Dimension reduction rests on a claim: t-SNE and UMAP preserve local structure, PCA global structure. Our DLP curve is the share of each point's k nearest neighbors a map keeps. Corrected for chance, it gives L at k=10 and G over 30 to 50% of the data, local and global fidelity. At the midpoint the curve is provably a rank correlation of distances. The LG reversal test decides, with a stated error rate, which of two maps is local and which global. On twelve datasets of up to 1.38 million points, G agrees with established global scores at 0.84 and 0.93, and the area under R_NX at 0.22 and 0.30. The entity fidelity E, read at each point's entity size, predicts which entities a map loses beyond L and G where entity sizes differ. In an agent memory of one million passages, t-SNE and PCA cross at 150,000 passages (p=2.6e-5).|[2611.00290](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00290)|
|**2026-11-05**|**VCoT-SQL: Enhancing Text-to-SQL Generation through Dual Chain-of-Thought Validation Training**|Rui Zhang et.al.|Open-source LLMs provide a flexible alternative to closed-source models for Text-to-SQL but still struggle with complex queries. Finetuning with chain-of-thought (CoT) data improves reasoning, yet standard datasets lack CoT annotations, requiring synthetic generation. Existing synthesis methods only verify the final answer, allowing flawed reasoning steps to corrupt the training data. We propose VCoT-SQL, a framework that improves Text-to-SQL generation through intermediate-step validated CoT supervision and a dedicated SQL validator, which enables small open-source models to outperform frontier closed-source models. VCoT-SQL trains two complementary models on curated reasoning traces: an SQL generator using clause-level CoT templates where reasoning steps are verified via execution and schema alignment checks, and an SQL validator using diagnostic CoT templates to detect syntactic, semantic, and type level errors. We further design a hierarchical schema filtering pipeline that integrates and refines complementary retrieval strategies to distill relevant database elements. VCoT-SQL achieves 66.82% execution accuracy on challenging dataset BIRD dev and 86.8% on Spider test, improving the base 7B model by 7% on BIRD. Notably, our CoT synthesis relies on 7B models yet outperforms methods that use 72B-scale or even proprietary closed source teachers for data generation, demonstrating that rigorous step-level validation can be more effective than scaling up teacher model size. Our dataset and code are available at https://github.com/INTREBID/VCoT-SQL.|[2611.00815](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00815)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Bidirectional Centered Score Matching for Discrete Diffusion Distillation**|Peng Tu et.al.|Discrete diffusion models with parallel decoding are often distilled using token-wise KL objectives. Although token-wise KL exactly matches the corresponding product-form block distributions, its probability-space optimization geometry and teacher-only candidate truncation may provide insufficient supervision under aggressive few-step distillation. Concrete Score Matching (CSM) provides an alternative within-position objective by aligning relative logit geometry, but its complete pairwise form incurs \mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{V}|^2) complexity. We propose Bidirectional Centered Score Matching (BCSM), an efficient within-position distillation objective that constructs an \mathcal{O}(K) bidirectional active set, reduces pairwise comparisons to \mathcal{O}(K^2), and factorizes them into an \mathcal{O}(K) objective. We further introduce cross-timestep transition alignment. Experiments demonstrate strong quality--efficiency trade-offs, including up to 6\times NFE reduction with a 16.9\% relative HumanEval gain and a 15.1\% relative MMMU gain at single-step inference.|[2611.04279](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04279)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Signals: Continuous Diagnostics of AI Agents**|Amey Rambatla et.al.|AI agents can make incorrect decisions or waste resources even when their tools and infrastructure work normally. Their nondeterministic behavior makes these problems difficult to reproduce and diagnose. We developed Signals to identify such problems from agentic traces and suggest concrete remediations. We built the method around a taxonomy of 45 problem types across eight families, with definitions and evidence requirements for each type. The method distinguishes realized failures, risk exposures, and inefficiencies, groups related findings into engineering issues called Signals, and assesses their severity from the supporting evidence. Each Signal includes actions a developer can take and checks for evaluating the change. Further executions can add evidence to an existing Signal or reveal a new problem. We evaluated the method on public agent executions and achieved 91.43% F1 when matching Signals to reference issues, about 18 percentage points higher than a one-pass LLM control that used the same model and taxonomy.|[2611.04507](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04507)|
|**2026-11-05**|**AgenticFinBench: Benchmarking Financial Agents on Diverse Tasks from Real-World Requests**|Shu Zhou et.al.|Financial agents are becoming an important class of real-world AI systems. Financial tasks vary widely in type and difficulty, and many are open-ended with no ground-truth answers. However, existing benchmarks neither capture this breadth and open-endedness nor separate grounding from correctness. To bridge this gap, we introduce $\textbf{AgenticFinBench}$, a benchmark of 230 real-world financial tasks spanning 8 categories, 2 languages, and 4 complexity levels. We also propose a reference-free evaluation framework that combines question-specific rubrics with a two-stage claim audit that detects hallucinations, defined as claims absent from the agent's trace and refuted by external evidence. This framework better matches how financial work is assessed in practice. Experiments across 18 agent configurations reveal three key findings: (1) Ignoring hallucinations can overstate agent performance: hallucination auditing lowers the baseline leader's score by 7.58 points and moves it to fourth place. (2) Harness differences explain more variation in evidence grounding than model differences (28.6% vs 8.8%). (3) Quality scores fall from 89.44 on direct retrieval to 79.18 on the most complex tasks. Case studies show that agents struggle to explain how events affect company profits or stock prices, compare financial data on a consistent basis, and justify their conclusions. By releasing AgenticFinBench, we aim to advance financial reasoning, factual verification, and agent design for more reliable responses to diverse real-world financial requests.|[2611.04813](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04813)|
|**2026-11-05**|**ScamWorld: An Interactive Environment for Evaluating Web Agents Against Online Fraud**|Yutao Wu et.al.|Web agents increasingly browse, shop, and manage accounts on behalf of their users. Prior work has shown that they can be misled by adversarial content, including prompt injections and deceptive interfaces. Beyond such attacks, agents operating on the open web also encounter online fraud designed to deceive humans, including fraudulent online stores, fake investment platforms, and advance-fee schemes. How web agents respond to such fraud during task execution remains underexplored, in part because scam websites are short-lived, making systematic evaluation difficult. To address this gap, we introduce ScamWorld, an interactive environment that reconstructs reported scam websites across diverse scenarios, fraud mechanisms, and languages. Each reconstructed website reproduces documented fraud mechanisms, making relevant signs of fraud observable before the agent's final action and enabling controlled, repeatable evaluation. Through an extensive study of web agents powered by six frontier LLMs, we find that baseline deception rates range from 11% to 35% across models. Agents proceed more readily with payments than with requests for credentials or wallet information. A closer analysis of agent trajectories shows that some agents acknowledge suspicious conditions yet proceed, at times treating the user's instructions as authorisation to accept additional terms imposed by the website. ScamWorld provides a reusable testbed for systematically evaluating and stress-testing web agents against real-world online fraud.|[2611.04879](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04879)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Learning to Incentivize Perfect-Knowledge Agents with Unobserved Rewards**|Ilgin Dogan et.al.|Motivated by a number of real-world applications from domains like healthcare and sustainable transportation, in this paper we study a scenario of repeated principal-agent games within a multi-armed bandit (MAB) framework, where: the principal gives a different incentive for each bandit arm, the agent picks a bandit arm to maximize its own expected reward plus incentive, and the principal observes which arm is chosen and receives a reward (different than that of the agent) for the chosen arm. Designing policies for the principal is challenging because the principal cannot directly observe the reward that the agent receives for their chosen actions, and so the principal cannot directly learn the expected reward using existing estimation techniques. As a result, the problem of designing policies for this scenario, as well as similar ones, remains mostly unexplored. In this paper, we construct a policy that achieves a low regret (i.e., square-root regret up to a log factor) in this scenario for the case where the agent has perfect-knowledge about its own expected rewards for each bandit arm. We design our policy by first constructing an estimator for the agent's expected reward for each bandit arm. Since our estimator uses as data the sequence of incentives offered and subsequently chosen arms, the principal's estimation can be regarded as an analogy of online inverse optimization in MAB's. Next, we construct a policy that we prove achieves a low regret by deriving finite-sample concentration bounds for our estimator. We conclude with numerical simulations demonstrating the applicability of our framework to personalized adherence-improving incentive design for long-term statin treatments.|[2304.07407](http://arxiv.org/abs/2304.07407)|
|**2026-11-05**|**When the Agent Learns Too: Learning to Incentivize with Unobserved Rewards**|Ilgin Dogan et.al.|In practice, incentive providers (i.e., principals) often cannot observe the reward realizations of incentivized agents, which is in contrast to many principal-agent models that have been previously studied. This information asymmetry challenges the principal to consistently estimate the agent's unknown rewards by solely watching the agent's decisions, which becomes even more challenging when the agent has to learn its own rewards. This complex setting is observed in various real-life scenarios ranging from renewable energy storage contracts to personalized healthcare incentives. Hence, it offers not only interesting theoretical questions but also wide practical relevance. This paper explores a repeated adverse selection game between a self-interested learning agent and a learning principal. The agent tackles a multi-armed bandit (MAB) problem to maximize their expected reward plus incentive. On top of the agent's learning, the principal trains a parallel algorithm and faces a trade-off between consistently estimating the agent's unknown rewards and maximizing their own utility by offering adaptive incentives to lead the agent. For a non-parametric model, we introduce an estimator whose only input is the history of principal's incentives and agent's choices. We unite this estimator with a proposed data-driven incentive policy within a MAB framework. Without restricting the type of the agent's algorithm, we prove finite-sample consistency of the estimator and a rigorous regret bound for the principal by considering the sequential externality imposed by the agent. Lastly, our theoretical results are reinforced by simulations justifying applicability of our framework to green energy aggregator contracts.|[2308.06717](http://arxiv.org/abs/2308.06717)|
|**2026-11-05**|**EgoGen: An Egocentric Synthetic Data Generator**|Gen Li et.al.|Understanding the world in first-person view is fundamental in Augmented Reality (AR). This immersive perspective brings dramatic visual changes and unique challenges compared to third-person views. Synthetic data has empowered third-person-view vision models, but its application to embodied egocentric perception tasks remains largely unexplored. A critical challenge lies in simulating natural human movements and behaviors that effectively steer the embodied cameras to capture a faithful egocentric representation of the 3D world. To address this challenge, we introduce EgoGen, a new synthetic data generator that can produce accurate and rich ground-truth training data for egocentric perception tasks. At the heart of EgoGen is a novel human motion synthesis model that directly leverages egocentric visual inputs of a virtual human to sense the 3D environment. Combined with collision-avoiding motion primitives and a two-stage reinforcement learning approach, our motion synthesis model offers a closed-loop solution where the embodied perception and movement of the virtual human are seamlessly coupled. Compared to previous works, our model eliminates the need for a pre-defined global path, and is directly applicable to dynamic environments. Combined with our easy-to-use and scalable data generation pipeline, we demonstrate EgoGen's efficacy in three tasks: mapping and localization for head-mounted cameras, egocentric camera tracking, and human mesh recovery from egocentric views. EgoGen will be fully open-sourced, offering a practical solution for creating realistic egocentric training data and aiming to serve as a useful tool for egocentric computer vision research. Refer to our project page: https://ego-gen.github.io/.|[2401.08739](http://arxiv.org/abs/2401.08739)|
|**2026-11-05**|**ENACT-Heart -- ENsemble-based Assessment Using CNN and Transformer on Heart Sounds**|Jiho Han et.al.|ENACT-Heart is a two-branch ensemble for heart-sound classification. A Vision Transformer (ViT) processes spectrogram images, while a convolutional neural network (CNN) processes visualizations of waveforms and spectral centroids derived from the same recordings. The branch predictions are combined by a weighted average with fixed, input-independent weights. On the PASCAL heart-sound dataset, the ensemble achieved an accuracy of 97.52%, compared with 93.88% for the ViT and 95.45% for the CNN. The ensemble achieved a macro-F1 of 0.94 and recall of 0.74 for extra heart sounds. On this dataset, late fusion improved accuracy and macro-F1 relative to both individual branches.|[2502.16914](http://arxiv.org/abs/2502.16914)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Hindsight Relabeling is All You Need for Reach-Avoid Learning**|Kevin Li et.al.|Offline goal-conditioned reinforcement learning methods have shown promise for reach-avoid tasks, where an agent must reach a target state while avoiding undesirable regions of the state space. Existing approaches typically encode avoid-region information into an augmented state space and cost function, which prevents flexible, dynamic specification of novel avoid-region information at evaluation time. They also rely heavily on meticulously designed reward and cost functions, limiting transferability to novel environments and specifications. We introduce RADT, a decision transformer approach for offline, reward-free, goal-conditioned, avoid-region-conditioned RL. RADT encodes goals and avoid-regions directly as prompt tokens, allowing any number of avoid-regions of arbitrary size to be specified at evaluation time. Using only suboptimal offline trajectories from a random policy, RADT can learn reach-avoid behavior in a completely data-driven manner using our novel avoid-region hindsight relabeling approach, without the need for reward/cost supervision. We benchmark RADT against existing offline goal-conditioned RL models across 17 tasks, environments, and experimental settings. RADT generalizes in a zero-shot manner to out-of-distribution avoid-region sizes and counts, outperforming baselines that require retraining. In one such zero-shot setting, RADT achieves 35.7% improvement in normalized cost over the best retrained baseline while maintaining high goal-reaching success. We also apply RADT to cell reprogramming in biology, demonstrating its versatility.|[2505.19337](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19337)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Goal-Oriented Sequential Bayesian Experimental Design for Causal Learning**|Zheyu Zhang (University of Michigan) et.al.|We present GO-CBED, a goal-oriented Bayesian framework for sequential causal experimental design. Unlike conventional approaches that select interventions aimed at inferring the full causal model, GO-CBED directly maximizes the expected information gain (EIG) on user-specified causal quantities of interest, enabling more targeted and efficient experimentation. The framework is both non-myopic, optimizing over entire intervention sequences, and goal-oriented, targeting only model aspects relevant to the causal query. To address the intractability of exact EIG computation, we introduce a variational lower bound estimator, optimized jointly through a transformer-based policy network and normalizing flow-based variational posteriors. The resulting policy enables real-time decision-making via an amortized network. We demonstrate that GO-CBED consistently outperforms existing baselines across various causal reasoning and discovery tasks-including synthetic structural causal models and semi-synthetic gene regulatory networks-particularly in settings with limited experimental budgets and complex causal mechanisms. Our results highlight the benefits of aligning experimental design objectives with specific research goals and of forward-looking sequential planning.|[2507.07359](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.07359)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Scalable Source Selection for Machine Learning Tasks**|Ambarish Singh et.al.|Given a query table, existing data lake discovery techniques seek to identify data sources related to the query table through schema similarity and joinability. The discovered sources, however, are not always useful for downstream analyses; while some improve task generalization, some have the potential to inadvertently introduce harmful noise or distribution shifts. We formalize source selection as the problem of finding a subset of the discovered sources that maximizes downstream task utility. We propose Splice, a target- and cost-aware source selection system that adopts the idea of gene splicing to identify sources which provide additional signals that the union of all sources misses. To speed up the process, we develop two surrogate variants, Splice-DP and Splice-Grad, that estimate the marginal contribution of subsets to task utility by leveraging data profiles in a meta-learning approach and gradient alignment, respectively. On classification tasks over two real-world benchmarks derived from SOTA table union search tools, Splice shows 3-5 percentage points improvement in binary F1 scores compared to the case when all discovered tables are considered. On five additional real-world ML tasks spanning classification and regression, Splice improves task utility over the all-sources baseline by 1.8-13.7 points, while Splice-DP and Splice-Grad match this quality with 3-7x fewer model evaluations (up to 77x at scale).|[2507.22186](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.22186)|
|**2026-11-05**|**When Reviews Are Scarce: Structure-Aware Review Imputation for Recommendation in Realistic Sparse Review Settings**|Leyao Wang et.al.|Textual reviews enrich recommender systems with fine-grained preference signals and enhanced explainability. However, in real-world scenarios, users rarely leave reviews, resulting in severe sparsity that undermines the effectiveness of review-aware recommenders under partial observability. A natural remedy is to synthesize the missing reviews to restore the textual signal these recommenders rely on. Yet, conventional imputation techniques, such as matrix completion and LLM-based augmentation, either lose contextualized semantics by embedding texts into vectors or overlook structural dependencies on the user-item graph. To address these shortcomings, we propose TWISTER (ToWards Imputation on Sparsity with Textual Edge Graph Representation), a unified framework for sparse-text recommendation that synthesizes missing reviews by jointly modeling semantic and structural signals. Specifically, we represent user-item interactions as a Textual-Edge Graph (TEG), treating reviews as edge attributes. To capture relational context, we construct line-graph views and employ an LLM as a graph-aware aggregator. For each interaction lacking a textual review, our model aggregates natural-language representations from neighboring interactions to generate a coherent and personalized review. Experiments on multiple datasets show that TWISTER consistently outperforms traditional numeric, graph-based, and LLM baselines, delivering higher-quality imputed reviews and, more importantly, improved downstream recommendation. In summary, TWISTER is a drop-in, structure-aware imputer that recovers the textual signal that review-aware recommenders need when reviews are scarce.|[2508.01128](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.01128)|
|**2026-11-05**|**DUP: Detection-guided Unlearning for Backdoor Purification in Language Models**|Man Hu et.al.|As backdoor attacks become more stealthy and robust, they reveal critical weaknesses in current defense strategies: detection methods often rely on coarse-grained feature statistics, and purification methods typically require full retraining or additional clean models. To address these challenges, we propose DUP (Detection-guided Unlearning for Purification), a unified framework that integrates backdoor detection with unlearning-based purification. The detector captures feature-level anomalies by jointly leveraging class-agnostic distances and inter-layer transitions. These deviations are integrated through a weighted scheme to identify poisoned inputs, enabling more fine-grained analysis. Based on the detection results, we purify the model through a parameter-efficient unlearning mechanism that avoids full retraining and does not require any external clean model. Specifically, we innovatively repurpose knowledge distillation to guide the student model toward increasing its output divergence from the teacher on detected poisoned samples, effectively forcing it to unlearn the backdoor behavior. Extensive experiments across diverse attack methods and language model architectures demonstrate that DUP achieves superior defense performance in detection accuracy and purification efficacy. Our code is available at https://github.com/ManHu2025/DUP.|[2508.01647](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.01647)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Benchmarking Pretrained Molecular Embedding Models For Molecular Representation Learning**|Mateusz Praski et.al.|Pretrained molecular embedding models spanning graph neural networks, text transformers, and hybrid architectures have been widely used in drug discovery, yet no large-scale, rigorous comparison of their representations existed. We evaluated 28 models across 25 classification datasets under a controlled framework using hierarchical Bayesian Bradley-Terry statistical testing. All neural models except CheMeleon failed to outperform the classical ECFP count fingerprint; CheMeleon was the only model rated as better. However, further testing on external validation datasets, based on scaffold cross-validation and a Bayesian hierarchical correlated t-test, revealed no definite advantage of either embedding method. As a practical takeaway, we recommend evaluating both ECFP fingerprints and CheMeleon embeddings in novel QSAR/QSPR studies. Overall, in the context of all neural networks evaluated, these results also challenge the perceived progress in molecular representation learning and highlight the need for rigorous evaluation standards in the field.|[2508.06199](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.06199)|
|**2026-11-05**|**A Financial Brain Scan of the LLM**|Hui Chen et.al.|We use sparse autoencoders (SAEs) to "brain scan" large language models, mapping internal activations to interpretable concepts and enabling targeted "steering" of specific features. We show that prompting models to adopt specific preferences activates many unintended concepts, complicating causal inference. In contrast, SAEs permit smooth, more isolated steering of target concepts. Furthermore, our scans reveal a stark disconnect between the model's internal feature contributions and its self-generated explanations, showing that these explanations are unreliable guides to its reasoning. Finally, SAEs provide a lightweight intervention for existing models, producing interpretable embeddings and allowing researchers to attenuate specific economic biases.|[2508.21285](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.21285)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Population-Aligned Persona Generation for LLM-based Social Simulation**|Zhengyu Hu et.al.|Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have enabled large-scale, high-fidelity social simulations, creating new opportunities for computational social science. However, constructing persona sets that faithfully reflect real-world population diversity remains a key challenge. Existing studies often emphasize agentic frameworks and simulation environments, while paying less attention to persona generation and the biases introduced by unrepresentative persona sets. In this paper, we propose a systematic framework for synthesizing high-quality, population-aligned persona sets for LLM-driven social simulation. Our approach begins by leveraging LLMs to generate narrative personas from long-term social media data, followed by rigorous quality assessment to filter out low-fidelity profiles. We then apply importance sampling to achieve global alignment with reference psychometric distributions, such as the Big Five personality traits. To address the needs of specific simulation contexts, we further introduce a task-specific module that adapts the globally aligned persona set to targeted subpopulations. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method significantly reduces population-level bias and enables accurate, flexible social simulation for a wide range of research and policy applications.|[2509.10127](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.10127)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Discovering Phase Space Structure in Learned Hamiltonian Systems**|Yulong Yang et.al.|Mechanics-inspired neural networks provide physically structured models of dynamical systems, but typically assume access to the coordinates in which the underlying dynamics are expressed. While recent generative approaches relax this assumption, they remain limited in their ability to jointly model different dynamical regimes and discover interpretable latent structure. We introduce Structured Phase Space GAN (SPS-GAN), a conditional generative framework that learns mechanical dynamics and discovers latent phase-space structure from Cartesian trajectories or video observations. We achieve this by combining adversarial generation with port-Hamiltonian latent dynamics and extending cyclic-coordinate regularization to account for dissipation and forcing. This formulation supports conservative, dissipative, and forced systems while encouraging compact latent representations from which active degrees of freedom can be identified. Experiments on simulated and real-world systems demonstrate improved trajectory accuracy and video generation quality over competitive baselines, while identifying active latent dimensions consistent with the expected effective degrees of freedom and recovering physically interpretable coordinates across observation modalities and dynamical regimes.|[2509.23003](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.23003)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Rethinking Reasoning: A Survey on Reasoning-based Backdoors in LLMs**|Man Hu et.al.|With the rise of advanced reasoning capabilities, large language models (LLMs) are receiving increasing attention. However, although reasoning improves LLMs' performance on downstream tasks, it also introduces new security risks, as adversaries can exploit these capabilities to conduct backdoor attacks. Existing surveys on backdoor attacks and reasoning security offer comprehensive overviews but lack in-depth analysis of backdoor attacks and defenses targeting LLMs' reasoning abilities. In this paper, we take the first step toward providing a comprehensive review of reasoning-based backdoor attacks in LLMs by analyzing their underlying mechanisms, methodological frameworks, and unresolved challenges. Specifically, we introduce a new taxonomy that offers a unified perspective for summarizing existing approaches, categorizing reasoning-based backdoor attacks into associative, passive, and active. We also present defense strategies against such attacks and discuss current challenges alongside potential directions for future research. This work offers a novel perspective, paving the way for further exploration of secure and trustworthy LLM communities.|[2510.07697](http://arxiv.org/abs/2510.07697)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Learning Soft Robotic Dynamics with Active Exploration**|Hehui Zheng et.al.|Soft robots offer adaptability and safety in unstructured environments, yet their compliant, high-dimensional, and nonlinear dynamics make modeling for control difficult. Existing data-driven approaches often fail to generalize, constrained by narrowly focused task demonstrations or inefficient random exploration. We introduce an uncertainty-aware active exploration framework that autonomously learns task-agnostic and generalizable dynamics models of soft robotic systems. We evaluate this framework on three simulated platforms---a continuum arm, an articulated fish in fluid, and a musculoskeletal leg---and on a real-world pneumatically actuated continuum arm. Compared with random exploration and task-specific baseline, our method achieves higher modeling accuracy by actively collecting informative data. This improved model quality yields higher returns in zero-shot control on unseen tasks and robust performance under sensing noise, actuation delays, and material nonlinearities. These results demonstrate that uncertainty-driven active exploration enables data-efficient dynamics learning across soft robotic morphologies, supporting autonomous and adaptable control for compliant robots.|[2510.27428](http://arxiv.org/abs/2510.27428)|
|**2026-11-05**|**AEGIS: Preserving Privacy of 3D Facial Avatars with Adversarial Perturbations**|Dawid Wolkiewicz et.al.|The growing adoption of photorealistic 3D facial avatars, particularly those utilizing efficient 3D Gaussian Splatting representations, introduces new risks of online identity theft, especially in systems that rely on biometric authentication. While effective adversarial masking methods have been developed for 2D images, a significant gap remains in achieving robust, viewpoint-consistent identity protection for dynamic 3D avatars. To address this, we present AEGIS, the first privacy-preserving identity masking framework for 3D Gaussian avatars, concealing identity from automated face recognition while preserving the avatar's perceptual realism and animation usability. AEGIS applies adversarial perturbations to the base (DC) color coefficients of the Gaussian primitives, guided by an ensemble of pre-trained face verification networks, embedding the protection directly in the 3D representation so that it applies across viewpoints and animations, without retraining or modifying the avatar's geometry. Evaluated against black-box recognizers, AEGIS reduces face retrieval and verification accuracy to near-zero while maintaining high perceptual fidelity. It also preserves key facial attributes such as age, gender, and emotion, demonstrating strong privacy protection with minimal visual distortion. We further introduce a viewpoint-robust leakage metric that quantifies residual identity leakage across an avatar's rendered viewpoints, which single-view evaluation cannot capture.|[2511.17747](http://arxiv.org/abs/2511.17747)|
|**2026-11-05**|**PointRAFT: 3D deep learning for high-throughput prediction of potato tuber weight from partial point clouds**|Pieter M. Blok et.al.|Key phenotypic traits, such as crop size, volume, and weight, can be estimated non-destructively from point clouds. In practice, however, point clouds of crops are typically incomplete due to self-occlusion, leading to systematic errors when estimating these traits. To overcome this limitation, crop traits can be inferred directly from incomplete point clouds using deep learning-based regression networks. These networks, however, may perform suboptimally, because their predictions do not incorporate prior geometric knowledge of the crop. Such geometric knowledge is often available in structured settings, such as on a potato harvester, where the fixed distance between the camera and the conveyor belt allows tuber height to be derived from the point cloud. To exploit this geometric cue, we introduce PointRAFT, a high-throughput point cloud regression network that incorporates object height through a dedicated height embedding. We hypothesized that this height embedding improves tuber weight estimation from partial point clouds. To test this hypothesis, we collected 26,688 point clouds of 859 potato tubers on an operational harvester, spanning four cultivars and three growing seasons. On a test set of 5,254 point clouds representing 172 tubers, PointRAFT achieved a mean absolute error (MAE) of 11.7 g and a root mean squared error (RMSE) of 17.1 g, outperforming six point cloud regression networks without a height embedding. Requiring only 6.3 ms per point cloud for preprocessing and inference, PointRAFT is fast enough for high-throughput weight estimation on a commercial potato harvester. Beyond potato weight estimation, PointRAFT provides a versatile regression network applicable to a wide range of 3D phenotyping and robotic perception tasks. The code, network weights, and a subset of the dataset are publicly available at https://github.com/pieterblok/pointraft.|[2512.24193](http://arxiv.org/abs/2512.24193)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Utilizing Earth Foundation Models to Enhance the Simulation Performance of Hydrological Models with AlphaEarth Embeddings**|Pengfei Qu et.al.|Accurate prediction in ungauged basins (PUB) requires effective transfer across hydrologically diverse regions. While deep learning has advanced large-sample hydrological modeling, its cross-regional generalization depends in part on the static basin descriptors used to guide model behavior. Earth Foundation Models (EFMs) provide a promising alternative by learning environmental representations from multi-modal satellite data. In this study, we evaluate the 64-dimensional AlphaEarth Foundations (AEF) embeddings as a new form of static basin representation and assess their value for streamflow prediction under both in-sample (IS) and out-of-sample (OOS) settings using the CAMELS dataset. Results show that AEF embeddings offer higher OOS robustness than CAMELS attributes, despite nearly identical IS performance. Mutual-information analysis reveals statistical associations between AEF embeddings and conventional catchment attributes related to terrain, vegetation, and rooting characteristics. We further investigate similarity-based donor-basin selection for PUB using CAMELS attributes and AEF embeddings. Across 100 target basins, AEF static inputs generally improved PUB performance. With AEF inputs, similarity-based donor selection outperformed random selection for smaller donor pools, but its advantage diminished as pool size increased. Adding more donor basins did not consistently improve performance. Leave-one-cluster-out experiments showed AEF input benefits depended on basin partitioning, suggesting that transferability remains sensitive to the catchment conditions represented during training. Overall, this study demonstrates that Earth Foundation Model embeddings provide a transferable representation for PUB prediction and underscores the importance of basin representation quality and donor pool design, motivating future research toward unified hydrological foundation models.|[2601.01558](http://arxiv.org/abs/2601.01558)|
|**2026-11-05**|**COL-Trees: Efficient Hierarchical Object Search in Spatial Networks**|Tenindra Abeywickrama et.al.|Many spatial network applications, such as map-based services, rely heavily on efficient methods to search for relevant nearby points-of-interest (POIs). A $k$ Nearest Neighbor ($k$NN) query is one such example that finds the $k$ closest POIs from an agent's location. While most existing techniques for spatial networks focus on retrieving nearby POIs for a single agent, these search heuristics do not translate to many other applications. For example, Aggregate $k$ Nearest Neighbor (A$k$NN) queries require POIs that are close to multiple agents. $k$ Farthest Neighbors ($k$FN) queries require POIs that are the antithesis of nearest. Such problems naturally benefit from a hierarchical approach, but existing methods rely on Euclidean-based heuristics, which have diminished effectiveness in spatial networks such as road network graphs. We propose a novel data structure, COL-Tree (Compacted Object-Landmark Tree), to address this gap by enabling efficient hierarchical graph traversal using a more accurate landmark-based heuristic. We then present query algorithms that utilize COL-Trees to efficiently answer A$k$NN, $k$FN, and other queries. Our experiments evaluate performance on real-world and synthetic datasets including multiple types of spatial networks, such as road networks of varying sizes and edge weights, terrain networks, and 2D physics meshes. The results demonstrate that our techniques significantly outperform existing approaches, achieving up to three orders of magnitude improvement. Moreover, this comes at a small pre-processing overhead in both theory and practice.|[2601.22183](http://arxiv.org/abs/2601.22183)|
|**2026-11-05**|**OpInf-LLM: Parametric PDE Solving with LLMs via Operator Inference**|Zhuoyuan Wang et.al.|Solving diverse partial differential equations (PDEs) is fundamental in science and engineering. Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong capabilities in code generation, symbolic reasoning, and tool use, but reliably solving PDEs across heterogeneous settings remains challenging. Prior work on LLM-based code generation and transformer-based foundation models for PDE learning has shown promising advances. However, a persistent trade-off between execution success rate and numerical accuracy arises, particularly when generalization to unseen parameters and boundary conditions is required. In this work, we propose OpInf-LLM, an LLM parametric PDE solving framework via operator inference. The proposed framework leverages small amounts of solution data to enable accurate prediction of diverse PDE instances, including unseen parameters and configurations, and provides seamless integration with LLMs for natural language task specification and physics-based reasoning of proper feature parameterization. Its low computational demands and unified solution pipeline further enable a high execution success rate across heterogeneous settings, opening new possibilities for generalizable reduced-order modeling in LLM-based PDE solving.|[2602.01493](http://arxiv.org/abs/2602.01493)|
|**2026-11-05**|**CompilerKV: Risk-Adaptive KV Compression via Offline Experience Compilation**|Ning Yang et.al.|Prefill-only KV compression freezes a token subset at the end of prefill and decodes from it without further eviction. The retention decision is therefore irreversible, yet existing methods estimate the corrective signals it relies on, per-head reliability and prompt-level compression sensitivity, online from a single noisy prompt. We argue this is the wrong statistical unit: these signals exhibit far higher cross-prompt regularity than within-prompt signal-to-noise. We introduce \textsc{CompilerKV}, a KV-retention policy whose corrective tables are compiled offline from a calibration corpus, reducing online correction after the standard observation-window scan to $O(1)$ lookups plus a budget clamp. We find that compiled retention tables behave as portable architectural priors: rankings transfer across disjoint corpora on four backbones (mean Spearman $\bar\rho{=}0.90$), and direct model-to-model table transfer costs only $0.4$--$0.8$ LongBench points on average. At a 512-token budget, \textsc{CompilerKV} attains compressed-SOTA on all four backbones, improving over the strongest prefill-only baseline by $+1.67$ points on average (task-bootstrap 95\% CI $[+1.08,+2.37]$). Pressure regimes amplify the gap: under a fixed $512/32k$ cache ratio, \textsc{CompilerKV} remains the strongest compressed method through 128k RULER ($\sim\!73$ vs.\ FullKV $\sim\!79$, SnapKV $\sim\!38$); on 32k NIAH it reaches $0.89$ vs.\ SnapKV $0.42$; and at 32k input, retaining only $1.56\%$ of the prefill KV, batch-16 serving remains feasible where FullKV is OOM.|[2602.08686](http://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08686)|
|**2026-11-05**|**A Lightweight Multi-View Approach to Short-Term Load Forecasting**|Julien Guit\'e-Vinet et.al.|Time series forecasting is a critical task across domains such as energy, finance, and meteorology, where accurate predictions enable informed decision-making. While transformer-based and large-parameter models have recently achieved state-of-the-art results, their complexity can lead to overfitting and unstable forecasts, especially when older data points become less relevant. In this paper, we propose a lightweight multi-view approach to short-term load forecasting that leverages single-value embeddings and a scaled time-range input to capture temporally relevant features efficiently. We introduce an embedding dropout mechanism to prevent over-reliance on specific features and enhance interpretability. Our method achieves competitive performance with significantly fewer parameters, demonstrating robustness across multiple datasets, including scenarios with noisy or sparse data, and provides insights into the contributions of individual features to the forecast.|[2602.09220](http://arxiv.org/abs/2602.09220)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Disentangle to create: Architecture-level disentangled diffusion model for few-shot Chinese font generation**|Jie Li et.al.|Few-shot Chinese font generation aims to synthesize new characters in a target style using only a handful of reference images. Achieving accurate content rendering and faithful style transfer requires effective disentanglement between content and style. However, most existing approaches achieve only feature-level disentanglement and allow the generator to re-entangle these features, leading to content distortion and degraded style fidelity. To address this, we propose the Architecture-Level Disentangled Diffusion Model (ALD-Font) with an asymmetric design. The U-Net backbone takes SimSun-style glyphs as content templates, concatenated with noisy latents to construct a spatial canvas. Style features extracted from target-style images are injected as modulation signals to guide generation. Based on theoretical validation of disentanglement effectiveness, we adopt a parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) strategy that updates only the style-related modules. This allows the model to better adapt to new styles while avoiding overfitting to the reference images' content under the few-shot setting. Additionally, we train a Background Noise Removal (BNR) module in the pixel space to remove background noise in complex stroke regions. We further introduce the Grey and OCR metrics to evaluate the content quality of generated characters. Experimental results show that ALD-Font achieves significantly higher style fidelity while maintaining comparable content accuracy to existing state-of-the-art methods. The implementation is available at https://github.com/JayLee1002/ALD-Font.|[2602.18874](http://arxiv.org/abs/2602.18874)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Golden Layers and Where to Find Them: Improved Knowledge Editing for Large Language Models Via Layer Gradient Analysis**|Shrestha Datta et.al.|Knowledge editing in Large Language Models (LLMs) aims to update the model's prediction for a specific query to a desired target while preserving its behavior on all other inputs. This process typically involves two stages: identifying the layer to edit and performing the parameter update. Intuitively, different queries may localize knowledge at different depths of the model, resulting in different sample-wise editing performance for a fixed editing layer. In this work, we hypothesize the existence of fixed golden layers that can achieve near-optimal editing performance similar to sample-wise optimal layers. To validate this hypothesis, we provide empirical evidence by comparing golden layers against ground-truth sample-wise optimal layers. Furthermore, we show that golden layers can be reliably identified using a proxy dataset and generalize effectively to unseen test set queries across datasets. Finally, we propose a novel method, namely Layer Gradient Analysis (LGA) that estimates golden layers efficiently via gradient-attribution, avoiding extensive trial-and-error across multiple editing runs. Extensive experiments on several benchmark datasets demonstrate the effectiveness and robustness of our LGA approach across different LLM types and various knowledge editing methods.|[2602.20207](http://arxiv.org/abs/2602.20207)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Same Words, Different Judgments: How Preferences Vary Across Modalities**|Aaron Broukhim et.al.|Preference-based reinforcement learning (PbRL) is the dominant framework for aligning AI systems to human preferences. However, evaluation protocols for such data were designed for text and have not been validated for speech. We present the first intraclass correlation coefficient (ICC)-based, controlled cross-modal study of human and synthetic preference annotations, comparing text and audio evaluations of identical semantic content across 100 prompts. We show that achieving \textit{good} (audio) to \textit{near good} (text) agreement within either modality (ICC(2,$k$) $\approx$ .81 audio, .74 text) requires $\sim$9 raters and modalities show marked differences in how people report preferences: audio raters exhibit narrower decision thresholds, reduced length bias, and more user-oriented evaluation criteria, with near-chance cross-modality agreement. We demonstrate that synthetic ratings can be used to effectively predict inter-rater agreement, thus serving as an early signal for stimulus selection and proxy for human annotations. Together, these findings argue that evaluation protocols for audio preference data require modality-specific design rather than direct adaptation from text. Code, data, and the audio corpus are available at: https://huggingface.co/datasets/sciminds/same-words-different-judgements|[2602.22710](http://arxiv.org/abs/2602.22710)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Farther the Shift, Sparser the Representation: Analyzing OOD Mechanisms in LLMs**|Mingyu Jin et.al.|In this work, we investigate how the internal representations of Large Language Models (LLMs) change with inputs of increasing difficulty. Although sparsity changes may not be obvious for individual samples, dataset-level results reveal a consistent statistical trend: as task difficulty increases (e.g., harder questions, longer contexts, or more answer choices), the last hidden states of LLMs become systematically sparser. In short, \textbf{the harder the task, the sparser the representation}. This sparsity--difficulty relation is observable across diverse models and domains, suggesting that harder inputs drive more concentrated activation patterns in the last hidden state. Through a series of controlled analyses and learning-dynamics experiments, we show that representational density is a learned property of data familiarity: models develop rich, distributed representations for mastered patterns, while unfamiliar inputs default to sparser activations. Our finding is also actionable. We illustrate this with Sparsity-Guided Curriculum In-Context Learning (SG-ICL), which uses sparsity to select few-shot demonstrations matched to the query's difficulty, outperforming standard CoT and Auto-CoT baselines on MATH-500. Our study provides new insights into how LLM representations reflect task difficulty and how this signal can be leveraged for inference. The source code is available at the URL: https://github.com/MingyuJ666/sparsityLLM.|[2603.03415](http://arxiv.org/abs/2603.03415)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Faces in Things, Faces in Nothing: Probing Face Selectivity in Vision Models with Pareidolia**|Qianpu Chen et.al.|An electrical outlet can look like a face without depicting a person. Face pareidolia is thus a revealing probe of how vision models turn ambiguous visual evidence into category judgments. When a model calls such an object Human, does that answer depend on its face-like arrangement? Using the FacesInThings dataset, we test six vision models, from a vision-only ViT to CLIP and generative vision-language models, with face detectors as a reference. Each face-like region is paired with three controls from the same photograph that lack the arrangement: a background crop, the region with its tiles shuffled, and its average color. A Human answer driven by the arrangement should appear less often on these controls. We also vary how the categories are described. Three findings emerge. First, models respond to illusory faces, but not as people do: face detectors rarely return a face for these regions, yet CLIP ViT-L/14 and two generative models label roughly half of them Human when the categories are described as kinds of face. Annotators, by contrast, read these faces as non-human, such as animal or cartoon faces. For these models, a face is a human face by default. Second, similar Human rates hide opposite behavior. LLaVA-1.5 answers Human less often once the arrangement is removed; Qwen2-VL calls every average-color patch Human and describes a person in each; CLIP ViT-B/32 calls shuffled regions Human more often than real faces; SmolVLM rarely answers Human, but mostly for face-like regions. Third, rewording the categories changes whether Human answers favor the face at all, removing this preference in LLaVA-1.5 and creating it in CLIP. A Human answer is not evidence of a face: vision models can keep giving it after the face is gone.|[2603.03989](http://arxiv.org/abs/2603.03989)|
|**2026-11-05**|**PREBA: Surgical Duration Prediction via PCA-Weighted Retrieval-Augmented LLMs and Bayesian Averaging Aggregation**|Wanyin Wu et.al.|Accurate prediction of surgical duration is pivotal for hospital resource management. Although recent supervised learning approaches, from machine learning (ML) to fine-tuned large language models (LLMs), have shown strong performance, they remain constrained by the need for high-quality labeled data and computationally intensive training. In contrast, zero-shot LLM inference offers a promising training-free alternative, but it lacks grounding in institution-specific clinical evidence, making its predictions clinically misaligned and prone to instability. To address these limitations, we present PREBA, a retrieval-augmented framework that integrates PCA-weighted retrieval and Bayesian averaging to ground LLM reasoning in institution-specific evidence without requiring task-specific fine-tuning. Specifically, PREBA first encodes heterogeneous clinical features into a unified representation. It then performs PCA-weighted retrieval, weighting each dimension by its contribution to the variation across institutional cases, instead of treating them as equally informative, and conditions the LLM to generate multiple candidate predictions. Finally, Bayesian averaging consolidates these predictions with population-level statistical priors, yielding a calibrated estimate that remains stable and clinically plausible. We evaluate PREBA on four real-world datasets spanning different institutions and languages, using three state-of-the-art LLMs, including Qwen3, DeepSeek-R1, and HuatuoGPT-o1. PREBA consistently outperforms zero-shot inference, reducing MAE by up to 64.9% and raising R2 to as high as 0.63. It further attains accuracy competitive with, and on several datasets superior to, fully supervised ML methods, while offering greater interpretability through retrieved exemplars and explicit priors. The code is available at https://github.com/kanxueli/PREBA.|[2603.13275](http://arxiv.org/abs/2603.13275)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Manifold-Matching Autoencoders: Embedding Data into Chosen Geometric Structures**|Laurent Cheret et.al.|Manipulating the structure of data in the latent space of an autoencoder is a long-standing goal of representation learning. We propose a simple regularization, Manifold Matching, that imposes an explicit geometric structure on that latent space. The idea is to take a reference representation R of the data, which need not be the raw input X fed to the network, and to minimize, batch-wise, the mean squared error between the pairwise distances in R and those in the latent space Z. Although the autoencoder still learns to reconstruct X, the shape of its latent space comes to mimic that of R. The objective requires no labels, only reference distances, and because those are scalars, the dimensionality of R is decoupled from that of Z: any reference can guide any bottleneck. Using this single instrument we study how different geometric structures can be imposed on a latent space, comparing against autoencoders built on geometric or topological regularizers. Across fifteen datasets under a shared protocol with statistical testing, the resulting Manifold-Matching Autoencoder (MMAE) matches the global-structure quality of persistence-based autoencoders, statistically tying RTD-AE on distance correlation and triplet accuracy, and stays competitive on the local and topological metrics that other methods lead, at the computational cost of a plain autoencoder: it uses only a batched distance loss, with no persistence computation. We frame MMAE not as a universal winner but as an efficient and controllable regularizer whose reference selects which structure the latent inherits, and which turns any fixed embedding into a reusable out-of-sample encoder. All experiments use a two-dimensional bottleneck.|[2603.16568](http://arxiv.org/abs/2603.16568)|
|**2026-11-05**|**TherapyGym: Evaluating and Aligning Clinical Fidelity and Safety in Therapy Chatbots**|Fangrui Huang et.al.|Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for mental-health support; yet prevailing evaluation methods--fluency metrics, preference tests, and generic dialogue benchmarks--fail to capture the clinically critical dimensions of psychotherapy. We introduce THERAPYGYM, a framework that evaluates and improves therapy chatbots along two clinical pillars: fidelity and safety. Fidelity is measured using the Cognitive Therapy Rating Scale (CTRS), implemented as an automated pipeline that scores adherence to CBT techniques over multi-turn sessions. Safety is assessed using a multi-label annotation scheme, covering therapy-specific risks (e.g., failing to address harm or abuse). To mitigate bias and unreliability in LLM-based judges, we further release THERAPYJUDGEBENCH, a validation set of 116 dialogues with 1,270 expert ratings for auditing and calibration against licensed clinicians. THERAPYGYM also serves as a training harness: CTRS and safety-based rewards drive RL with configurable patient simulations spanning diverse symptom profiles. Models trained in THERAPYGYM improve on expert ratings, with average CTRS rising from 0.10 to 0.60 (and 0.16 to 0.59 under LLM judges). Our work enables scalable development of therapy chatbots that are faithful to evidence-based practice and safer in high-stakes use.|[2603.18008](http://arxiv.org/abs/2603.18008)|
|**2026-11-05**|**MemReward: Graph-Based Experience Memory for LLM Reward Prediction with Limited Labels**|Tianyang Luo et.al.|Reinforcement learning (RL) fine-tuning improves large language model (LLM) reasoning using reward signals on sampled rollouts. When reward labels are available for only a subset of rollouts, restricting policy updates to that subset leaves the remaining rollouts unused for policy optimization. Semi-supervised learning propagates labels from labeled to unlabeled samples, but existing methods either diffuse labels with fixed similarity weights or treat each sample independently, ignoring the relations among queries, thinking processes, and answers. We propose MemReward, a graph-based experience memory that propagates rewards to unlabeled rollouts during online policy optimization. MemReward stores queries and the rollouts (thinking processes and final answers) of an initial LLM policy as nodes of a heterogeneous graph connected by similarity and structural edges, and warms up a GNN on labeled rollouts to propagate rewards over this graph. During online RL fine-tuning, unlabeled rollouts are attached to the graph by query similarity, and the policy is updated with ground-truth rewards on labeled rollouts and GNN-predicted rewards on the rest. We evaluate MemReward on 13 benchmarks in mathematics, question answering, and code generation with verifiable rewards, and further with LLM-judge, human-preference, and continuous rewards. With ground-truth rewards on only 20% of rollouts, MemReward achieves 96.6% of the performance of fully supervised training (Oracle) on Qwen2.5-1.5B and 97.3% on Qwen2.5-3B, where it also outperforms label propagation and FixMatch-style self-training. It carries over to Llama backbones up to 8B and closely approaches Oracle on out-of-domain tasks.|[2603.19310](http://arxiv.org/abs/2603.19310)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Does Explanation Correctness Matter? Linking Computational XAI Evaluation to Human Understanding**|Gregor Baer et.al.|Explainable AI (XAI) methods are commonly evaluated using functional correctness metrics, also called faithfulness or fidelity, which estimate how closely an explanation reflects the model's reasoning. Higher correctness is assumed to produce better human understanding, but this link has not been tested with controlled levels. We conducted a user study (N=200) that manipulated explanation correctness at four levels (100%, 85%, 70%, 55%) in a synthetic time series classification task where participants could not rely on domain knowledge or visual intuition. Explanations were constructed to resemble feature attributions, with correctness defined against a known ground truth rather than estimated from a trained model. Participants predicted a simulated AI's decisions from these explanations (forward simulation), and we used their forward simulation accuracy as a proxy for understanding. Correctness affected forward simulation accuracy, but not at every level: accuracy dropped at 70% and 55% relative to 100%, with no further reduction below 70% and no conclusive difference between 85% and 100%. Lower correctness reduced how many participants predicted accurately rather than lowering accuracy uniformly, and even with fully correct explanations, only a subset of participants predicted well above chance. In exploratory analyses, some participants predicted decisions accurately but described the wrong pattern when asked what the AI used, and self-reported ratings correlated with forward simulation accuracy only when explanations were fully correct. These findings suggest that, for feature-attribution-style explanations, not all differences in functional correctness translate to differences in human understanding, highlighting the need to validate functional metrics against human outcomes.|[2603.25251](http://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25251)|
|**2026-11-05**|**AromaGen: Interactive Generation of Rich Olfactory Experiences with Multimodal Language Models**|Awu Chen et.al.|Smell's connections with food, memory, and social experience have motivated researchers to bring olfaction into interactive systems. Multimodal AI opens new possibilities for generating smells from natural language, yet it remains unclear whether pretrained models encode sufficient olfactory knowledge to translate language into perceptible compositions. We present AromaGen, an AI-powered 12-channel wearable olfactory system that maps free-form natural-language descriptions to 12 base odorants selected to cover a semantically derived olfactory space, while supporting iterative refinement through natural-language feedback. In a between-subjects study (N=60) across 50 real-world benchmark smells, participants distinguished AromaGen-generated target smells above chance in a three-alternative forced-choice task, with retrieval-augmented AI composition performing comparably to human-expert composition and zero-shot AI composition. Natural-language feedback significantly improved the perceived similarity of both AI- and human-expert compositions. Our findings demonstrate the feasibility of using pretrained multimodal AI, with human aroma composition data, to generate perceptible olfactory experiences from natural language.|[2604.01650](http://arxiv.org/abs/2604.01650)|
|**2026-11-05**|**ReMoA: Recursive Modular Adaptation via Sparse Latent Refinement in Multimodal Diffusion Models**|Yuwei Sun et.al.|Diffusion models have achieved success in high-fidelity data synthesis, yet their capacity for more complex, structured reasoning such as text following remains constrained. While advances in language models have leveraged strategies such as latent reasoning and recursion to enhance text understanding capabilities, extending these to multimodal text-to-image generation tasks is challenging due to the continuous and non-discrete nature of visual tokens. To tackle this problem, we draw inspiration from modular human cognition and propose a Recursive Modular Adaptation (ReMoA) framework integrated into joint attention layers of conventional diffusion models, iteratively refining visual tokens over multiple recursion steps while efficiently sharing parameters via sparse selection of neural modules. Comprehensive evaluations on both class-conditioned ImageNet generation and text-to-image generation using the GenEval and DPG benchmarks demonstrate that recursive visual latent refinement contributes to richer object details and better-aligned outputs. Additionally, visual latent trajectory and routing analyses reveal that neural modules learn to specialize across diffusion timesteps, with each module's trajectories diverging at later, low-noise steps to support context-adaptive refinement. ReMoA increases recall for DiT-XL from 0.57 to 0.62 on ImageNet, and improves the overall GenEval score for SD3-medium from 68.33% to 71.18%, without introducing additional parameters through recursion.|[2604.25299](http://arxiv.org/abs/2604.25299)|
|**2026-11-05**|**In-Context Black-Box Optimization with Unreliable Feedback**|Nicolas Samuel Blumer et.al.|Black-box optimization in science and engineering often comes with side information: experts, simulators, pretrained predictors, or heuristics can suggest promising candidates. Such feedback can accelerate search, but can also be biased, input-dependent, or misleading. Feedback-aware Bayesian optimization methods typically handle tasks independently, limiting adaptation across feedback sources. In-context optimizers enable cross-task adaptation, but usually rely only on optimization history at test time. We study feedback-informed in-context black-box optimization (FICBO), where a pretrained optimizer conditions on both the observed history and cheap auxiliary feedback for the current candidate set. We introduce structured priors over how auxiliary feedback relates to the objective, and use them to pretrain a feedback-aware transformer across regimes ranging from highly informative to misleading. At test time, the model estimates source reliability in context by comparing observed objective values with auxiliary signals, improving query selection.
