With eight controls per decision, X-Fly reaches 74.18% SR on test-seen and 51.73% on test-unseen, compared with 16.45% and 18.43% for full-chunk BC and 32.04% and 29.46% for DreamFly.|[2610.21450](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21450)|
|**2026-11-05**|**RefCraft: Benchmarking Visual-Reference-Conditioned Agentic Generation through Web Development**|Yuchi Wang et.al.|Beyond basic tool use and coding skills, effectively incorporating visual references into agentic generation is equally essential for applying AI agents to real-world productivity tasks. We study this crucial capability through webpage development, a practical and readily evaluable setting in which existing benchmarks remain largely idealized: they typically focus on reconstructing visual targets or generating webpages from textual instructions alone, overlooking the more common workflow of developing from visual examples. Specifically, we introduce RefCraft, a benchmark comprising 300 realistic multimodal webpage-development tasks that combine natural-language instructions with screenshots from one to five reference webpages. Agents must selectively interpret and utilize these references while rendering, visually inspecting, and iteratively refining their outputs. We evaluate the resulting open-ended artifacts using independently verifiable keypoints and complementary programmatic and VLM judges. Across nine frontier (M)LLMs, the strongest system achieves an overall score of only 52.12 out of 100, revealing that visual-reference-conditioned agentic generation remains a substantial challenge for current models in practical webpage-development settings.|[2610.26826](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26826)|
|**2026-11-05**|**From Augmentation to Synthetic Evidence: Evaluating Generated Chest Radiographs at Dataset Intake**|Shaikh Akib Shahriyar et.al.|While generated chest radiographs can serve as valuable data augmentation when explicitly declared, the same pixels become unsupported evidence when submitted as records of real clinical examinations. We study this dataset-intake vulnerability by disentangling three questions: (1) whether an image passes an automated technical quality check, (2) whether an origin classifier flags it as synthetic, and (3) whether a trusted provenance record supports the claimed clinical acquisition. In this study, a submitter uses Qwen3.8-27B to select up to 20 candidate radiographs from pools of 1,000 Cheff-identified or SynthCheX images, while a receiver evaluates image quality via MedGemma 1.5 and predicts origin using a linear probe over frozen DINOv3 features. Although quality selection increases MedGemma quality passes from 12/20 to 19/20 for Cheff-identified images and from 8/20 to 17/20 for SynthCheX, we find no evidence that candidate selection increases passage through both checks simultaneously: zero selected Cheff-identified images pass both gates, and uniform, quality-guided, and surrogate-guided selection yield 2, 1, and 2 SynthCheX passes out of 20, respectively. On unprocessed test cohorts, the origin classifier rejects 4,677 of 5,000 SynthCheX images at a nominal 5% false-alarm rate (flagging 110 of 2,297 real NIH radiographs), yet leaves 152 SynthCheX images that pass both pixel-level checks. Moreover, pixel-level screening is fragile to routine distribution shifts: JPEG compression reduces SynthCheX-versus-NIH AUROC from 0.987 to 0.841, and applying the NIH-calibrated threshold to an external genuine cohort (Open-I) triggers false alarms on 568 of 957 authentic radiographs (59.4%). Because pixel-level checks can filter submissions but cannot verify clinical acquisition claims, we argue that dataset intake pipelines must record and verify source provenance independently of image pixels.|[2610.29152](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29152)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Asymmetric On-Policy Distillation Learns In-Context Exploration**|Sriyash Poddar et.al.|Sequential decision-making under partial observability is difficult because agents must explore to infer hidden, task-relevant information before acting effectively. Learning such information-gathering behaviors tabula rasa via online reinforcement learning is sample-inefficient, whereas offline imitation averages expert actions across hidden states and fails to learn exploration. In this work, we propose ASTEROID, a simple and effective student--teacher supervised learning framework that can learn test-time exploration. Although a privileged expert never needs to explore, we demonstrate that a history-conditioned student can learn to do so when trained with on-policy contexts: the student first collects on-policy context under partial observability, and then imitates the privileged expert conditioned on that context. By distilling expert actions directly conditioned on the student's on-policy history, the student learns to systematically resolve partial observability, i.e., search while uncertain and exploit once the hidden state is revealed. This enables efficient test-time exploration (similar to Bayesian posterior sampling) without incurring the impractical training-data costs of reinforcement learning. Across a diverse range of eleven different environments, including discrete exploration benchmarks, robotic manipulation, navigation, and Procgen environments, ASTEROID achieves ~2x higher returns under fixed exploration budgets, uses ~100x fewer interactions than RL baselines, and outperforms frontier models at exploration. Finally, our method transfers policies trained in simulation to vision-denied real-world robot manipulation, exhibiting efficient exploration and increasing success by +40% over standard distillation. |**2026-11-05**|**Bridging the Data Gap: Source-Informed Task-Space Transport for Few-Shot Diffusion**|Jinjian Jiang et.al.|When an object category is well represented in one domain but scarcely observed in another, adapting a diffusion model to the target can be unreliable. Yet the same source-target relationship may have been observed for other categories. We ask whether this recurring relation can be learned and reused for a new semantic task. We introduce Meta-Diff, which embeds source and target distributions in a shared low-dimensional task space, learns an affine transport map across paired training tasks, and generates from the transported source coordinate using a shared diffusion backbone. At deployment, it requires source examples and the identity of a previously learned relation, but neither target images nor target-specific optimisation. Gaussian-mixture experiments examine how transfer depends on task information retained by the encoder, relation predictability and the size of the source--target shift. On held-out CIFAR-100 validation tasks, correct-source transport achieves 13.9% lower mean sliced Wasserstein distance than wrong-source transport. In the one-target final-test comparison, it achieves 14.7% lower mean distance than target-only adaptation and 32.7% lower than full fine-tuning under validation-selected settings. These gains have clear limits: directly applying the known corruption to clean-source generations performs better on CIFAR, and requested-class identity remains weak. Office-Home shows domain alignment without reliable object identity; clinical stress tests show no resolved source-specific advantage. The results characterise when reusable task-space relations help and where their generative limitations remain.|[2610.31323](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31323)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Entity-Aware Evaluation of OCR Systems for Historical Document Transcription**|Camilo Mari\~no et.al.|Optical Character Recognition (OCR) is essential for digitizing historical archives, yet standard evaluation metrics such as Character Error Rate (CER) do not necessarily reflect whether meaningful information, such as names, dates, and identifiers, is preserved. In this work, we investigate entitylevel analysis of OCR errors as a complementary perspective for evaluating OCR quality in historical documents. Using a publicly available benchmark of 175 documents from the Berrutti Archive, augmented with 5,209 manually annotated entity mentions that will be released upon acceptance, we compare sequence-recognition OCR systems, OCR-specialized vision-language models, and general-purpose vision-language models. Beyond global transcription accuracy, we analyze entity preservation and recoverability under varying character-level edit tolerances. Our experiments show that OCR rankings depend on the evaluation criterion: while Qwen 3.6 achieves the highest entity coverage across all tested tolerances, several systems with lower exact-match coverage recover substantially when small errors are tolerated, suggesting that many of their errors correspond to localized character-level deviations rather than fundamentally incorrect entity transcriptions. Conversely, some systems remain unable to recover many entities even under relaxed matching. These findings show that OCR quality cannot be fully characterized by aggregate transcription metrics alone and highlight the importance of evaluating how OCR errors affect the preservation and recoverability of meaningful information.|[2610.33004](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33004)|
|**2026-11-05**|**SPARTA: A Sparse Ternary LLM Accelerator with Adaptive Attention and Unified Datapath for Long-Context Edge Inference**|Yanzhen Zhou et.al.|LLMs on edge devices are constrained by limited memory and bandwidth. Although 1.58-bit ternary weights greatly reduce model storage, long-context inference is increasingly bottlenecked by KV-cache traffic and attention. Existing accelerators also use separate datapaths for ternary linear layers and attention, incurring hardware and execution overhead. We present SPARTA, a sparse ternary LLM accelerator for efficient long-context edge inference. SPARTA integrates: (1) a phase-aware sparse dataflow exploiting heterogeneous sparsity across compute-bound prefill and memory-bound decode; (2) a unified compute fabric mapping ternary SpMM/SpMV and bit-serial attention onto shared processing elements to exploit zero-weight and zero-bit sparsity; and (3) an adaptive two-stage attention engine combining page-level Top-k selection to reduce K-cache accesses with token-level Top-p pruning to reduce V-cache traffic. SPARTA reduces KV traffic by 4.1-4.8x over full attention and 1.3-1.5x over a state-of-the-art sparse-attention scheme, with task-dependent quality loss. Compared with NVIDIA Jetson AGX Orin, SPARTA achieves 3.9x higher generation throughput and 8.3x higher end-to-end energy efficiency on average; versus AMD Ryzen 7 9700X, the gains are 5.0x and 81.7x. It also delivers 1.6-12.0x higher throughput, 2.0-4.2x higher energy efficiency, and 1.3-51.4x higher area efficiency than FIGLUT, TeLLMe, and MCBP.|[2610.34824](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34824)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Tactus: Tactile & Pose Sensing Glove for Natural Dexterity Data Collection**|Li Guan et.al.|Collecting naturalistic human manipulation demonstrations with accurate contact force and hand pose is essential for robot dexterous manipulation policy and foundational world model learning - yet no existing wearable platform provides both calibrated fingertip force sensing and occlusion-resilient hand tracking in a form factor that preserves natural manipulation behavior. Tactus includes a documented calibration pipeline for cross-glove consistency and per-session calibration for subject-dependent correction. To enable objective comparison, we introduce standardized benchmarking protocols for tactile force regression and hand pose estimation, and apply them to Tactus and two commercially available tactile gloves - to our knowledge the first quantitative cross-platform evaluation of this kind. Tactus resolves a minimum detectable force of 0.59 N at 95% confidence, 1.6 - 2.7x lower than the commercial baselines, and with 0.87 cross-participant consistency, while our pose tracking sustains 11.0 mm fingertip keypoint relative error during manipulation, when occlusion is heaviest. We show that these combined signals support object property estimation, slip detection, and representative recording of hand-object interaction - yielding demonstration data with the fidelity required for contact-aware manipulation policies and world models.|[2610.39076](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39076)|
|**2026-11-05**|**When Disclosure Is Compulsory: Evaluating Ride-Hailing Rebalancing Policies with Self-Interested LLM Driver Agents**|Xinyu Wang et.al.|The EU Platform Work Directive, to be transposed by December 2026, requires ride-hailing platforms to state in plain language the main parameters of their automated decisions and how a worker's behaviour affects them. Such rules are exactly what a large language model (LLM) can read, so drivers will be able to let one decide for them. We compare two main families of rebalancing policy once their rules are public: a reactive policy (R) that sends idle drivers to cover requests just lost, and an anticipatory policy (A) that plans moves on a demand forecast. Each driver is an LLM agent maximizing its own net income, given no rules (N) or the rules at the directive's level of detail (L). We test two LLMs, Claude Haiku 5.5 and Jev, which understand the rules equally well, in a three-zone model and in Manhattan. With the rules disclosed, drivers follow the platform more closely: in Manhattan, the share of relocation offers they decline falls from about four in five to one in four under both policies. Yet the effects differ by policy. For the platform, disclosure costs R 3.7% of its profit in Manhattan, as R now pays for moves drivers used to make for free, while A's profit is unchanged; the two policies, which tie on profit under full compliance, end 6.1% apart. For drivers, disclosure raises income in the small model under both policies and LLMs; for users, it raises the service rate there by 10 to 18 percentage points. In Manhattan, self-interested drivers also make A a win-win-win against full compliance, raising driver income, platform profit and service rate by 10 to 12% each. The study offers a framework to evaluate such policies under the new requirement and is, to our knowledge, the first to do so with LLM agents.|[2610.41132](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41132)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Labadain-MedQA: A Tetun Medical QA Dataset for Fine-Tuning and Evaluating Large Language Models**|Gabriel de Jesus et.al.|Advances in large language models (LLMs) have created opportunities for cross-lingual transfer of medical knowledge from high-resource to low-resource languages to support access to healthcare information. To address this gap, we translated MedAlpaca's Medical Flashcards and WikiDoc Patient Information collections into Tetun using Gemini 2.5 Flash, producing 39,077 medical QA pairs. Translation quality was assessed on 50 randomly sampled QA pairs (100 documents) using automatic metrics against human reference translations and evaluations by two native Tetun-speaking healthcare professionals, focusing on medical accuracy and Tetun fluency. Human translations achieved higher mean ratings than LLM-generated translations for both criteria (4.43/5 vs. 4.02/5), with statistically significant differences (Wilcoxon signed-rank test, p < 0.001). To assess downstream utility, we fine-tuned Gemma and Llama variants on the translated MedAlpaca dataset using parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) and evaluated them on 325 Tetun-translated United States Medical Licensing Examination multiple-choice questions. Gemma 4 12B fine-tuned using QLoRA achieved relative improvements in mean accuracy of 10.79% with 4-bit NormalFloat (NF4) inference and 10.42% after merging the LoRA adapters into the BF16 base model, compared with the corresponding base models. These results highlight the viability of automatic dataset translation for Tetun medical QA and the effectiveness of PEFT in enhancing model performance. The datasets, models, and evaluation resources are accessible at https://github.com/labadain/labadain-medqa to support reproducible research on Tetun medical QA and facilitate machine translation, cross lingual, and multilingual research.|[2611.00935](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00935)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Wave Physics as Representation: Tensor-Structured Neural Reconstruction for Ocean Acoustic Fields**|Yifan Sun et.al.|Reconstructing three-dimensional (3D) complex ocean acoustic fields from sparse measurements is challenging due to highly oscillatory wave structures and restricted sensing patterns/routes. Learning-based approaches fall into two categories. First, purely data-driven models can learn complex field patterns, but do not explicitly exploit wave physics that may enhance interpretability and reduce training costs. Second, physics-informed methods impose a specified physical equation as a reconstruction constraint, but often require environment-dependent parameters that are difficult to acquire. As a result, practical 3D acoustic field reconstruction that is both physically grounded and robust to uncertain environmental information remains largely unexplored. This work takes an alternative route: instead of solving a fully specified wave equation, it extracts a structured representation from wave physics and learns its components from data. From the Helmholtz equation and normal mode propagation, we derive a structured spatial-angular tensor representation, termed the S/c representation. Under a low-rank specialization, it takes the form of a rank-(L,L,1) block-term tensor decomposition, which connects structured sampling patterns/routes used in ocean sensing to sufficient conditions for field recovery. Building on this tensor representation, we propose TIDAL, a neural reconstructor that uses the S/c representation as an inductive bias for structured decoding and aligns information aggregation with the sampling geometry. The neural learner removes the need for known model parameters required by conventional physics-based recovery methods. Experiments across three structured sampling geometries show that TIDAL achieves the best reconstruction performance and fastest inference among baselines, with benefits from the physics-derived representation under limited data and model capacity.|[2611.01680](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01680)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Testing concept-level audits of melanoma classifiers with diffusion counterfactuals and matched positive controls**|Md Shohanoor Rahman et.al.|Concept-based explanations of melanoma classifiers are rarely tested against controls with known targets. We built a counterfactual audit from a diffusion model conditioned on seven 7-point checklist concepts and tested it with such controls. Colour-calibrated start noise reduced colour outliers from 78.5-84.8\% to 6.6-13.3\%. Four of seven knobs were target-dominant but also changed other concepts. Quartile-based positive controls were confounded, and the audit failed its pre-specified rule. It recovered the targets of all 16 controls matched on the generator's pseudo-labels and of 12 of 12 controls labelled by a second evaluator, for three concepts; concept activation vectors recovered fewer (11 of 16). In 18 permuted-label classifiers, significant sweep responses and target rankings also arose, whereas standardised effect margins separated the controls from these null classifiers. The pigmentation sweep raised the melanoma logit in 15 of 15 ISIC 2020 classifiers (+3.12 on average) and 6 of 6 HAM10000 classifiers (+4.01), about four times the null response in scale-free units; the regression effect did not transfer. An exploratory decomposition, evaluated against the controls, suggested that knob cross-talk partly hides vascular responses. Matched controls and null classifiers are practical checks of concept audits.|[2611.02119](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02119)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Exploiting Textual Semantics for Robust Cross-View Object Correspondence**|Bing Fan et.al.|Cross-view object correspondence (CVOC) aims to establish associations of the same target between query and search videos captured from different viewpoints. Existing methods often rely on visual cues for CVOC. Despite recent progress, these methods struggle in complicated scenarios with substantial viewpoint changes and appearance variations, due to lacking sufficient target information. Addressing this, we introduce TeSCo, a framework that exploits rich Textual Semantics of an object, in addition to its visual cues, for cross-view object Correspondence. Our key insight is, textual description of a target, by capturing diverse attributes such as category, texture, and color, provides viewpoint- and appearance-invariant semantics, which are complementary to visual cues and can hence enhance correspondence robustness in complicated scenes. Inspired by this, TeSCo first generates a textual expression of the target from the given query view using a vision-language model, and then extracts textual feature to enhance visual features for localization in the search view. To realize this, we introduce a text-conditioned cross-modal fusion (TCF) module that incorporates the textual feature into visual features with text-guided modulation, producing more robust multimodal target representation for object correspondence. Since not all textual cues are equally compatible with the target in current search-view frame, we propose an iterative textual feature refinement (ITFR) module that progressively adjusts textual feature using search-view information before the TCF module, enabling search-view-aware textual semantics to be fused into visual features and leading to better performance. In extensive experiments on Ego-Exo4D and HANDAL-X, TeSCo demonstrates state-of-the-art results and largely outperforms other models, validating its efficacy. Our code is released at https://github.com/VAIL-Fan/TeSCo.|[2611.02356](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02356)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Separating Common and Unique Directions for Model Merging**|Shiting Wang et.al.|Model merging combines multiple fine-tuned models without additional training, and arithmetic methods have emerged as a simple yet effective way to merge models without data. However, existing arithmetic methods treat task vectors in parameter space and apply a single element-wise rule to directions that can differ in mergeability. In a shared directional basis, we find a consistent two-regime structure: a low-dispersion common bulk shared across tasks and a high-dispersion task-dominated tail that carries disproportionate energy. These findings motivate Common--Unique Subspace Decomposition (CUSD), a data-free pre-merging method that separates task vectors into common and unique directional components, merges the common components with a downstream arithmetic operator, and retains unique residuals separately. Experiments across $11$ base models, four model families, and four arithmetic-based merging operators show that CUSD improves 42 of 44 model--operator combinations and achieves up to 22.5% improvement over the corresponding baselines. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/stwang666/CUSD|[2611.04108](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04108)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Predicting protein structures at any folding stage from sequence**|Lisheng Zhang et.al.|Protein structure prediction has largely focused on native conformations, leaving the continuum of unfolded states, folding intermediates, and native structures difficult to access. We present FoldScape, a sequence-conditioned deep learning framework for generating protein conformational ensembles at specified folding fractions $Q$. Trained on approximately one million conformations from 10,000 protein sequences, FoldScape combines coarse-grained folding and all-atom mechanical-unfolding trajectories. It reconstructs three-dimensional $C_\alpha$ chains through hierarchical sliding-window regressions of inter-residue distances, chain-level assembly, and a rectified-flow decoder. By conditioning on folding progress, FoldScape generates conformations from unfolded states through intermediates to native-state structures without protein-specific molecular dynamics at inference. In matched-sample comparisons, FoldScape achieves median conformational-space coverage of 0.989, 0.722, and 0.877 along radius of gyration, fraction of native contacts $Q$, and secondary-structure fraction, respectively, versus 0.05, 0.05, and 0.10 for seed-varied AlphaFold 3 ensembles. Coverage advantages are consistent across the evaluated proteins and robust to binning choices. Reconstruction accuracy remains approximately stable across the evaluated stages of a folding trajectory, and stride-one crop-and-superpose inference extends predictions to chains several times longer than the single-pass limit. Analyses of mechanical-unfolding trajectories show residue-resolved pathways, reproducible extension-conditioned observables across pulling velocities, and geometrically coherent segments in partially unfolded chains. FoldScape extends sequence-based structure prediction beyond a single folded endpoint to controllable conformational-ensemble generation across protein folding.|[2611.04287](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04287)|
|**2026-11-05**|**Aligning Training and Rollout Policies for End-to-End FP4 RL on Mixture-of-Experts LLMs**|Hei Yi Mak et.al.|In this work, we study end-to-end FP4 reinforcement learning (RL) post-training for Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) large language models. Recent work on FP4 RL has introduced effective recovery recipes that restore accuracy under aggressive low-precision quantization for dense models, but we find that the same approach yields no improvement over the naive FP4 baseline in the MoE setting. We conduct an in-depth study to understand why. Our analysis identifies the root cause in the routing mechanism unique to MoE: because the router makes discrete expert-selection decisions, training-inference policy alignment becomes acutely sensitive to whether the same recovery technique is applied symmetrically on the training and inference sides. When an asymmetric recipe is used-including the dense-model approach, which corrects only the rollout-training-inference alignment deteriorates sharply, as measured by the Pearson correlation coefficient between training and inference probabilities, and router selection matching rates. Building on this finding, we propose MoEResQ, a quantization-recovery recipe for MoE RL post-training that enforces symmetry between the training- and inference-side corrections. MoEResQ restores training-inference alignment and substantially closes the accuracy gap to full precision, establishing symmetric recovery as the key principle in FP4 RL for MoE models.|[2611.04376](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04376)|
|**2026-11-05**|**FloMo: 3D Scene Flow as a World Action Model Intermediate for Learning from Human Video**|Jeremy Collins et.al.|Pretrained video generation models distill internet-scale motion priors into robot policies, but full video is a redundant signal for action prediction. We argue that dense 3D motion is the medium through which video pretraining best transfers to robot actions, and that predicting motion rather than video affords better sample-efficiency for action prediction. FloMo is a 5B video-generation model fine-tuned to predict 3D scene-flow and action tokens. Rendering scene flow as RGB and encoding it into the same latent space as video enables a pretrained video generation backbone to efficiently adapt to predict 3D scene flow. Co-trained on action-free human video and action-labeled robot teleoperation data, our model benefits from human-video pretraining and generalizes to unseen objects, motion primitives, and tasks. Replacing motion prediction with a video prediction objective degrades out-of-distribution generalization, while adding a video prediction target alongside motion lowers policy success rather than improving it. When motion, video, and action are predicted jointly, action tokens attend far more to motion than to video, suggesting that predicting video wastes model capacity. Together, these results indicate that motion effectively mediates the transfer from video pretraining to action. More information can be found at flomo-wam.github.io.|[2611.05062](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05062)|
|**2026-11-05**|**RoboTryOn: Synthesizing Dexterous Robot Demonstrations from Human Videos via Virtual Try-On**|Seungyong Lee et.al.|Robot foundation models have shown remarkable performance in robot control, yet adapting them to a new task still relies heavily on teleoperated demonstrations. Egocentric human videos offer a scalable substitute, but overlaying rendered robots on human videos or retargeting human poses into robot actions leaves significant visual or action gaps for high-DoF robot embodiments. To address this limitation, we propose RoboTryOn, which treats human-to-robot translation as virtual try-on: try-on keeps the person and swaps the garment, RoboTryOn keeps the demonstrated motion and swaps the human for the robot. Specifically, RoboTryOn converts a human video into a video of the target robot specified by a reference image, while preserving the original task, objects, and background. To maintain visual consistency, we translate selected keyframes autoregressively and use them to guide video synthesis with motion conditioned on the human hand skeleton. Additionally, we use an inverse dynamics model (IDM) that incorporates depth features to infer action labels from synthesized videos for policy learning. Our experiments across multiple humanoid embodiments show that policies trained with our synthetic data improve the average success rate on novel tasks, including challenging bimanual manipulation, from 2% to 60%, without any real robot demonstration of those tasks. Moreover, training with our synthetic data matches the performance of policies trained solely on teleoperated demonstrations at approximately a third of the collection cost, demonstrating the cost efficiency of our approach.|[2611.05163](http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05163)|
